Estimating Multiple Weighted Networks with Node-Sparse Differences and Shared Low-Rank Structure
Hao Yan, Keith Levin

Abstract
We study the problem of modeling multiple symmetric, weighted networks defined on a common set of nodes, where networks arise from different groups or conditions. We propose a model in which each network is expressed as the sum of a shared low-rank structure and a node-sparse matrix that captures the differences between conditions. This formulation is motivated by practical scenarios, such as in connectomics, where most nodes share a global connectivity structure while only a few exhibit condition-specific deviations. We develop a multi-stage estimation procedure that combines a spectral initialization step, semidefinite programming for support recovery, and a debiased refinement step for low-rank estimation. We establish minimax-optimal guarantees for recovering the shared low-rank component under the row-wise ℓ 2 , ∞ \ell_{2,\infty} norm and elementwise ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} norm, as well as for detecting node-level perturbations under various signal-to-noise regimes. We demonstrate that the availability of multiple networks can significantly enhance estimation accuracy compared to single-network settings. Additionally, we show that commonly-used methods such as group Lasso may provably fail to recover the sparse structure in this setting, a result which might be of independent interest.



1
Introduction

Networks are a fundamental data object in modern statistical applications, arising in domains as varied as neuroscience
(
Sporns, 2011
;
Bassett and Sporns, 2017
;
Guha et al., 2024
)
, economics
(
Easley and Kleinberg, 2010
;
Diebold and Yılmaz, 2014
)
and social science
(
Granovetter, 1973
;
Borgatti et al., 2009
)
, to name just a few.
While modeling a single network has received extensive attention in recent decades— e.g., through Stochastic Block Models
(
Holland et al., 1983
;
Airoldi et al., 2008
;
Karrer and Newman, 2011
, SBM;)
, latent space models such as Hoff models
Hoff et al., 2002
, Random Dot Product Graphs
(
Young and Scheinerman, 2007
;
Sussman et al., 2012
;
Athreya et al., 2018
, RDPG;)
and graphons
(
Lovász, 2012
)
— modeling multiple networks
(
Kivelä et al., 2014
, also known as multilayer networks)
has recently been of growing interest to the network analysis community.
In many practical scenarios, one observes multiple networks on a common set of nodes, with individual networks corresponding to different experimental conditions, populations, or time points.
For example, functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) data yield brain networks in which nodes represent brain regions.
These regions can be consistently aligned across subjects, enabling comparisons of brain connectivity across different populations, disease statuses, ages, or cognitive tasks
(
Vaiana and Muldoon, 2020
)
.
Other examples include temporal gene co-expression networks
(
Bakken et al., 2016
)
and international food and agriculture trade networks
(
De Domenico et al., 2015
)
.
A central goal of inference in these settings is to identify both the structure that is shared across conditions and the systematic differences between them.

We briefly review recent developments in the statistical analysis of multiple networks.

Levin et al., 2017
proposed the omnibus embedding method for jointly embedding multiple networks sharing a common low-rank mean structure.
Subsequently,
Levin et al., 2022
extended this framework to accommodate heterogeneous edge noise across graphs while maintaining a shared low-rank structure in expectation.
Moving beyond shared mean structure,
Lei and Lin, 2023
analyzed a multilayer SBM (MLSBM), where each network shares a common community structure but permits heterogeneous edge probabilities.

Lei et al., 2024
further investigated the fundamental limits of community detection and parameter estimation in MLSBM.
As an extension of the MLSBM,
Nielsen and Witten, 2018
considered a model with multiple RDPGs sharing a common eigenspace.
Building on this idea,
Arroyo et al., 2021
assumed that the adjacency matrices share a common invariant subspace in expectation, and
Xie, 2024
proposed a bias-corrected joint spectral embedding method tailored for this setting.

All of the above works assume a shared subspace structure underlying the edge probabilities.
Relaxing this assumption,
MacDonald et al., 2022b
considered collections of networks following the generalized RDPG model
(
Rubin-Delanchy et al., 2022
)
, under the assumption that the latent position matrix of each network is a concatenation of shared and individual latent position matrices.
Several works have also explored incorporating auxiliary covariates into multilayer network models
(
MacDonald et al., 2022a
;
Zhang et al., 2023
, e.g.,)
.
More broadly, various extensions of other models to the multilayer setting have been proposed; see, for example,
Gollini and Murphy, 2016
;
Chen and Hero, 2017
;
Salter-Townshend and McCormick, 2017
;
D’Angelo et al., 2019
;
Sosa and Betancourt, 2022
;
He et al., 2023
.

This paper addresses the problem of modeling symmetric, weighted networks observed on a common set of nodes.
We introduce a model in which networks from different groups are expressed as the sum of a shared low-rank matrix, capturing global latent structure, and a node-sparse perturbation that accounts for localized structural differences that are unique to each group.
The node-level sparsity of these perturbations reflects the belief that interventions or condition-specific effects often impact only a small subset of entities (e.g., individual brain regions in connectomic data or small groups of individuals in social networks), whose connectivity patterns are selectively altered under various conditions.
Such sparsity assumptions are common in network estimation to promote model parsimony.
For example,
Paul and Chen, 2020
proposed a random effect SBM in which each subject-specific community assignment is a random perturbation of a group-level mean structure, with the assumption that most nodes across different networks retain their group-level memberships.
Similarly,
Zhang et al., 2023
considered multiple networks with covariate information, and assume sparsity in the coefficient tensor associated with the covariates.

Existing work on multiplex and multilayer networks often assumes that differences across layers are smooth or low-rank, arising from shared latent spaces with layer-specific embeddings, or via stochastic block models with group-specific cluster parameters
(
Paul and Chen, 2020
;
MacDonald et al., 2022b
;
Lei and Lin, 2023
)
.
Due to this low-rank or smooth structure, these frameworks are ill-suited to detect sparse or localized deviations.
In contrast, our model is specifically designed to capture such heterogeneity, which cannot be adequately represented by global low-rank structure alone.
Besides adding a new model to the multilayer network literature, another primary goal is to provide simple examples where the availability of multiple observations provides non-trivial benefits in estimation over a single observation.

To formalize our setting, we begin with a simplified two-group model, where each group consists of a single undirected, weighted network with adjacency matrices
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}
respectively.
We model them according to

\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)},

(1)

where
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a symmetric rank-
r
matrix representing the shared latent structure,
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a symmetric matrix capturing the differential connectivity between the two groups, and
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)},\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
are independent symmetric noise matrices.
The assumption that
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
consists solely of the shared component plus noise simplifies identifiability by providing a natural reference point.
This is analogous to the classical ANOVA framework, where one distinguishes a baseline (i.e., control) group from one or more treatment conditions, and one aims to characterize the treatment-induced deviations from the baseline group.

Extending this to a more general setting, we consider two groups of weighted networks.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{0}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}
denote index sets,
with adjacency matrices in group
\mathcal{G}_{0}
modeled as

\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{i}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{i},\quad i\in\mathcal{G}_{0},

(2)

and adjacency matrices of networks in group
\mathcal{G}_{1}
modeled as

\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{j}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}_{j},\quad j\in\mathcal{G}_{1}.

(3)

Here,
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
captures the network structure that is shared across all groups, while
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}
encodes a subject-specific perturbation for subject
j
in group
\mathcal{G}_{1}
.
To enhance interpretability and reflect practical constraints, we assume that
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}
exhibits node-level sparsity, meaning that only a small subset of nodes display altered connectivity patterns under conditions different from the baseline.
We will call this type of sparsity
node-sparsity
, since it captures the node-level differences between treatment groups, and this difference is assumed to be sparse.
The precise definition of node-level sparsity is provided below in Equation (
5
) in Section
2
.
The same notion of sparsity is also considered in
Mohan et al., 2014
for graphical models and
Relión et al., 2019
for network classification.

The formulation given in Equations (
2
) and (
3
) allows for multiple independent network observations per group, enabling the model to capture subject-specific heterogeneity in the group
\mathcal{G}_{1}
.
Within this proposed framework, we are interested in addressing the following key questions:

❒

How does the availability of multiple observations from
\mathcal{G}_{0}
and/or
\mathcal{G}_{1}
improve the recovery of the shared structure
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
or the subject perturbations
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}
?

❒

What estimation procedures are effective for recovering the shared low-rank structure
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
and the subject-specific perturbations
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}
?

We now provide a brief overview of our main contributions.

1.

In Section
3
, we show that a single observation from the control group
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is sufficient to estimate the shared low-rank structure
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
at the minimax-optimal rate under the
\ell_{2,\infty}
norm.

2.

On the other hand, in Section
5
, we demonstrate that having multiple observations from
\mathcal{G}_{0}
and/or
\mathcal{G}_{1}
can substantially improve the estimation of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
, both in terms of eigenspace estimation under the
\ell_{\infty}
and
\ell_{2,\infty}
norms, and in estimating the low-rank signal matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
under the
\ell_{\infty}
norm.

3.

In Section
4
, we establish that recovering the perturbation matrices
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}
from a single observation in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
requires strong conditions on the noise distribution.
However, when multiple observations share the same
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}
, these assumptions can be significantly relaxed, leading to improved recovery guarantees.

These results offer a nuanced understanding of how multiple observations from
\mathcal{G}_{0}
and/or
\mathcal{G}_{1}
contribute to the recovery of both shared and individual components of network structure.

On the algorithmic front, we develop a suite of estimation procedures that adapt to the number of observations in each group.
These include a spectral initialization step (Section
3
), semidefinite programming (SDP) approaches for identifying individual perturbations (Sections
4.2
and
4.3
), and a refinement procedure for accurately recovering the shared low-rank structure (Section
5
).

In addition to algorithm development, we establish minimax lower bounds for both support recovery and low-rank estimation in our model, and demonstrate that our proposed methods attain these bounds under suitable conditions.
Moreover, we demonstrate that certain intuitive baseline methods, such as group Lasso, can provably fail to recover the individual perturbations
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}
, highlighting the need for specialized methods in this setting.

Notation

Before proceeding, we pause to establish notation.
Throughout,
c
and
C
will denote positive constants whose precise values may change from line to line.
For a positive integer
n
, we write
[n]=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.
For a set
S
, we use
|S|
to denote its cardinality.
For a vector
\boldsymbol{v}=\left(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}\right)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we denote the norms
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}^{2}}
and
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\infty}=\max_{i}\left|v_{i}\right|
.
We denote the standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
i\in[n]
and write
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\left\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}=1\right\}
for the unit sphere.
For a matrix
\boldsymbol{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we write
\boldsymbol{M}_{i,\cdot}
for its
i
-th row, taken as a row vector, and
\boldsymbol{M}_{\cdot,i}
for its
i
-th column, taken as a column vector.

\|\boldsymbol{M}\|
denotes its operator norm and
\|\boldsymbol{M}\|_{2,\infty}:=\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{M}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}
denotes its maximum row-wise
\ell_{2}
norm.
We write
\boldsymbol{I}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for the
n
-by-
n
identity matrix and
\boldsymbol{J}
for the matrix of all ones, with dimensions clear from the context.
We use
\operatorname{Sym}(n)
to denote the space of symmetric
n
-by-
n
matrices.
We use both standard Landau notation and asymptotic notation, switching between the two conventions as is convenient.
Given positive functions
f(n)
and
g(n)
, we write
f(n)\gg g(n)
,
f(n)=\omega(g(n))
or
g(n)=o(f(n))
if
f(n)/g(n)\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
We write
f(n)\gtrsim g(n)
,
f(n)=\Omega(g(n))
or
g(n)=O(f(n))
if for a positive constant
C
, we have
f(n)/g(n)\geq C
for all sufficiently large
n
.
We write
f(n)=\Theta(g(n))
if both
f(n)=O(g(n))
and
g(n)=O(f(n))
.

2
Setup

Recall the model defined in Equations (
2
) and (
3
)

\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{i}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{i},\;\text{ for }i\in\mathcal{G}_{0},\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{j}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}_{j},\;\text{ for }j\in\mathcal{G}_{1}.

Throughout, we will impose a set of assumptions on the noise matrices
\boldsymbol{W}
.

Assumption 1
.

The noise matrix
\boldsymbol{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is symmetric, with independent entries
(W_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}
on and above its diagonal.
Furthermore, the entries of
W
satisfy

1.

(Heteroscedasticity.)

For all
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
,

0<\sigma_{\min}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}W_{ij}^{2}=\sigma^{2}_{ij}\leq\sigma^{2}
, with
\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\min}}=\Theta(1).

2.

(Boundness.)

For all
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
,
|W_{ij}|\leq L
almost surely.

3.

(Symmetry about zero.)

For all
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
,
W_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-W_{ij}
.

This assumption is standard in the literature on low-rank matrix estimation
(
Chen et al., 2021a
;
Fan et al., 2022
;
Zhou and Chen, 2025
)
.
The lower bound on the noise variance
\sigma^{2}_{\min}
in the heteroscedasticity condition is not required for establishing the upper bounds in our analysis.
Rather, it is included to facilitate the derivation of minimax lower bounds.
The boundedness condition on the noise entries can be relaxed to a high-probability version.
Specifically, it suffices to assume that
\mathbb{P}(|W_{ij}|\leq L)\geq 1-o(n^{-c})
for some suitably large constant
c>0
.
We adopt the deterministic version of this bound for technical simplicity and clarity of exposition.
The symmetry assumption on the noise distribution is also introduced primarily for convenience, as it simplifies the analysis, but is not essential to the main results.

Additionally, we will need a near-homoscedastic condition in some scenarios.

Assumption 2

(
Near-homoscedasticity).

Let
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=(\sigma^{2}_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. There exists a value
\sigma_{0}>0
such that
\sigma_{0}=\Theta(\sigma)
and

\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\sigma^{2}_{0}\boldsymbol{J}\right\|\leq C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n}.

Remark 1
.

Although Assumption
2
requires the overall behavior of the noise to be close to a homoscedastic noise, it does not require the individual entries of the noise to be homoscedastic.
Consider a symmetric random matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
with i.i.d. sub-Gaussian entries with parameter
\sigma^{2}
, for example,
\Sigma_{ij}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(0,2)
for
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
.
Suppose further that
\mathbb{E}\Sigma_{ij}=\sigma^{2}_{0}
for some
\sigma_{0}\asymp\sigma
.
then
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\sigma^{2}_{0}\boldsymbol{J}
.
By the matrix Bernstein inequality, we have
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|\lesssim\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n}
, which satisfies Assumption
2
.
This implies that Assumption
2
can be satisfied by heteroscedastic noise.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}={\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}
be the spectral decomposition of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
, where
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is a diagonal matrix corresponding to the nonzero eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
The nonzero elements of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}
are given by
\lambda^{\star}_{1},\lambda^{\star}_{2},\dots,\lambda^{\star}_{r}
indexed so that
|\lambda^{\star}_{1}|\geq|\lambda^{\star}_{2}|\geq\cdots\geq|\lambda^{\star}_{r}|
.
Denote the condition number of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
as
\kappa
, which is given by
\kappa=|\lambda^{\star}_{1}|/|\lambda^{\star}_{r}|
.
For simplicity of presentation, we assume that
\kappa=O(1)
and refer the reader to
Zhou and Chen, 2025
for tackling high conditional number.
We define the incoherence of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
to be

\mu=\frac{n}{r}\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|_{2,\infty}^{2}\in\left[1,\frac{n}{r}\right],

We impose the following requirement on
L
and
\sigma
introduced in Assumption
1
:

\sigma\leq L\leq C\sigma\sqrt{\frac{n}{\mu\log n}},

(4)

where
C>0
is a universal constant.
We will be primarily interested in the setting where
L
and
\sigma
are of the same order up to logarithmic factors, so that we allow a wider range of
\mu
; but whenever possible, we will extend our results to a more general setting, allowing for the possibility that
L
is much larger than
\sigma
.
As demonstrated in the proof of Theorem
2
, Equation (
4
) enables us to localize the row-wise estimation error of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
and
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
. Without this assumption, the interplay between high coherence and large magnitude can compromise the accuracy of the entire matrix estimation.

Before we proceed to discuss the estimation procedure, we need to ensure that the model is identifiable.
In particular, the structure of the matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{l}
requires careful consideration.
For notational convenience, we will omit the subscript
l
in
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{l}
and refer to a generic node-sparse signal matrix simply as
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
To formalize the notion of
node-sparsity
, we have the folloing definition.

Definition 1
.

The matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
is node-sparse if there exists a subset of indices
{I_{\star}}\subseteq[n]
with
|{I_{\star}}|=m\leq n
, such that

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}=\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\cdot,i}\|_{2}>0,

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }i\in{I_{\star}};

(5)

\displaystyle B^{\star}_{ij}=0,

\displaystyle\mbox{ for any }(i,j)\in I_{\star}^{c}\times I_{\star}^{c}.

We refer to
{I_{\star}}
as the node-level support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
, representing the subset of nodes involved in nontrivial perturbations in our treatment group(s).

While the classical definition of support for a matrix typically refers to the set of nonzero entries, we adopt the term node-level support to highlight the row/column sparsity pattern described in Equation (
5
).
More generally, for any symmetric matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we introduce the following definition:

Definition 2
.

For any symmetric matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we define
I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}\subseteq[n]
to be the smallest index set such that Equation (
5
) holds with
{\boldsymbol{B}}
in place of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.

An immediate question is whether this node-level support set
{I_{\star}}
is well-defined.
Although we require
{I_{\star}}
to be the smallest such set, minimality alone is not sufficient for uniqueness, as multiple sets of the same size could satisfy the condition in Equation (
5
).
To resolve this ambiguity and guarantee identifiability of the node-level support set, we introduce the following assumption.

Assumption 3
.

There exists a subset
I\subset[n]
with
|I|=m
such that if
i\in I
, then
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}
has at least
m+1
nonzero entries.

Lemma
1
shows that under this assumption, the node-level support
{I_{\star}}
is uniquely identified.

Lemma 1
.

If
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
satisfies Assumption
3
, then the minimal index set
{I_{\star}}
satisfying Equation (
5
) exists, is unique, and
{I_{\star}}=I
, where
I\subseteq[n]
is the set guaranteed by Assumption
3
.

Proof.

We note that the set
I
ensured by Assumption
3
satisfies the conditions of Equation (
5
), so
|{I_{\star}}|\leq|I|=m
trivially.
For any
i\in I
, Assumption
3
ensures the existence of at least
m+1
indices
j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{m+1}\in[n]
such that
B^{\star}_{ij_{k}}\neq 0
for all
k\in[m+1]
.
Since
B^{\star}_{ij_{k}}\neq 0
, and Equation (
5
) requires
B^{\star}_{ij}=0
whenever
i,j\notin{I_{\star}}
, it must be that either
i\in{I_{\star}}
or
j_{k}\in{I_{\star}}
for all
k\in[m+1]
.
If
i\notin{I_{\star}}
, then at least
m+1
distinct indices (namely,
j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{m+1}
) must belong to
{I_{\star}}
, contradicting the bound
|{I_{\star}}|\leq m
.
Therefore,
i\in{I_{\star}}
, implying
I\subseteq{I_{\star}}
.
Since
|{I_{\star}}|\leq|I|
, it follows that
{I_{\star}}=I
, completing the proof.
∎

Assumption
3
is not a necessary condition for the uniqueness of the node-level support set.
However, our goal here is not to identify the weakest possible conditions, but rather to provide a simple assumption that guarantees identifiability and easy to check on any given
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
Moreover, Assumption
3
is not particularly restrictive.
If a row
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}
satisfies that
\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{0}=O(m)
, then both the
i
-th row and column are sparse, reflecting a form of edge-level sparsity.
While such sparsity could be leveraged to improve estimation accuracy in principle, our focus in this paper is on identifying and modeling node-level effects.
Thus, we do not pursue additional sparsity assumptions beyond those specified in Equation (
5
) in the present work.
The notation and definitions introduced above are also ready to be extended to the more general case considered in Equation (
3
).
We denote the node support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{l}
as
I_{l,\star}
and assume that
|I_{l,\star}|\leq m_{l}
for all
l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
.

2.1
Overview of the Proposed Algorithm

With the setup in place, we now provide an overview of our proposed algorithm, which also serves as a roadmap for the remainder of the paper.
The procedure is outlined below in Algorithm
1
and involves three key subroutines, which we refer to as algorithms
\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
Specifically,
\mathcal{A}_{0}
provides an initial estimate of the shared structure
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
produces an estimated
\hat{I}_{l}
of node-level support set of the perturbation
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{l}
for each
l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
refines the initial estimate of the shared structure
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
A more detailed discussion of each step follows.

In Step
3
of Algorithm
1
, we obtain an initial estimate
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
of the shared low-rank structure
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
using Algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, applied to the control group observations
\{\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{0}}
.
We specify algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{0}
to be the standard spectral estimator for low-rank matrices.
The resulting estimator
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
is itself low-rank and can be factorized as
\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}
.
In Step
4
, we identify a subset of nodes
\mathcal{U}\subseteq[n]
whose corresponding rows in the singular vector matrix
\boldsymbol{U}
have low coherence. The details are provided in Section
3
.

Next, we restrict the observations
\{\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}
to the selected node set
\mathcal{U}
and subtract the estimated shared structure
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
, yielding residual matrices
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{l}
.
In Step
6
, we apply algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{1}
to the residual matrices
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{l}
to estimate the node-level support
\hat{I}_{l}
for each subject in the treatment group.
We analyze multiple possible implementations of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
in Section
4
.
Our primary proposal is an estimator based on semi-definite programming (SDP), offering both computational efficiency and minimax optimality guarantees.
In Section
4.3
, we further demonstrate that the availability of multiple observations is crucial for relaxing the assumptions required to recover the node-level support
I_{l,\star}
.

As an alternative to SDP, we also consider a method based on the group lasso penalty, described in Section
4.5
.
This approach, originally proposed in
Mohan et al., 2014
for graphical models exhibiting sparsity patterns similar to ours, can be computationally more efficient than SDP.
However, its theoretical justification in prior work was limited.
Specifically, it lacked high-probability guarantees for recovering the support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{l}
.
We show that because the group lasso method is a greedy algorithm, it can fail to recover the correct support in certain regimes.

Finally, in Step
8
, we refine the estimate of the shared structure
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
For each
l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
, we remove the rows and columns corresponding to the estimated node support
\hat{I}_{l}
from
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{l}
, obtaining a refined matrix
\boldsymbol{M}_{l}
.
We then apply algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{2}
to the collection of
\{\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{0}}
and
\{\boldsymbol{M}_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}
to produce the final estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}
.
Algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can be viewed as a debiased version of the spectral method, where we make use of multiple observations to improve the estimation of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
Details are provided in Section
5
.

Algorithm 1

General procedure to estimate
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{l}

1:

\left\{\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathcal{G}_{0}},\left\{\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{l}\right\}_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}
; Algorithms
\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

2:

Estimators
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}
and
\{{\boldsymbol{B}}_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}
.

3:

\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)\top}=\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{i}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{G}_{0}}\right)
.

4:

Let

\mathcal{U}:=\left\{i\in[n]:\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\leq Cn^{-1/4}\right\}.

(6)

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathcal{U}=[\widetilde{n}]
for
\widetilde{n}\leq n
.

5:

Set

\widetilde{Y}_{l,ij}\leftarrow Y^{(1)}_{l,ij}-M^{(0)}_{ij},\mbox{ for }\;i,j\in\mathcal{U},

(7)

where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{n}\times\widetilde{n}}
.

6:

Set
\hat{I}_{l}
to be the estimated node support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{l}
given by
\{\hat{I}_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\left\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{l}\right\}_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}\right)

7:

Set the rows and columns in the node support
\hat{I}_{l}
from
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{l}
to
0
and obtain
\boldsymbol{M}_{l}
as follows:

M_{l,ij}:=\begin{cases}Y^{(1)}_{l,ij}&\mbox{ if }(i,j)\in\hat{I}^{c}_{l}\times\hat{I}^{c}_{l},\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise }.\end{cases}

8:

Set
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathcal{G}_{0}},\left\{\boldsymbol{M}_{l}\right\}_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}\right)
.

We finish this section with an informal statement of our main results, which provides a high-level overview of the theoretical guarantees of Algorithm
1
.

Theorem 1
.

Under suitable conditions on the noise matrices and the parameters of the model in Equations (
2
) and
3
, Algorithm
1
provides the following guarantees:

1.

The spectral initialization procedure
\mathcal{A}_{0}
in Step
3
yields an estimate
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
that estimates
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
under the row-wise
\ell_{2,\infty}
norm at the minimax optimal rate.

2.

The support recovery procedure
\mathcal{A}_{1}
in Step
6
yields an estimate
\hat{I}_{l}
of the node-level support
I_{l,\star}
that recovers the true support with high probability under minimal signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) conditions.

3.

The debiased refinement
\mathcal{A}_{2}
in Step
8
yields near-minimax optimal estimates of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
under the row-wise
\ell_{2,\infty}
norm, and of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
under the elementwise
\ell_{\infty}
norm.

Theoretical results for the spectral initialization are presented in Theorems
2
and
3
in Section
3
.
Upper bounds for support recovery are established in Theorems
7
,
10
, and
11
, while the related lower bounds are provided in Theorems
5
and
8
in Section
4
.
For the debiased refinement procedure, upper bounds are given in Theorems
16
,
18
, and
19
, and a minimax lower bound is presented in Theorem
20
, all in Section
5
.

3
Spectral Initialization

In this section, we focus on analyzing Steps
3
and
4
of Algorithm
1
.
We focus on the case where
|\mathcal{G}_{0}|=1
, and denote the only matrix in
\mathcal{G}_{0}
by
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
.
The case where
|\mathcal{G}_{0}|>1
is discussed in Section
5
.
Let the spectral decomposition of
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
be

\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)\top}+\boldsymbol{U}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{U}_{\perp}^{\top},

where
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda^{(0)}_{1},\lambda^{(0)}_{2},\dots,\lambda^{(0)}_{r})
is the diagonal matrix of the leading
r
eigenvalues, the columns of
\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
are the corresponding
r
orthonormal eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
,
and the columns of
\boldsymbol{U}_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(n-r)}
are the remaining
n-r
orthonormal eigenvectors.
We take
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)\top}
as the initial spectral estimator of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
and denote the estimation error by the residual matrix

\boldsymbol{\Delta}:=\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}.

(8)

Adapting the proof of Corollary 4.3 in
Chen et al., 2021b
, we obtain the following refined row-wise and entry-wise bounds.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
is given by Equation (
1
) with
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
satisfying Assumption
1
and Equation (
4
).
Assume that
|\lambda^{\star}_{r}|\geq C\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n\log n}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
and
\kappa=O(1)
.
Then with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, the following bounds hold for the residual matrix
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
defined in Equation (
8
):

1.

(Row-wise bound) Uniformly over all
\ell\in[n]
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{{\ell,\cdot}}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\sigma\kappa^{2}\sqrt{n}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}\lesssim\left(\sigma\sqrt{n}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\sigma\sqrt{r\log n},

2.

(Entry-wise bound) Uniformly over all
k,\ell\in[n]
:

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\ell k}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sigma\kappa^{2}\sqrt{n}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{{k,\cdot}}\right\|_{2}+\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}\max\left\{\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{k,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right\}+\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\sigma\sqrt{n})\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{{k,\cdot}}\right\|_{2}+\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}\max\left\{\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{k,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right\}

The proof Theorem
2
is provided in Appendix
C
, alongside the proof of an analogous result for subspace estimation, which appears as Theorem
21
.

Remark 2
.

The bound in Theorem
2
above reveals that the row-wise
\ell_{2}
-norm of
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
scales with the corresponding row norm of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
.
The condition in Equation (
4
) is imposed to prevent estimation errors associated with rows having large
\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}
from propagating to those with small row norms.
The proof of Theorem
2
traces this cross-contamination to the term
\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|_{2}
.
As an illustration, consider
|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{1}|
.
We have

|W^{(0)}_{\ell,i}u^{\star}_{1,i}|\leq L\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{1}\|_{\infty}

for all
i\in[n]
and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left(W^{(0)}_{li}u^{\star}_{1i}\right)^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}.

Applying Bernstein’s inequality highlights that the condition in Equation (
4
) is essential for obtaining an upper bound for
|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{1}|
that ensures row-wise error localization.

To recover the node support
{I_{\star}}
of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
, it is necessary for all
i\in{I_{\star}}
, each
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}
has sufficiently large norm.
Specifically, Theorem
5
in Section
4.1
establishes that a signal strength of at least
\Omega(\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n)
is required for recovering
i\in{I_{\star}}
with high probability.
However, support recovery becomes more challenging when the rows of the low-rank component
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
exihibit high coherence.
According to Theorem
2
, the row-wise estimation error of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
scales with
\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}
, implying that high-coherence rows may suffer from larger estimation errors under spectral initialization.
If the node support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
overlaps with these high-error rows, the estimation error in
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
may exceed the minimal signal strength required for detecting
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
, making recovery impossible.
More generally, high coherence rows of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
can lead to identifiability issues.
Consider the rank-
4
matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}=\lambda^{\star}\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}^{\top}\right)
, and let
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}
, where
v_{1}=v_{2}=0
and
v_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{B}^{2})
for
3\leq i\leq n
.
Under our setup, we observe

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)},\;\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}.

If
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
still has rank
4
, then we can set
\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
and consider

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)},\;\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}.

If the first two rows of
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
have higher variance than the remaining rows, then unless
\sigma_{B}=\Theta(\sigma)
, one cannot reliably distinguish the above two settings.
In this paper, to avoid this possible ambiguity between high coherence rows of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
and the node support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
, we impose the following assumption:

Assumption 4
.

Define the index set

\mathcal{U}^{\star}:=\left\{\ell\in[n]:\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}=O(n^{-1/4})\right\}\subset[n].

(9)

The node support set
{I_{\star}}
obeys
{I_{\star}}\subset\mathcal{U}^{\star}
.

Under Assumption
4
, we only need to discard the high-coherence rows of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
to ensure that the estimation error does not exceed the minimal signal strength required for support recovery.
Theorem
21
in Appendix
C
shows that the spectral estimator
\boldsymbol{U}
is sufficient for us to accurately estimate the magnitude of each row norm
\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}
up to the tolerable statistical error rate.
Consequently, Step
4
in Algorithm
1
reliably identifies and removes the high-error rows of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
, which is critical for accurate support recovery.

Remark 3
.

Under Assumption
4
, the high-coherence rows of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
in
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
can be identified and removed with high probability.
As a result, they do not impact the analysis of the support recovery problem.
For simplicity of exposition, in Sections
4
and
5
, we assume without loss of generality that
\left(\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right)^{c}=\emptyset
, so that
\mathcal{U}^{\star}=[n]
holds with high probability.

It is worth noting that explicit estimation of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
is not strictly required for recovering
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
For instance, one could instead subtract any matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{i}
for
i\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
from the observed matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{j}
to cancel out the shared component
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
, resulting in the residual matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
.
This differencing approach removes the low-rank component without explicitly estimating it.
The benefit of this approach is that it can recover
{I_{\star}}
even when Assumption
4
does not hold, but this hinges on the assumption that the noise matrices
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
also satisfy Assumption
2
.
Further discussion of Assumption
2
is provided in the beginning of Section
4.1
.
In contrast, estimating and subtracting the low-rank component
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
avoids the need to impose Assumption
2
on the control noise
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
.
Moreover, the naïve differencing approach increases the effective noise level, which can hinder support recovery.
In practice, one can apply both methods on the data as the analysis and methods developed in Section
4
also apply to both the residual-based and direct “differencing” approaches.
For this reason, we focus on the residual-based strategy in what follows.

A natural follow-up question to Theorem
2
concerns whether having more samples in the control group
\mathcal{G}_{0}
significantly improves the row-wise estimation error rate of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
Interestingly, even without any additional correction, the spectral estimator
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
already achieves the minimax-optimal rate for estimating
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
under the
\ell_{2,\infty}
norm.

Theorem 3
.

Let

\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mu,\sigma_{\min}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}=\lambda^{\star}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}:\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}\right\|_{\infty}=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{n}},|\lambda^{\star}|\geq\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{n}\right\}.

Suppose that we observe
N\geq 1
independent copies of
\boldsymbol{M}_{l}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}_{l}
, where
W_{l,ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{l,ij})
are independent random variables for
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
and
l\in[N]
.
Then

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mu,\sigma_{\min})}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\right\|_{2,\infty}\geq\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{27\sqrt{\log 4}}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{N}},

where the infimum is over all estimators of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.

It is straightforward to extend Theorem
3
to the case where
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
is not necessarily rank-one.
However, we focus on the rank-one setting for simplicity, as it is sufficient to convey the key message.
When
N=1
, Theorem
2
implies that the spectral estimator achieves this lower bound up to logarithmic factors, and is thus nearly minimax-optimal under the
(2,\infty)
-norm.
When
N>1
, the minimax rate improves with
1/\sqrt{N}
, and is easily attained by averaging the
N
independent observations
\boldsymbol{M}_{1},\boldsymbol{M}_{2},\dots,\boldsymbol{M}_{N}
and applying the spectral estimator to the result.
In particular, when
N=\Theta(1)
, a single copy from
\mathcal{G}_{0}
suffices to achieve the minimax-optimal rate.
This observation highlights a key theme of this paper: while the estimation error rate under the
\ell_{2,\infty}
norm is unaffected when increasing
N
from
1
to a larger constant, other settings, such as support recovery for
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
and elementwise estimation of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
, exhibit a more pronounced distinction between
N=1
and
N=2
.
Details are given in Section
4.3
and Section
5
, respectively.

4
Recovery of the Differences
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}

In this section, we focus on recovering the difference matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
from the residual matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
defined in Equation (
7
).
We begin by analyzing the case where
|\mathcal{G}_{1}|=1
, and for notational simplicity, we drop all the subscripts and superscripts associated with the group
\mathcal{G}_{1}
.
Specifically, in the remainder of this section, we write
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
,
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
, and
\boldsymbol{W}
to denote
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{1}
,
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{1}
, and
\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}_{1}
, respectively.
The more general case where
|\mathcal{G}_{1}|>1
will be addressed in Section
4.3
.

A natural approach to estimating
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
and recovering its node support
{I_{\star}}
is to solve the following constrained least squares problem:

\displaystyle\arg\min_{{\boldsymbol{B}},I}

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}(\widetilde{Y}_{ij}-B_{ij})^{2},

(10)

\displaystyle\text{s.t. }

\displaystyle B_{ij}=0\text{ for any }i,j\in I^{c},|I|\leq m

where
m
is an upper bound on the cardinality of
{I_{\star}}
.
For theoretical analysis, we assume that the true support size
m=|{I_{\star}}|
is known.
Practical strategies for choosing
m
are discussed in Section
4.4
.
The formulation in Equation (
10
) is equivalent to selecting a set of indices
I\subseteq[n]
that minimizes the squared residuals outside the support, i.e.,

\hat{I}=\arg\min_{I\subseteq[n]}\sum_{i,j\in I^{c},i\leq j}\widetilde{Y}_{ij}^{2},\quad|I|=m.

The corresponding estimator
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{B}}}
then takes the form:
B_{ij}=\widetilde{Y}_{ij}
if
i\in\hat{I}
or
j\in\hat{I}
, and
B_{ij}=0
otherwise.
This problem is equivalent to the
(n-m)
-Lightest Subgraph problem, which is known to be NP-hard in general
(
Watrigant et al., 2012
)
.
This NP-hardness in the symmetric setting stands in sharp contrast to the asymmetric case.
In an asymmetric version the problem, where
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
is assumed to be row-sparse (or column-sparse), support recovery becomes significantly easier.
In that setting, one can simply rank the row norms of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
and greedily select the top-
m
rows, making the problem computationally trivial.
This contrast highlights the added difficulty posed by the symmetric node-sparse structure considered here.

Remark 4
.

The problem of recovering
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
is closely related to the submatrix detection problem, which involves recovering a row- and column-sparse signal matrix from a noisy observation
(
Ma and Wu, 2015
;
Chen and Xu, 2016
;
Cai et al., 2017
;
Cai and Wu, 2020
;
Sohn and Wein, 2025
)
.
Much of the existing work in this area focuses on the asymmetric setting with i.i.d. noise and investigates both statistical and computational limits.
Notably, these studies identify regimes in which the problem becomes computationally intractable.
Extending these results to the symmetric setting, which is more relevant to our model, is an interesting direction for future research.
Related work has also examined the estimation of matrices with either row-sparse or column-sparse structure
(
Klopp and Tsybakov, 2015
;
Obozinski et al., 2011
;
Yang et al., 2016
)
.
While these problems are similar in spirit, they typically require different techniques and do not directly address the symmetric, node-sparse structure considered here.

As a first step towards understanding the behavior of our proposed method, we consider a simplified benchmark model

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W},

(11)

in which we ignore the estimation error introduced by
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
.
To formalize the minimal signal strength required for recovery, we define

b=\min_{i\in{I_{\star}}}\left\|\left(B_{ij}^{\star}\right)_{j\in\left([n]\backslash{I_{\star}}\right)\cup\{i\}}\right\|_{2}.

(12)

We will discuss the definition of
b
in more detail below in Section
4.1
.
For now, we simply note that it is the key quantity in determining sufficient conditions for recovering the node support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
, as the following theorem demonstrates.

Theorem 4
.

Consider an observed matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
from Equation (
11
), where
\boldsymbol{W}
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
, and
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
satisfies Assumption
3
. Assume that
n\geq 3m
and that

\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\right\|_{\infty}=o\left(\sigma\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log n}}\right).

(13)

With
b
as defined in Equation (
12
),
suppose that

b\geq c_{0}(\sigma L)^{1/2}\left(n\log n\right)^{1/4}

(14)

for a sufficiently large universal constant
c_{0}
.
Then it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
that the maximum-likelihood estimator
\hat{I}
recovers
{I_{\star}}
exactly.

Furthermore, if for some
k\in[m]
, we have

b\geq c_{0}(\sigma L)^{1/2}\left(n\log\frac{n}{k}\right)^{1/4},

(15)

then with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, we have

|\hat{I}\cap{I_{\star}}|\geq m-k.

The proof is provided in Appendix
A
. The conditoin in Equation (
13
) is a technical condition to simplify the analysis.
In practice, this is a very mild condition, as violating it would imply that some entries of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
are very large, making the support recovery problem trivial.

4.1
Minimax Risk for Support Recovery

Before discussing computationally efficient methods to estimate
{I_{\star}}
, we first examine whether the lower bound in Equation (
14
) is optimal in a minimax sense.
To illustrate the necessity of certain assumptions on the noise matrix, we begin with a simple example showing that Assumption
2
is essential when only a single observation of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
is available.
Consider the following hypothesis testing problem:

\mathcal{H}_{0}:\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{w}_{1}\quad\text{vs}\quad\mathcal{H}_{1}:\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{w}_{2},

where
\boldsymbol{b}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{n})
,
\boldsymbol{w}_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{n})
and
\boldsymbol{w}_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,2\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{n})
are independent.
Clearly,
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}
induce the same probability distribution on
\boldsymbol{y}
, making them indistinguishable.
This example shows that without further structural assumptions on
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
, or a large signal strength (e.g.,
\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}\gg\sigma\sqrt{n}
for
\ell\in{I_{\star}}
), recovering
{I_{\star}}
from
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
is impossible when Assumption
2
does not hold.
In contrast, we show in Section
4.3
that Assumption
2
is not necessary if two independent observations of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
are available.

We now turn to the case where Assumption
2
holds.
To formalize the support recovery problem, define the binary selector vector
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\star}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
by

\eta^{\star}_{i}=\mathbbm{1}\{i\in{I_{\star}}\}\quad\text{for }i\in[n],

(16)

and let

\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}^{\star}=(\eta^{\star}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n:j\neq i}.

Consider the collection of hypothesis testing problems

H_{i,0}:\eta_{i}^{\star}=0\quad\text{vs}\quad H_{i,1}:\eta_{i}^{\star}=1,\quad\text{ for }i=1,2,\dots n,

and note that this task is strictly harder than solving the corresponding set of conditional testing problems,

H_{i,0}:\eta_{i}^{\star}=0\quad\text{vs}\quad H_{i,1}:\eta_{i}^{\star}=1,\quad\text{given }\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}^{\star},\quad\text{ for }i=1,2,\dots,n,

where one assumes knowledge of the inclusion or exclusion status of all nodes except node
i
.
Each of these conditional problems is equivalent to testing

H_{i,0}:(B^{\star}_{ij})_{j\in([n]\backslash{I_{\star}})\cup\{i\}}=0\quad\text{vs}\quad H_{i,1}:(B^{\star}_{ij})_{j\in([n]\backslash{I_{\star}})\cup\{i\}}\neq 0.

This motivates the introduction of the following parameter set, which captures node-sparse symmetric matrices with minimum signal strength:

\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m):=\left\{{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\operatorname{Sym}(n),\;|I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}|\leq m:\begin{array}[]{cc}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_{i}\|_{2}\geq b,\|{\boldsymbol{B}}_{i,\cdot}\|_{0}\geq m+1&\forall i\in I\\
B_{ij}=0,&\forall i,j\in I^{c}\end{array}\right\},

(17)

where for any symmetric matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we denote
\tilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_{i}=(B_{ij})_{j\in[n]\backslash I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}\cup\{i\}}
and
I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}
is the node support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
defined in Definition
2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
be the binary support indicator associated with
{\boldsymbol{B}}
, defined analogously to
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\star}
in Equation (
16
), with
I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in place of
{I_{\star}}
.
For any estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}
derived from the observation
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}
, we measure the estimation error using the Hamming distance,

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\hat{\eta}_{j}-\eta_{j}|.

To facilitate the comparison between bounds on
\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\neq\boldsymbol{\eta}\right)
and bounds on the expected Hamming error
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}
, we note the following implication:
if
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\neq\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\star})\leq\alpha
for some
\alpha\in[0,1]
, then by the trivial upper bound
\|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\leq n
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\leq 0\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[\|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}=0\right]+n~\mathbb{P}\left[\|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\neq 0\right]\leq n\alpha.

As a result, upper bounds on the misclassification probability
\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\neq\boldsymbol{\eta}\right)
can be translated into corresponding bounds on
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}
.
Theorem
5
provides a minimax lower bound for the expected Hamming error over the parameter set
\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)
.

Theorem 5
.

Consider observed data
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}
, where
\boldsymbol{W}
is a symmetric noise matrix with entries
(W_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}
drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
.
Assume
|{I_{\star}}|=m<n/3
.
Then, there exists a universal constant
C>0
, such that for sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\sup_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{m^{\prime}\in(0,m]}4m^{\prime}\left[\frac{1}{16}f_{n}\left(\frac{Cb^{4}}{\sigma^{4}},m^{\prime}\right)-e^{\frac{-(m-m^{\prime})^{2}}{2m}}\right],

(18)

where for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, the function
f_{n}(a,m^{\prime})
is defined as

f_{n}(a,m^{\prime})=3-\frac{m^{\prime}}{n}\left(\exp\left\{a-C^{\prime}n\log\left(\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right)\right\}+\exp\left(\frac{a}{\log n}-C^{\prime}n\right)+\exp\left\{\frac{C^{\prime}a}{n}\right\}\right)

for some universal constant
C^{\prime}>0
.

A proof of Theorem
5
is given in Appendix
B
, alongside a proof of the following corollary.

Corollary 1
.

Under the same setting as Theorem
5
, for any
m<n/3
and
m^{\prime}\in[1,m]
, there exists a universal constant
C>0
, independent of all other parameters,
such that if

b<C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(n/3m^{\prime}\right),

then it follows that

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\sup_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq 4m^{\prime}\left[\frac{1}{8}-\exp\left\{-\frac{\left(m-m^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{2m}\right\}\right].

(19)

Corollary
1
is readily extended to a multiple sample setting, where we observe
N
independent copies of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
.

Theorem 6
.

Suppose we observe
N\geq 1
independent samples of the form

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\ell}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}_{\ell},~~~\ell=1,2,\dots,N

where
\{\boldsymbol{W}_{\ell}:\ell\in[N]\}
are independent symmetric noise matrices with entries
(W_{\ell,ij})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}
drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
.
Assume that
m<n/3
.
Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
, such that for sufficiently large
n
and any
m^{\prime}\in[1,m]
, if

b<\frac{C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(n/3m^{\prime}\right)}{\sqrt{N}},

it holds that

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\sup_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq 4m^{\prime}\left[\frac{1}{8}-\exp\left\{-\frac{\left(m-m^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{2m}\right\}\right].

(20)

The proof of Theorem
6
is similar to that of Theorem
5
.
Details are provided in Appendix
B.3
.

Remark 5
.

By Equations (
19
) and (
20
), when
m\geq 6
, we may take
m^{\prime}=1
and conclude that, for some constant
c_{0}
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\sup_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}>0,

provided that
b<CN^{-1/2}\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(n/3\right)
.
To express this condition in terms of the signal-to-noise ratio, define

\operatorname{SNR}=\frac{\min_{i\in{I_{\star}}}\sqrt{\sum_{j\in([n]\backslash{I_{\star}})\cup\{i\}}{B^{\star}_{ij}}^{2}}}{\sigma/\sqrt{N}}.

Our results imply that no estimator can achieve exact recovery of the node support
{I_{\star}}
when

\operatorname{SNR}<Cn^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n

for some universal constant
C>0
.
We refer to the rate
\Omega(n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n)
as the exact recovery threshold.

Moreover, taking
m^{\prime}=m/2
in Equations (
19
) and (
20
) yields

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\sup_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq\frac{1}{8}m,

provided that
b<CN^{-1/2}\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(2n/3m\right)
and
m\geq 25
.
Therefore, to recover a non-trivial fraction of the entries of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
correctly, Equation (
18
) implies that we require

\operatorname{SNR}=\Omega\left(n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(n/m\right)\right).

We refer to this lower bound on the SNR as the partial recovery threshold.

Both the exact recovery threshold and the partial recovery threshold can be achieved by the least squares estimator (LSE) when the noise satisfies
L\asymp\sigma
,
as established in Equation (
15
) in Theorem
4
.
Consequently, the LSE is minimax rate-optimal in recovering
{I_{\star}}
under this regime.

4.2
Semidefinite Programming: Single Observation

Motivated by the optimality of the LSE, we now seek a computationally tractable approximation by formulating a Semidefinite Programming (SDP) relaxation.
In this section, we continue to focus on the case where
|\mathcal{G}_{1}|=1
.
Recall from Equation (
7
) that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
denotes the residual matrix after subtracting off the initial estimate produced by procedure
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}
denote the estimation error matrix
\boldsymbol{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{U}|\times|\mathcal{U}|}
restricted to rows and columns indexed by
\mathcal{U}
, so that

\widetilde{\Delta}_{ij}:=\Delta_{ij}\quad\text{for }i,j\in\mathcal{U}.

(21)

Similarly, let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}
denote the restriction of the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}
to the same index set.
Following the same reason stated in Remark
3
, without loss of generality, we assume
\mathcal{U}^{\star}=[n]
so that with high probability, we have
\mathcal{U}=[n]
.
Under this setup, the residual matrix can be decomposed as

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}.

We define the incoherence parameter for the remaining rows of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
as

\tilde{\mu}:=\max_{i\in\mathcal{U}}\frac{n}{r}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|^{2}_{2}\lesssim n^{1/2},

where the last inequality follows from Step
4
in Algorithm
1
.
Let
\boldsymbol{C}:=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
be the entrywise square and let
K=n-m
.
We consider the following convex relaxation of the LSE as defined in Equation (
10
):

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{\mathrm{SDP}}=\underset{\boldsymbol{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}{\arg\min}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{C},\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle

(22)

\displaystyle\text{ s.t. }

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}\succeq 0

\displaystyle Z_{ii}\leq 1,\quad\forall i\in[n]

\displaystyle Z_{ij}\geq 0,\quad\forall i,j\in[n]

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{I},\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle=K

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle=K^{2}.

This SDP formulation is closely related to the one proposed in
Hajek et al., 2016
for the community detection problem under the SBM with two clusters.
Similar SDP-based relaxations have been extensively studied in the context of community detection.
See the survey
Li et al., 2021
for a comprehensive overview of convex optimization methods in this domain.

Denote the indicator vector for nodes not in the support set and the corresponding support matrix as

\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\star}:=\boldsymbol{1}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\star}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}:=\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\star}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\star}^{\top}.

(23)

We aim to identify sufficient conditions under which the SDP formulation in Equation (
22
) recovers the ground truth matrix
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
.
Given the recovered support encoded by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{\mathrm{SDP}}
, we construct an estimator for
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
by masking the residual matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
:

\widehat{{\boldsymbol{B}}}_{\mathrm{SDP}}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{\mathrm{SDP}}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}.

To understand the theoretical performance of the SDP estimator in Equation (
22
),
we begin by analyzing a simplified structure for
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
Specifically, we consider the case where

\left|B_{ij}^{\star}\right|=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }i,j\in{{I_{\star}}}^{c}\\
0,&\text{ if }i,j\in{I_{\star}}\\
\beta,&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(24)

A more general structure we will later consider is:

\left|B_{ij}^{\star}\right|\begin{cases}=0,&\text{ if }i,j\in{I_{\star}}^{c}\\
\geq 0,&\text{ if }i,j\in{I_{\star}}\\
\geq\beta,&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(25)

which permits heterogeneous signal strengths within the support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.

We quantify the estimation error between
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
using the
\ell_{1}
norm.
This choice facilitates the analysis of support recovery guarantees for
{I_{\star}}
.
The following key lemma provides a bound on this error.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
satisfies Equation (
24
).
Let

\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}=({\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)})\circ({\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}),

(26)

where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}
is a noise matrix satisfying Assumption
1
.
If
n>3m
, then for
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
defined in Equations (
72
) and (
23
) respectively, we have

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1}\leq\frac{5}{2\beta^{2}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}},\boldsymbol{C}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\sigma_{0}^{2}\boldsymbol{J}\right\rangle,

(27)

where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
and
\sigma_{0}
are as defined in Assumption
2
.
Furthermore, conditioned on the event
\{\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq C\sigma\sqrt{nr}\}
and under Assumption
2
, we have

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1}\lesssim\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}},\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}+2\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}\right\rangle\right|+\frac{\sigma^{2}n^{3/2}+\sigma^{2}nr}{\beta^{2}}+\frac{\sigma r^{1/2}n^{3/2}}{\beta}.

The proof of Lemma
2
is provided in Appendix
D.1
.
We note that a similar result was established by
Fei and Chen, 2018
in the context of community detection under the SBM.
We will adapt several proof techniques from their work to obtain a sharp bound on the estimation error
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1}
in our setting.
To this end, it suffices to derive a upper bound on the right-hand side of Equation (
27
).

Our first step toward analyzing the estimation error is to establish a preliminary, albeit coarse, upper bound for the matrix inner product using Grothendieck’s inequality,
which states that for any symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{Z}\succeq 0,\\
\max_{i\in[n]}Z_{ii}\leq 1\end{subarray}}|\langle\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{A}\rangle|\leq c_{0}\max_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}\left|\sum_{i,j}A_{ij}x_{i}y_{j}\right|,

(28)

where
0<c_{0}<1.783
is an absolute constant.
For a detailed reference, see Theorem 3.5.1 in
Vershynin, 2018
.

Lemma 3
.

Under the same conditions as Lemma
2
, suppose that
\beta\leq L
.
Conditioned on the event

\{\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq C\sigma\sqrt{rn}\}\cap\{\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|_{\infty}\leq C\sigma\}

(29)

for some constant
C>0
, we have with probability at least
1-O(n^{-40})

\left|\langle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star},\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}+2\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}\rangle\right|\leq C^{\prime}\sigma Ln^{3/2}

for a constant
C^{\prime}
sufficiently large.

A proof is provided in Appendix
D.2
.
It is reasonable to focus on the regime
\beta\leq L
.
Otherwise, the signal in the entries of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
would dominate the noise, rendering the support recovery problem trivial.
Combining Lemma
3
with Equation (
27
) shows that when
\beta=\omega(\sigma^{1/2}L^{1/2}n^{-1/4})
, the estimation error
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1}
satisfies

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right\|_{1}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(30)

While this provides a consistency guarantee, the bound in Equation (
30
) is rather weak: it becomes vacuous as soon as
m=o(n)
, since in that case any feasible solution to the SDP in Equation (
22
) would trivially satisfy the bound.
Nonetheless, establishing Equation (
30
) serves as a crucial first step that enables sharper control through more refined analysis.

To improve upon Equation (
30
), we leverage the low-rank structure of
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
.
Recall from Equation (
23
) that
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
is a rank-one matrix with spectral decomposition given by

\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}=K\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\star}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\star}\right)^{\top}.

Motivated by the geometric structure of the manifold of rank-one symmetric matrices, we decompose the key inner product in Lemma
2
into components along the tangent space at
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
and its orthogonal complement.
For further background on tangent space geometry in low-rank matrix manifolds, see Chapter 7.5 of
Boumal, 2023
.
Specifically, following the approach of
Fei and Chen, 2018
, we define

S_{1}:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}},\mathcal{P}_{T}\left(\boldsymbol{C}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\sigma_{0}^{2}\boldsymbol{J}\right)\right\rangle

(31)

and

S_{2}:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}},\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{C}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\sigma_{0}^{2}\boldsymbol{J}\right)\right\rangle

(32)

and decompose the key inner product term as

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}},\boldsymbol{C}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\sigma_{0}^{2}\boldsymbol{J}\right\rangle=S_{1}+S_{2},

where for any matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
the projections
\mathcal{P}_{T}(\boldsymbol{A})
and
\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{A})
are defined as

\mathcal{P}_{T}(\boldsymbol{A}):=K^{-1}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{A}+K^{-1}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}-K^{-2}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{A}):=\boldsymbol{A}-\mathcal{P}_{T}(\boldsymbol{A}).

(33)

This decomposition is standard in the low-rank matrix estimation literature.
See, for example,
Recht, 2011
and
Negahban and Wainwright, 2011
.
The operator
\mathcal{P}_{T}
captures deviations aligned with the column and row space of
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
, while
\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}
captures orthogonal directions.
Bounding
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
precisely is key to obtaining a sharp control of the estimation error.
This is carried out in
Lemmas
14
and
15
in Appendices
D.4
and
D.3
, respectively.

We are now ready to state a result characterizing the estimation error
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1}
under the SDP relaxation.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose the noise matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
, as well as Equation (
4
).
Assume the conditions of Theorem
2
hold.
Furthermore, suppose
n>3m
,
\beta\leq L
, and either
\beta=\Omega(\sigma^{1/2}L^{1/2}n^{-1/4})
or
m=o(n)
.
If
\tilde{\mu}r^{3/2}\leq cn\beta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
for some sufficiently small constant
c>0
,
then any solution to the SDP in Equation (
22
) with
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
given by Equation (
24
) satisfies

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1}\leq n^{2}\exp\left\{-\frac{Cn\beta^{4}}{\sigma^{2}L^{2}}\right\}

(34)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.

The proof is included in Appendix
D.5
.
Next, we extend the results established under the simplified model in Equation (
24
) to the more general setting of Equation (
25
),
which allows for heterogeneous signal strengths on the support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
To accommodate this extension, we introduce the following condition on the noise distribution:

Assumption 5
.

For any
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
,
W_{ij}
is a continuous random variable.
The density function
f_{ij}
of
W_{ij}
is such that
f_{ij}(x)\geq f_{ij}(y)
for any
0\leq x\leq y
.

Combined with the symmetric-about-zero conditon in Assumption
1
, this monotonicity condition ensures that smaller absolute values of
W_{ij}
are more likely, which allows us to apply stochastic dominance arguments.

Corollary 2
.

Suppose the noise matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
, as well as Equation (
4
).
Assume the conditions of Theorem
2
hold.
If
n>3m
,
\tilde{\mu}r^{3/2}\leq cn\beta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
for some sufficiently small constant
c>0
and either
\beta=\Omega(\sigma^{1/2}L^{1/2}n^{-1/4})
or
m=o(n)
,
then under Assumption
5
,
any solution to the SDP in Equation (
22
) with
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
from Equation (
24
) satisfies

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1}\leq n^{2}\exp\left\{-\frac{Cn\beta^{4}}{\sigma^{2}L^{2}}\right\}

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.

When
L/\sigma=\Theta(1)
, as a consequence of Theorem
2
,
if
\beta=C\sigma n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
for some sufficiently large constant
C
,
any solution
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}
to the SDP in Equation (
22
) with
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
from Equation (
24
) obeys the bound

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right\|_{1}\leq cn

with high probability, where
c>0
is a small constant.
In this case, we can recover the exact support
{I_{\star}}
by sorting the rows of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}
according to their sums and selecting the indices corresponding to the
m
smallest row sums.
Moreover, if
\beta=C\sigma n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}(n/m)
for some sufficiently large
C
, then the solution satisfies

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right\|_{1}\leq cmn.

Under the above setting, for
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}
, at most
2cm
rows of
i\in{I_{\star}}
have row sum greater than
n/2
, while at most
2cm
rows
i\in I_{\star}^{c}
have row sum less than
n/2
, and we have
|I_{\star}^{c}\cap\hat{I}|+|I_{\star}\cap\hat{I}^{c}|\leq 4cm
.
Thus, when
c
is sufficiently small, this guarantees partial recovery of the support
{I_{\star}}
.

4.2.1
Minimax Recovery for SDP

Compared to the minimax lower bound in Corollary
1
,
the SDP approach imposes an additional structural requirement, namely, an entrywise lower bound on the signal strength of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
While it may be possible to derive stronger guarantees for SDP under weaker conditions, we instead focus on a restricted parameter space where this additional assumption holds.
Toward this end, define the restricted class

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{S}(\beta,n,m):=\left\{{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\operatorname{Sym}(n):|I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}|\leq m,~\begin{array}[]{cc}|B_{ij}|\geq\beta,&\forall i\in I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}},j\in I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{c}\\
B_{ij}=0,&\forall i,j\in I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{c}\end{array}\right\},

which enforces a uniform lower bound
\beta
on the signal magnitude between active and inactive nodes.

We show that over the class
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{S}(\beta,n,m)
, the SDP estimator achieves the minimax optimal rate for support recovery under Gaussian noise.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose we observe
N\geq 1
independent samples of the form

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\ell}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}_{\ell},~~~\ell=1,2,\dots,N,

where
\boldsymbol{W}_{\ell}
are independent symmetric noise matrices with i.i.d. entries
W_{\ell,ij}
drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
.
Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that for sufficiently large
n
and any
m^{\prime}\in[1,m]
with
m<n/3
, if

\beta<C\sigma n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(n/\left(3m^{\prime}\right)\right),

then

\inf_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\sup_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{S}(\beta,n,m)}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq 4m^{\prime}\left[\frac{1}{8}-\exp\left(-\frac{\left(m-m^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{2m}\right)\right].

Remark 6
.

Following Remark
5
, define

\operatorname{SNR}_{\beta}:=\frac{\min_{i\in{I_{\star}},j\in I_{\star}^{c}}|B_{ij}^{\star}|}{\sigma/\sqrt{N}}.

Theorem
8
shows that no method can achieve exact recovery
over
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{\prime}(\beta,m,n)
when

\operatorname{SNR}_{\beta}<Cn^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n.

We call
n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
the exact recovery threshold over
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{\prime}(\beta,m,n)
.
Following Remark
5
, we also call
n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}(n/m)
the partial recovery threshold over
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{\prime}(\beta,m,n)
.
As shown in Corollary
2
, both the exact and partial recovery thresholds over
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{\prime}(\beta,m,n)
can be achieved by SDP.

4.2.2
Truncated SDP

Compared to the minimax lower bound established in Theorem
8
, the estimation error bound in Theorem
7
demonstrates that the SDP estimator in Equation (
22
) achieves the minimax rate.
This result relies on two key assumptions:
(i) the noise is approximately isotropic,
and (ii) the truncation level satisfies
L\asymp\sigma
.
In this section, we examine the setting where the truncation level is much larger than the noise scale,
i.e.,
L\gg\sigma
.
We defer analysis of the heteroscedastic case to Section
4.3
.

When
L
is large,
the observed noise entries
W_{ij}^{(1)}
as defined in Equation (
3
)
may exhibit heavy-tailed behavior,
potentially following a power law distribution.
This presents challenges for both the SDP and the LSE, as the
\ell_{2}
norm is sensitive to large outliers and lacks robustness under such noise conditions.
A principled remedy is to replace the squared loss in the least squares formulation with a robust alternative, such as the Huber loss or Jaeckel’s dispersion function, and then develop a suitable convex relaxation or other computationally tractable approximation.
For an overview of such methods, see standard references on robust statistics, e.g.,
Hettmansperger and McKean, 2010
;
Huber and Ronchetti, 2011
.
We leave this direction for future work.
Instead, to demonstrate that our SDP-based approach can be extended to handle some heavy-tailed scenarios, we instead adopt a simple truncation strategy.

Consider the truncation operator
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(\cdot)
defined
entrywise as

\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(a)=a\wedge\gamma.

Let
\tau_{0}^{2}
be a threshold that serves as a proxy for the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
.
To reduce the impact of extreme noise values,
we apply truncation to the entrywise square of the residual matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
,
and solve the SDP using the truncated matrix

\overline{\boldsymbol{C}}:=\mathcal{T}_{\tau_{0}^{2}}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}})

in place of
\boldsymbol{C}
in Equation (
22
).

We impose the following regularity condition on the noise distribution.

Assumption 6
.

For
c\sigma\leq\tau_{0}\leq C\sigma
, where
c
is a sufficiently large constant, we have for any
|x|\leq\frac{\tau_{0}}{2}
,

|f(\tau_{0}+x)-f(\tau_{0})|\leq\gamma x,\quad\text{and }\gamma\leq c^{\prime}\sigma^{-2}(\tau_{0}/\sigma)^{-3}

for some sufficiently small constant
c^{\prime}>0
.

Remark 7
.

A distribution
f(x;\mu,\sigma)
is a
location-scale family
if it can be expressed as

f(x;\mu,\sigma)=\frac{1}{\sigma}g\left(\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma}\right),

where
g
is a standardized density function with mean
0
and variance
1
,
\mu
is a location parameter and
\sigma
is a scale parameter.
Assumption
6
is satisfied by location-scale family with
g
that has a small Lipschitz constant.
Indeed, one has

\left|f(x_{1})-f(x_{2})\right|\leq\gamma_{0}\left|x_{1}-x_{2}\right|\quad\text{for all }x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}

with

\gamma_{0}=\sigma^{-2}\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left|g^{\prime}(z)\right|.

Thus, if the Lipschitz constant of
g
is sufficiently small, then Assumption
6
holds.
For example, consider the
t_{3}
distribution, which has density

f(t)=\frac{2}{\eta\pi\sqrt{3}\left(1+\frac{t^{2}}{3\eta^{2}}\right)^{2}},

standard deviation of
\sqrt{3}\eta
and derivative given by

f^{\prime}(t)=\frac{4t}{3\eta^{3}\pi\sqrt{3}\left(1+\frac{t^{2}}{3\eta^{2}}\right)^{3}}\leq\frac{t/\eta}{4\eta^{2}\left(1+\frac{t^{2}}{3\eta^{2}}\right)^{3}}\leq\frac{9}{4\eta^{2}(t/\eta)^{4}}.

This satisfies Assumption
6
with
c_{1}=1/2
when
t>9\eta
. By picking a larger
t
, one can then choose a smaller value for
c_{1}
.

To select a suitable truncation level
\tau_{0}
,
we require it to lie within a constant factor of the noise standard deviation:

c_{0}\sigma\leq\tau_{0}\leq C_{0}\sigma

(35)

for some fixed constants
0<c_{0}<C_{0}
.
We obtain
\tau_{0}
using a subset of rows from the initial observation matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
.
Specifically, define the index set

\mathcal{S}:=\left\{\ell\in[n]:\sqrt{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\leq C\sqrt{\log n}\right\},

and denote the submatrix of
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
corresponding to the rows and columns in
\mathcal{S}
as

\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{\mathcal{S}}:=\left(Y^{(0)}_{ij}\right)_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}|\times|\mathcal{S}|}.

Using the estimated low-rank component
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
obtained from
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
,
we define the residual matrix as

\boldsymbol{W}^{\natural}:=\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}_{\mathcal{S}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\mathcal{S}},

(36)

and estimate the truncation threshold
\tau
via

\tau:=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{S}|}\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{\natural}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

(37)

The following theorem guarantees that
\tau
satisfies the required bounds in Equation (
35
) with high probability.
A proof is included in Appendix
D.8
.

Theorem 9
.

Suppose the observed matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
follows the model in Equation (
3
) with
|\mathcal{G}_{1}|=1
and that the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
satisfies Assumption
1
along with the condition in Equation (
4
).
If
\kappa r=O(1)
, then
the estimated threshold
\tau
satisfies the bound in Equation (
35
) with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.

With a properly-chosen threshold
\tau
, we now turn to its application in robustifying the SDP estimator.
We truncate the input matrix used in the SDP formulation and establish the following robust recovery guarantee.
Specifically, we truncate the input matrix in the SDP formulation by applying the entrywise operator
\mathcal{T}_{\tau^{2}}
to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
,
resulting in the modified input

\overline{\boldsymbol{C}}:=\mathcal{T}_{\tau^{2}}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}})
.
We then solve the SDP in Equation (
22
) using
\overline{\boldsymbol{C}}
in place of the original matrix
\boldsymbol{C}
.
To establish theoretical guarantees for this truncated formulation,
we impose an additional condition to control the second-order behavior of the truncated noise matrix.

Assumption 7
.

For any
\tau
satisfying
c_{0}\sigma\leq\tau\leq C_{0}\sigma
, let

\overline{\boldsymbol{W}}:=\mathcal{T}_{\tau^{2}}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}).

There exists a scalar
\sigma_{\tau}^{2}
such that
