\left\|\mathbb{E}(\overline{\boldsymbol{W}}\circ\overline{\boldsymbol{W}})-\sigma^{2}_{\tau}\boldsymbol{J}\right\|_{1}\leq C\sigma^{2}n^{3/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|\mathbb{E}(\overline{\boldsymbol{W}}\circ\overline{\boldsymbol{W}})-\sigma^{2}_{\tau}\boldsymbol{J}\right\|\leq C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n}.

Remark 8
.

By requiring uniform concentration for a range of
\tau
, Assumption
7
is stronger than Assumption
2
.
It is satisfied, for example, when the majority of entries in
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}
have identical variance and only a small subset exhibit larger deviations.

The next theorem establishes a non-asymptotic error bound for the truncated SDP estimator.

Theorem 10
.

Suppose the noise matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
, as well as Equation (
4
).
Assume the conditions of Theorem
2
hold.
When
n>3m
, if
\kappa=O(1)
,
\tilde{\mu}r^{3/2}=O(\sqrt{n})
, and either
\beta=\Omega(\sigma n^{-1/4})
or
m=o(n)
holds,
then under Assumption
5
,
6
and
7
,
any solution to the SDP in Equation (
22
) with
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
from Equation (
24
)
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{C}}
substituted for
\boldsymbol{C}
,
satisfies

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right\|_{1}\leq n^{2}\exp\left\{-\frac{Cn\beta^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\right\}

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.

A proof is provided in Appendix
D.9
.
As a result of Theorem
10
, we conclude that under some heavy-tailed noise regimes, the exact and partial recovery thresholds can still be achieved by SDP, after suitable entrywise truncation.

4.3
SDP: Multiple Observations

We now extend the above results to
|\mathcal{G}_{1}|>1
.
In general, if we do not impose any additional assumptions on
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}
for
j\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
, we may treat each
j
to be its own singleton group
\mathcal{G}_{1,j}
, and apply the methods introduced in the previous section independently to recover the support
{I_{\star}}_{j}
for each
j\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
.
In particular, the SDP-based procedures introduced earlier remain applicable and yield accurate recovery under the same conditions as Theorems
7
and
10
.

However, when multiple observations share the same underlying difference matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
, i.e.,
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{j}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
for several
j\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
, we can refine both the estimation procedure and the accompanying analysis.
In Section
4.1
, we demonstrated that Assumption
2
is essential to attain the minimax lower bound in Theorem
5
when
|\mathcal{G}_{1}|=1
.
Remarkably, we now demonstrate that this assumption can be relaxed in the presence of multiple independent observations sharing the same
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
Specifically, suppose we are given two independent copies
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{2}
following Equation (
3
) with
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{1}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{2}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
Constructing
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{2}
from Equation (
7
) in Algorithm
1
,
we set
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{C}}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{1}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{2}
and run the SDP in Equation (
22
) on
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{C}}
in place of
\boldsymbol{C}
.
Owing to the independence of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{2}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{C}}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\circ{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
This construction effectively removes the bias introduced by heteroscedastic noise, enabling accurate estimation under weaker assumptions.

Theorem 11
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
2
hold.
Under Assumption
1
, if
\tilde{\mu}r^{3/2}\leqslant cn\beta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
, any solution
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}
to the SDP in Equation (
22
), when applied to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{C}}
in place of
\boldsymbol{C}
and with
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
given by Equation (
25
), satisfies

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right\|_{1}\lesssim n^{2}\max\left\{\exp\left(-\frac{Cn\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}\right),\exp\left(-\frac{Cn\beta^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\right)\right\}

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.

Theorem
11
holds under significantly weaker assumptions compared to Theorem
7
and Corollary
2
.
In particular, it does not require the near-isotropic noise condition in Assumption
2
,
nor does it rely on Assumption
5
regarding the noise density.
Moreover, the bound remains valid for any
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
that satisfies the general structure in Equation (
25
).
When the noise parameters are such that
L\asymp\sigma
,
the SDP based on multiple observations also achieves the exact and partial recovery thresholds established in Theorem
8
.
Moreover, this approach naturally extends to
N
independent observations via sample splitting and averaging: divide the
N
observations into two groups of size
N/2
, compute the empirical averages within each group to obtain
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{2}
, and apply the same procedure using the elementwise product
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{C}}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{1}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{2}
.
As discussed above in Section
4.2.2
, when
L\gg\sigma
, the performance of the SDP might be suboptimal due to heavy-tailed noise.
We anticipate that a truncation-based approach similar to that presented in Section
4.2.2
can be applied in the present setting, but we leave a thorough investigation of this regime to future work.

Remark 9
.

A similar phenomenon to that described in Theorem
11
, in which access to multiple networks leads to a nontrivial improvement, has also been observed in
Ghoshdastidar et al., 2020
.
In that work, the authors studied the problem of two-sample graph testing under Bernoulli edge weights, with the goal of determining whether two collections of graphs are drawn from the same distribution.
In contrast, our focus is on weighted networks and recovery of the node support of the difference matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
The underlying intuition is that when only a single observation is available, the difference matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
behaves similar to a noise matrix, necessitating stronger assumptions for recovery or testing to be feasible.
By contrast, when multiple observations are available, the signal component
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
must remain consistent across them, which enables accurate recovery under substantially weaker conditions.

4.4
Model Selection in SDP

One important practical consideration is the selection of the parameter
m
.
Under a homoscedastic Gaussian model, the entries of the matrix outside the support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
follow a noncentral chi-squared distribution, specifically
\chi^{2}_{1}(\Delta^{2}_{ij})
, where
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
is the residual matrix defined in Equation (
8
).
Assume without loss of generality that the set
\mathcal{U}
in Step
4
is
[n]
.
For all
i\in I_{\star}^{c}
, it follows from Theorem
2
and
\chi^{2}
-concentration inequality that

\left|\sum_{j\in I_{\star}^{c}}\widetilde{Y}^{2}_{ij}-\sigma^{2}(n-m)\right|\leq C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{(n-m)\log n}+C\sigma^{2}\kappa^{4}\tilde{\mu}r+C\sigma^{2}r\log n

holds with high probability.
When
\kappa^{4}\tilde{\mu}r=o(\sqrt{n\log n})
and
r=o(\sqrt{n/\log n})
, we will thus have that

-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{(n-m)\log n}\leq\max_{i\in I_{\star}^{c}}\sum_{j\in I_{\star}^{c}}\widetilde{Y}^{2}_{ij}-\sigma^{2}(n-m)\leq C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{(n-m)\log n}.

This suggests using a heuristic threshold of
C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{(n-m)\log n}
to guide the choice of
m
.
A practical selection strategy is as follows.

Algorithm 2

Heuristic Selection of
m
for SDP

1:

Observed matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
, noise variance estimate
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
, initial guess
m_{0}

2:

Set
m\leftarrow m_{0}

3:

repeat

4:

Solve the SDP in Equation (
22
) with cardinality parameter
m
, and obtain solution
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}

5:

Let
\hat{I}
be the set of indices corresponding to the
m
smallest row sums of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}

6:

Compute the maximum row sum in
\hat{I}^{c}
:
s_{\max}\leftarrow\max_{i\in\hat{I}^{c}}\sum_{j\in\hat{I}^{c}}\widetilde{Y}^{2}_{ij}

7:

Compute heuristic threshold
t\leftarrow c\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\sqrt{(n-m)\log n}
for some constant
c>0

8:

if

|s_{\max}-\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(n-m)|>t

then

9:

Increase
m\leftarrow m+1

10:

else

11:

Decrease
m\leftarrow m-1

12:

end

if

13:

until

s_{\max}>t
and the maximum row sum drops below threshold when
m
is decreased by one

14:

return
Final value of
m

The above procedure combines an SDP with a hard-thresholding step. While hard-thresholding alone is generally insufficient for recovering the support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
(see Theorem
14
), it remains a useful diagnostic tool for detecting signal components that may be excluded from the estimated support set
\hat{I}
. In particular, evaluating the maximum row sum over
\hat{I}^{c}
can help identify whether the current choice of
m
is too small. To implement the thresholding rule, one may estimate the noise variance
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
using the same approach employed in constructing the truncation threshold for the SDP, as defined in Equation (
37
).
In this work, we assume that either
m
is known a priori or there is a preferred choice based on domain knowledge.
A complete development of a data-driven selection procedure is an interesting direction for future research, but we do not pursue it here.

4.5
Group Lasso

While semidefinite programming offers a principled framework for exact and partial recovery of the support set
{I_{\star}}
, it can be computationally intensive for large-scale problems.
Using a general-purpose interior-point method to exactly solve the SDP in Equation (
22
) requires a per-iteration runtime complexity of
O(n^{6})

(
Vandenberghe et al., 2005
)
.
Faster implementations exist, but they still require at least
O(n^{4})
runtime complexity per iteration in our setting
(
Jiang et al., 2020
)
.
As discussed earlier in Section
2
,
an alternative and potentially more scalable approach is to exploit the sparsity structure of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
through a structured penalization framework.
In particular,
the overlapping group Lasso has been widely used in the network analysis literature, including applications in graphical model selection
(
Mohan et al., 2014
)
, network regression
(
Kessler et al., 2022
)
, and classification
(
Relión et al., 2019
)
.
On the theoretical side,
Mohan et al., 2014
provided sufficient conditions under which the overlapping group Lasso yields node-sparse solutions, though without guarantees on support recovery.

Relión et al., 2019
later established high-probability support recovery guarantees under the irrepresentability condition, which unfortunately does not hold in our setting.

To formulate the overlapping group Lasso, for each
i\in[n]
,
we define the
i
-th group to be the collection of parameters

\{B_{ij}:j\in[n]\}\cup\{B_{ji}:j\in[n]\},

resulting in
n
overlapping groups.
Each parameter appears in two groups, and we maintain the symmetry of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
via the reparameterization
B_{ij}=v_{ij}+v_{ji}
for all
i,j\in[n]
.
Applied to an observed matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}=[y_{ij}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
, the overlapping group Lasso solves the following convex optimization problem:

\min_{\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}(y_{ij}-v_{ij}-v_{ji})^{2}+\lambda\Omega({\boldsymbol{B}}),

(38)

where
\lambda\geq 0
is the regularization parameter,
\Omega({\boldsymbol{B}})
is the group Lasso penalty defined as

\Omega({\boldsymbol{B}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{ij}^{2}}.

This formulation has a natural interpretation: each
v_{ij}
can be viewed as the contribution of node
i
to edge
(i,j)
.
Defining

\alpha_{i}:=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{ij}^{2}},\quad\text{for }i\in[n],

(39)

the group Lasso selects the
i
-th group if and only if
\alpha_{i}>0
.
To solve the optimization problem in Equation (
38
),
following
Mohan et al., 2014
,
we adopt an approach based on the alternating direction method of multipliers approach
(see
Boyd et al., 2011
, for a comprehensive review)
.
Details are provided in Algorithm
5
in Appendix
E
.

We also extend the theoretical analysis of the overlapping group Lasso in our setting.
A particularly useful structural property that we establish is the existence of a stable solution path:

If
\alpha_{i}>0
for some penalty parameter
\lambda_{0}
, then
\alpha_{i}>0
for all
\lambda<\lambda_{0}
.

In other words, the overlapping group Lasso behaves like a greedy procedure:
once a node enters the model (i.e., its group is selected), it remains active as the regularization strength
\lambda
decreases.
A formal statement and proof of this property can be found in Lemma
27
in Appendix
E
.
While not central to our main goal of establishing exact recovery guarantees for
{I_{\star}}
,
this result provides insight into the behavior of group Lasso estimators and may guide practical strategies for algorithm choice.

Rather than analyzing the full solution path of the group Lasso estimator,
we focus on its performance near the exact recovery threshold.
Specifically, Theorem
12
shows that under Gaussian noise and appropriate conditions on the signal strength and sparsity, the group Lasso achieves exact support recovery with high probability.
A proof is given in Appendix
F.1
.

Theorem 12
.

Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
be the symmetric matrix obtained from Step
4
in Algorithm
1
,
where the data-generating model follows Equation (
3
) with signal matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
and noise matrix
\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}
.
Assume that
\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}
is a symmetric noise matrix with entries
(W^{(1)}_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}
drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
.
Suppose that
n>3m
,
\kappa^{2}\sqrt{\tilde{\mu}r}=O(n^{1/4}\log^{-1/4}n)
, and that

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[m]}\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\asymp\min_{i\in[m]}\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}=\Theta(\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n),

(40)

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[m]^{c}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{ik}^{\star 2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=O(\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n)~~~\text{ and }

\displaystyle\min_{i\in[m]}\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{\ell\in[m]^{c}}\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{\ell k}^{\star 2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}

for some suitably large constant
C>0
.
Then, for sufficiently large
n
, there exists a regularization parameter
\lambda>0
such that the group Lasso estimator in Equation (
38
),
when applied to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
, selects exactly the true support set
{I_{\star}}
with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.

Theorem
12
characterizes a parameter regime in which group Lasso succeeds with high probability.
Notably, this regime coincides with the minimax exact recovery threshold described in Remark
5
, highlighting the near-optimality of the method.
Interestingly, under the same set of conditions, a much simpler procedure, namely top-
m
hard thresholding, also achieves exact support recovery.
Theorem
13
formalizes this observation.
Its proof is given in Appendix
F.3
.

Theorem 13
.

Under the same set of conditions as Theorem
12
,
the hard thresholding method selects all and only groups from
{I_{\star}}
with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.

While the preceding results demonstrate that both the group Lasso and hard thresholding can successfully recover the true support under suitable conditions,
their inherently greedy nature makes them vulnerable in more challenging regimes.
The following result formalizes a specific setting in which both methods provably fail to achieve exact support recovery.
The proof is given in Appendix
F.4
.

Theorem 14
.

Consider a signal-plus-noise model of the form

\boldsymbol{Y}={\boldsymbol{B}}+\boldsymbol{W},

where
\boldsymbol{W}
is a symmetric matrix with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
entries on and above diagonal.
Let the true node support be
{I_{\star}}=[m]
, and define the signal matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}
with entries

B_{ij}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }i,j\in[m]\text{ or }i,j\in[m]^{c};\\
\beta_{1}&\text{ if }i=m+1,j\in[m]\text{ or }i\in[m],j=m+1\\
\beta_{2}&\text{ if }i\in[m],j\in[m+1]^{c}\text{ or }j\in[m],i\in[m+1]^{c},\end{cases}

(41)

where
\beta_{2}=C\sigma n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
for some constant
C
sufficiently large.
Then, both the group Lasso and the top-
m
hard-thresholding approach fail to recover the true support
{I_{\star}}
with high probability whenever

\beta_{1}\geq C\sigma\frac{(n\log n)^{1/4}}{\sqrt{m}}

for some constant
C
sufficiently large.

Remark 10
.

When
\beta_{1}=\Theta(\sigma)
,
Theorem
14
implies that the group Lasso and hard thresholding methods fail to exactly recover the support
{I_{\star}}
when the sparsity level satisfies
m=\Omega(\sqrt{n})
.
Together with Theorems
12
and
13
,
this highlights a nuanced landscape:
although these methods can succeed under favorable signal-to-noise and sparsity conditions,
their effectiveness is fundamentally constrained by their greedy selection procedure.
Compared to semidefinite programming (SDP), group Lasso and hard thresholding offer computational advantages.
However, due to the NP-hardness of the underlying problem, their effective regime is fundamentally restricted.
It is plausible that analogous failure modes may arise in related models, such as the graphical Lasso, and a more thorough investigation into such phenomena is a promising direction for future research.

5
Estimating the Common Structure

We now turn to the final stage of the procedure, which refines the estimation of the low-rank signal component
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
using the residual data.
Specifically, we analyze Step
8
of Algorithm
1
, where spectral estimation is performed after we have subtracted off the estimated node-sparse component.
To reduce the bias introduced by node-wise sparsity, we follow the asymmetric rearrangement strategy proposed by
Chen et al., 2021a
.
For clarity, we first focus on the simplified case where both
|\mathcal{G}_{0}|=1
and
|\mathcal{G}_{1}|=1
,
as described in Equations (
2
) and (
3
).

Let
\hat{I}_{1}
denote the estimated node support obtained from Step
6
of Algorithm
1
.
We define the matrix
\boldsymbol{M}_{1}
as the remaining of
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}_{1}
after zeroing out rows and columns corresponding to
\hat{I}_{1}
:

M_{1,ij}:=\begin{cases}Y^{(1)}_{1,ij}&\mbox{ if }(i,j)\in\hat{I}^{c}_{1}\times\hat{I}^{c}_{1},\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise }.\end{cases}

(42)

Notably, if
{I_{\star}}\subseteq\hat{I}_{1}
, then the entries in
\boldsymbol{M}_{1}
are independent elements drawn from a low-rank-plus-noise model.
Define

M_{0,ij}=\begin{cases}Y^{(0)}_{0,ij}&\mbox{ if }(i,j)\in\hat{I}^{c}_{1}\times\hat{I}^{c}_{1}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(43)

The asymmetric rearranged matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
obtained from
\boldsymbol{M}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}
is then given by

M_{ij}=\begin{cases}M_{1,ij}&\mbox{ if }1\leq i<j\leq n\\
M_{0,ij}&\mbox{ if }1\leq j\leq i\leq n.\end{cases}

Since restricting a low-rank matrix to a submatrix cannot increase its rank, the rearranged matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
still adheres to a low-rank-plus-noise structure.
For notational simplicity, we continue to use
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
to denote the low-rank signal component corresponding to the submatrix
\boldsymbol{M}
, and denote its spectral decomposition as
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}
.
See Remark
11
for further discussion of this notation.
To differentiate its incoherence parameter from that of the original matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
, we denote its incoherece parameter as
\bar{\mu}
with

\bar{\mu}:=\frac{n}{r}\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|^{2}_{2,\infty}.

(44)

Given
\hat{I}_{1}={I_{\star}}
, this construction ensures that the entries of
\boldsymbol{M}
are independent,
and we have

\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{H},

(45)

where
\boldsymbol{H}
is an asymmetric noise matrix with independent entries satisfying the same set of distributional assumptions as those in Assumption
1
and Equation (
4
).
In fact, the results in this section holds under weaker assumption than Equation (
4
):

L\ll\sigma\min\left\{\frac{n}{\bar{\mu}\log^{3/2}n},\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log^{3}n}}\right\}

(46)

Throughout this section, we assume that the true support
{I_{\star}}
is successfully recovered,
i.e.,
{I_{\star}}\subseteq\hat{I}_{1}
.
As a reminder, the regime under which recovering
{I_{\star}}
is possible is analyzed in Section
4
and the conditions under which this recovery is guaranteed are established in Section
4
.
Since
\hat{I}_{1}
recover
{I_{\star}}
with high probability, without loss of generality, we can assume that
\hat{I}^{c}_{1}=[n]
.
Following the same reason as given in the beginning of Section
4
, we also assume that the selected node set
\mathcal{U}
from Step
4
is equal to
[n]
.

Remark 11
.

We give another reason for using the same notation
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
for the low-rank signal component of the rearranged matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
.
If we have multiple observations in
\mathcal{G}_{0}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}
with estimated node support
\hat{I}_{k}
for
k\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
, then we can similarly obtain

M_{k,ij}=\begin{cases}Y^{(0)}_{0,ij}&\mbox{ if }(i,j)\in\hat{I}^{c}_{k}\times\hat{I}^{c}_{k}\\
*&\mbox{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(47)

If
|\mathcal{G}_{0}|\geq 2
, then we can divide the matrices in
\mathcal{G}_{0}
into two sets
\mathcal{G}_{0,1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{0,2}
with size difference at most one.
We can also divide matrices
\boldsymbol{M}_{k}
for
k\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
into two sets
\mathcal{G}_{1,1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1,2}
with size difference at most one.
Define
\Omega_{l}:=\{k\in\mathcal{G}_{0,l}\cup\mathcal{G}_{1,l}:M_{k,ij}\neq*\}
for
l=1,2
.
For
l=1,2
, we then combine
\mathcal{G}_{0,l}
with
\mathcal{G}_{1,l}
to obtain an averaged matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{avg}}_{l}
with

M^{\text{avg}}_{l,ij}=\frac{1}{|\Omega_{l}|}\sum_{k\in\Omega_{l}}M_{k,ij}.

We can then asymmetrically arrange the entries of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{avg}}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{avg}}_{2}
to obtain the final matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
.
Under this construction, the obtained matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
indeed has
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
as its low-rank signal component.

With the above setup, we now pause to provide an overview of the goal of this section.

•

The primary objective is to construct an improved entry-wise estimator for the low-rank signal matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
Although Theorem
2
establishes an entry-wise error bound for the initial spectral estimator
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
,
we show that this estimator is sub-optimal in the minimax sense.
In Section
5.2
, we propose a refined estimator that achieves the optimal entry-wise rate.

•

While restricting the original signal matrix to a submatrix alters its eigenspace structure,
accurate estimation of the resulting eigenspace remains essential, both as an independent objective and as a key ingredient in achieving optimal entry-wise recovery.
In Section
5.1
, we analyze the estimation error of linear forms of the eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
and derive sharp perturbation bounds.
We also introduce an improved eigenspace estimator that achieves the minimax-optimal rate.

We adopt the estimation method proposed in
Cheng et al., 2021
,
which provides sharp entry-wise error bounds for eigenvectors under certain perturbation regimes.
Let the right and left eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{M}
be

\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}=\lambda_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{M}^{\top}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}=\lambda_{l}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}

(48)

for all
l\in[r]
, where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{r}
are eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{M}
.
To ensure that the leading eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{M}
provide a reliable estimate,
we impose a lower bound on the minimal signal strength:

Assumption 8
.

There exists some sufficiently large constant
C>0
such that

\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\geq C\max\left\{\sigma\sqrt{n\log^{3}n},L\log^{3}n\right\}.

(49)

To establish fine-grained eigenspace perturbation bounds, we introduce the notion of eigengaps.
For each
l\in[r]
, define the eigengap of the
l
-th eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
as

\delta_{l}^{\star}:=\begin{cases}\min_{1\leq k\leq r,k\neq l}\left|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}\right|,&\mbox{ if }l>1\\
\infty&\mbox{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(50)

We caution the reader not to confuse this quantity with the matrix
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
used earlier in Section
3

to denote the estimation error of the low-rank initializer
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
.
For notational convenience, we also define

\delta^{\star}_{\max}:=\max_{l\in[r]}\delta^{\star}_{l}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\star}_{\min}:=\min_{l\in[r]}\delta^{\star}_{l}.

To control the perturbation of individual eigenspaces,
we require that each one-dimensional signal eigenspace is sufficiently well-separated from the others.
The following eigengap condition guarantees such separation:

Assumption 9
.

There exists a sufficiently large constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\delta^{\star}_{\min}\geq C\kappa^{3}r^{2}\!\max\!\left\{\!\frac{\bar{\mu}L\!\log^{5}\!n}{n},\sigma\log^{7/2}n\right\}.

(51)

5.1
Linear form of eigenvectors

We begin by considering estimating a linear form
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}
,
that is, the projection of
\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}
onto a known direction
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
This formulation generalizes the entry-wise estimation problem, which corresponds to taking
\boldsymbol{a}
to be a standard basis vector.
To estimate this quantity, we adopt the estimator proposed by
Cheng et al., 2021
,

\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}:=\min\left\{\sqrt{\left|\frac{\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}\right)}{\boldsymbol{w}_{l}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}\right|},\|\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}\right\},

(52)

where
\boldsymbol{u}_{l}
and
\boldsymbol{w}_{l}
are the left- and right-eigenvectors of the observed matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
,
defined in Equation (
48
).
Our analysis builds on a combination of techniques from
Yan and Levin, 2025
and
Cheng et al., 2021
,
and improves the original results in
Cheng et al., 2021
in terms of the dependence on the incoherence parameter
\bar{\mu}
of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
(see Equation (
44
) for the reason to use the notation
\bar{\mu}
rather than
\mu
).

Before presenting the formal result, we provide some intuition behind the estimator.
By the standard Neumann expansion (see Lemma
35
in Appendix
I
),
we have

\boldsymbol{u}_{l}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{j}}{\lambda_{l}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right)\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{j}}{\lambda_{l}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right)\left\{\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{l}^{s}}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}\right\}.

(53)

From Theorem
24
in Appendix
I
, we know that
\lambda_{l}\approx\lambda^{\star}_{l}
.
Therefore, when the condition number
\kappa
obeys
\kappa=O(1)
, it follows that

\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\approx\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{j}}{\lambda^{\star}_{l}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right)\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}O(1)\cdot\left\{\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{l}^{s}}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}\right\}.

Moreover, Theorem
22
refines this expansion and gives the sharper bound

\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\approx\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{j}}{\lambda^{\star}_{l}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right)\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}+\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\right),

(54)

where
\widetilde{O}(\cdot)
denotes that we are suppressing logarithmic factors.
This indicates that
\boldsymbol{u}_{l}
is well-approximated to first order by a linear combination of the true eigenvectors
\{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}\}_{j\in[r]}
.
This accurate first-order expansion guides the development of methods to perform bias-correction when estimating the linear form
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}
.

To formalize the above intuition, we first rederive certain results from
Cheng et al., 2021
,
focusing on the empirical right eigenvectors and obtaining an improved dependence on the incoherence parameter
\bar{\mu}
.
The proof of the following theorem is provided in Appendix
G.1
.

Theorem 15
.

Consider the model given in Equation (
45
) and suppose that the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{H}
with independent entries satisfies Assumption
1
and Equation (
4
).
Assume that
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
satisfies Assumption
8
and
\delta^{\star}_{\min}
satisfies Assumption
9
.
Then

1.

(
\ell_{2}
-perturbation) It holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
that for all
l\in[r]
,

\min\left\{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\pm\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\|_{2}\right\}\lesssim\kappa\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}+\frac{\sigma}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)}\kappa^{2}r\log^{7/2}n,

(55)

\left|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{\kappa^{4}r\log^{7}n}}{\delta^{\star}_{l}},

(56)

and

\left|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right|\geq 1-O\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}_{\max}\kappa^{4}r^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}_{\max}n\log n}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\right)^{2}}\right);

(57)

2.

(perturbation of linear forms) For any fixed vector
\boldsymbol{a}
with
\|\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}=1
and any
l\in[r]
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
, we have

\displaystyle\min\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\!\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\pm\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim~\sigma_{\max}\sqrt{\kappa^{2}r\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\kappa r\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\star}\right|}{\left|\lambda_{l}^{\star}-\lambda_{k}^{\star}\right|}\right\}

(58)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\sigma_{\max}^{2}\kappa^{4}r^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta_{l}^{\star}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\kappa^{2}\sigma_{\max}^{2}n\log n}{\left(\lambda_{\min}^{\star}\right)^{2}}\right)\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right|.

3.

(
\ell_{\infty}
-perturbation) For any
l\in[r]
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
, we have

\displaystyle\min\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\pm\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma_{\max}\sqrt{\kappa^{2}r\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\frac{\kappa r}{\delta^{\star}_{l}}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}}\right\}

(59)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\sigma_{\max}^{2}\kappa^{4}r^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\kappa^{2}\sigma_{\max}^{2}n\log n}{\left(\lambda_{\min}^{\star}\right)^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}}.

Remark 12
.

By Theorem
15
, we have the approximation

\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\approx\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\left|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}\right|}\right)

for
k\neq l
.
In addition, ignoring the terms involving eigengaps, we also have

\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\approx 1-\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\right)^{2}}\right).

We observe that these approximations resemble the asymptotic behavior seen in the BBP phase transition limit
(
Benaych-Georges and Nadakuditi, 2011
)
.
Unfortunately, deriving the precise limiting form in our setting is challenging due to the heteroscedasticity in the variance structure of
\boldsymbol{H}
.

Armed with the previous results, we are now ready to establish an error bound for the improved estimator defined in Equation (
52
).
To maintain technical simplicity, we state the following results under
\kappa=O(1)
.
The precise dependence on
\kappa
can be found in the Appendix.

Theorem 16
.

Suppose that
\kappa=O(1)
and
r=O(1)
.
Under the same assumptions as Theorem
15
, for any
l\in[r]
, the estimation error of the improved estimator in Equation (
52
) is bounded by

\min\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}\pm\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right|\lesssim\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}\right|}{\left|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}\right|}\right\}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\log^{7}n}+\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right|\frac{\sigma^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}}.

(60)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
.

The proof is included in Appendix
G.2
.
To establish the tightness of Theorem
16
, we have the following minimax result adapted from Theorem 3 in
Cheng et al., 2021
.

Theorem 17
.

Suppose that we observe
N\geq 1
independent copies of symmetric matrices

\{\boldsymbol{M}^{(l)}:l\in[N]\},

where
\boldsymbol{M}^{(l)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}
.
Suppose that
\{\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}:l\in[N]\}
has independent Gaussian entries on and above diagonal, with

W_{ij}^{(l)}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\left(\sigma_{ij}^{(l)}\right)^{2}\right)

and
\sigma_{\min}\leq\sigma_{ij}^{(l)}\leq\sigma
for all
i,j\in[n]
and
l\in[N]
.
Let

\mathcal{M}_{0}(\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}\mid\operatorname{rank}(\boldsymbol{A})=r,\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{A})=\lambda^{\star}_{i}\;(i\in[r]),\left\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{2}\right\}.

Assume that
|\lambda^{\star}_{l}|\geq 4\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{n/N}
and
\delta^{\star}_{l}\geq\sigma_{\min}/\sqrt{N}
.
Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\inf_{\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(\boldsymbol{M}^{\star})}\mathbb{E}\left[\min\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}\pm\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}(\boldsymbol{A})\right|\right]\geq c\left\{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right|\frac{\sigma_{\min}^{2}}{N\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{\sqrt{N}}\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\star}\right|}{\left|\lambda_{l}^{\star}-\lambda_{k}^{\star}\right|}\right\}.

The proof largely follows that of Theorem 3 in
Cheng et al., 2021
, which uses Le Cam’s two-point method.
The main difference is that we have
N
independent observed matrices, resulting in a factor of
N
in the upper bound of the KL-divergence between the two hypotheses, which in turn leads to a factor of
1/\sqrt{N}
in the lower bound.
We omit the proof for the sake of brevity.
Comparing Equation (
60
) with the rate in Theorem
17
, we see that the estimator in Equation (
52
) is optimal up to a logarithmic factor under Gaussian noise.

For any
l\in[r]
, taking
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2},\dots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n}
in Equation (
60
), the following corollary follows immediately from Theorems
16
and
15
.

Corollary 3
.

Suppose that
\kappa r=O(1)
.
Under the same assumptions in Theorem
15
, for any
l\in[r]
, the estimation error of the improved estimator in Equation (
52
) is bounded by

\min\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l}\pm\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sigma\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\mu}/n}}{\delta^{\star}_{l}}\right\}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}}

(61)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
, where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l}
is given by

\widehat{u}_{l,i}:=\operatorname{sgn}(u_{l,i})\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{i},l}\right|,\quad i\in[n].

(62)

More generally, writing

\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}:=\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1},\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{2},\dots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{r}\right),

(63)

we have that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
,

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{S}=\operatorname{diag}(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{r}),\\
s_{l}\in\{-1,1\},\;l\in[r]\end{subarray}}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{S}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\sigma\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\mu}/n}}{\delta^{\star}_{\min}}\right\}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}.

(64)

When the incoherence parameter satisfies
\sqrt{\bar{\mu}/n}/\delta^{\star}_{\min}\asymp\left(\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\right)^{-1}
, we obtain the row-wise bound

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{S}=\operatorname{diag}(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{r}),\\
s_{l}\in\{-1,1\},\;l\in[r]\end{subarray}}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{S}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

This rate matches the known minimax lower-bound up to logarithmic factors, provided that the eigengap is sufficiently large.
See, for example, Remark 7 in
Agterberg and Zhang, 2024
.

Remark 13
.

When estimating the linear form
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}
from a single observation
\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}
, where
\boldsymbol{W}
is a symmetric noise matrix, our analysis reveals that the term
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}
can introduce a non-negligible bias.
Due to the heteroscedasticity of the noise, removing this bias without additional samples is challenging.
A similar issue arises when estimating the eigenspace
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
: the term
\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}
introduces systematic bias that is difficult to correct in the single-sample setting.
One possible remedy might involve diagonal deletion techniques, as explored in
Zhang et al., 2022
.
In contrast, our goal is to demonstrate that with multiple independent network copies, one can construct estimators that match the minimax optimal rate using conceptually simpler methods and more straightforward analysis.

If additional independent samples are available, the eigenspace can be estimated more accurately under weaker eigengap conditions.
Suppose that we observe two extra independent copies,
\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{(2)}
, each generated as

\boldsymbol{M}^{(i)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}_{i},\quad\text{for }i=1,2.

Here,
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
are symmetric noise matrices satisfying Assumption
1
and Equation (
4
).
This could be obtained from having multiple copies from either
\mathcal{G}_{0}
or
\mathcal{G}_{1}
.
Define the matrix

\boldsymbol{G}:=\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{M}^{(2)}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\right)^{-1}

(65)

and its symmetrized version

\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{G}+\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\right).

(66)

Let the spectral decomposition of
\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}
be

\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\text{symm}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-2}_{\text{symm}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{\top}_{\text{symm}}

(67)

and define the inverse square root matrix

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\text{symm}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}_{\text{symm}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{\top}_{\text{symm}}.

(68)

We then obtain the following result, the proof of which is provided in Appendix
G.4
.

Theorem 18
.

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
15
, the
\ell_{2,\infty}
estimation error of the corrected estimator
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
is bounded by

\min_{\boldsymbol{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{r}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{O}\right\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa r\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}

(69)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
.

Notably, by leveraging two additional independent networks and a simple correction step, the estimator
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
achieves a near-optimal
\ell_{2,\infty}
error rate.
This result holds under significantly milder eigengap conditions compared to single-copy estimators, highlighting the benefit of using additional copies to perform bias-correction in recovering low-rank eigenspace.

5.2
Entrywise Estimation

We now conclude by addressing the entry-wise estimation of the low-rank signal matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
Using the best rank-
r
approximation of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}
, the spectral estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
yields an entry-wise estimation error of the form

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\mathrm{spec}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sigma\kappa^{2}\bar{\mu}r\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}

(70)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-5})
(See Corollary 4.3 in
Chen et al., 2021b
).
This bound has a suboptimal dependence on the incoherence parameter
\bar{\mu}
.
We will give more comments on this in Section
6
.
By using the improved eigenvector estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}
from Corollary
3
along with the empirical eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{M}
,
we obtain the following bound on the entry-wise estimation error.

Corollary 4
.

Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}
, where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}
is given by Equation (
63
) and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is the diagonal matrix of eigenvalues of the observed matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
given by Equation (
45
).
Suppose that
\kappa r=O(1)
.
Under the same assumptions as Theorem
15
, it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
that

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}\log^{7}n}{n}}+\frac{\sigma\lambda^{\star}_{\max}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\delta^{\star}_{\min}}\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}.

The proof is stated in Appendix
G.5
.
While Corollary
4
shows that accurate entry-wise estimation is achievable, it still requires a sufficiently large eigengap to control the second term in the upper bound.
To further improve the rate, especially under weaker eigengap conditions, we make use of the corrected estimator
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
from Theorem
18
, which leverages additional independent copies.
The following result provides a sharper entry-wise bound for this improved estimator, provided that such additional independent networks are available.
A proof is given in Appendix
G.6
.

Theorem 19
.

Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
be as defined in Equation (
68
).
Under the same assumptions as Theorem
18
, it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
that

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sigma\kappa^{2}r^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}\log^{7}n}{n}}+\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}\frac{\delta^{\star}_{\max}}{\delta_{\min}^{\star}}\sigma\kappa^{3}r^{3}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}.

This result shows that incorporating additional independent observations enables near-optimal entry-wise recovery of the low-rank structure,
even under weak eigengap conditions.
We suspect that the dependence on the eigengap likely reflects a technical artifact of the proof rather than a fundamental limitation, and we conjecture that a more careful analysis may eliminate this dependence altogether.

Finally, we complement our upper bounds with a matching minimax lower bound for the entry-wise estimation error in the rank-one case.
The proof is given in Appendix
H.2
.

Theorem 20
.

For
1\leq\mu\leq n
, define the parameter set

\mathcal{M}(\mu,\sigma_{\min})=\left\{\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}=\lambda^{\star}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}:\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{n}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}\|_{\infty}\geq 2\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{n}},\;\left|\lambda^{\star}\right|\geq\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{n}\right\}.

(71)

Suppose that the observed matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
is given by Equation (
45
), where
\boldsymbol{H}
has independent Gaussian entries, with

H_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{ij}^{2})

and
0<\sigma_{\min}\leq\sigma_{ij}\leq\sigma
for all
i,j\in[n]
.
We have

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}~\sup_{\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\in\mathcal{M}(\mu,\sigma_{\min})}~\mathbb{E}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\right\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{8}\sqrt{\frac{3\mu}{n}}.

An immediate question is whether access to multiple independent copies of the matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
can lead to a fundamental improvement in the minimax rate for entrywise estimation.
The following result shows that the benefit of having
N
independent samples is limited to a standard variance reduction factor of
1/\sqrt{N}
.
No further gain is possible in terms of dependence on other parameters.

Corollary 5
.

Suppose that we observe
N\geq 1
independent matrices
\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{M}^{(2)},\dots,\boldsymbol{M}^{(N)}
from the model specified in Equation (
45
), where
\{\boldsymbol{H}^{(l)}:l\in[N]\}
has independent Gaussian entries, with

H^{(l)}_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\left(\sigma^{(l)}_{ij}\right)^{2}\right)

and
0<\sigma_{\min}\leq\sigma^{(l)}_{ij}\leq\sigma
for all
i,j\in[n]
and
l\in[N]
.
Over the set of rank-one signal matrices
\mathcal{M}(\mu,\sigma_{\min})
as defined in Equation (
71
), we have

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}~\sup_{\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\in\mathcal{M}(\mu,\sigma_{\min})}~\mathbb{E}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\right\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{8\sqrt{N}}\sqrt{\frac{3\mu}{n}},

where the infimum is over all estimators of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
that take as input the observed matrices
\{\boldsymbol{M}^{(l)}\}_{l\in[N]}
.

This corollary quantifies the impact of sample size on entrywise estimation: having
N
independent observations improves the minimax rate by a factor of
1/\sqrt{N}
, reflecting the classical variance reduction from averaging.
However, this improvement is purely in terms of sample size and does not affect the dependence on the structural parameters
\mu
,
n
, or
\sigma_{\min}
.
In this sense, additional samples help only in a trivial way, and do not change the intrinsic difficulty of the problem.
Comparing with the upper bound in Theorem
19
, we see that when
\delta^{\star}_{\max}\asymp\delta^{\star}_{\min}
, our estimator
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}
achieves a near-optimal rate, up to constant factors.

6
Numerical Results

From our prior analysis, we see that recovering the node support
{I_{\star}}
of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
and estimating the common structure
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
are two nearly orthogonal tasks.
In the experiments below, we treat them separately, with Section
6.1
focusing on the recovery of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
and Section
6.2
on estimating
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.

6.1
Recovering the Node Support of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}

In this section, we conduct a series of experiments to evaluate the recovery of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
under various conditions.
In what follows, the symmetric noise matrix
\boldsymbol{W}
is generated with independent entries
(W_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}
, where the variances and distribution may vary across entries depending on the experimental setting.
Unless otherwise specified, we assume that
(W_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and set
W_{ji}=W_{ij}
for
i<j
to ensure symmetry.
Unless otherwise specified,
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
is generated with signal strength
\sigma_{B}=2n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
as follows:

Algorithm 3

Generate
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}

1:

: Matrix dimension
n
, node-support size
m
, signal strength parameter
\sigma_{B}
.

2:

:
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}

3:

Randomly select a subset
{I_{\star}}\subset[n]
with
|{I_{\star}}|=m
.

4:

Construct a matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
row-wise:

{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0,i,\cdot}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{0}_{n},&\text{if }i\notin{I_{\star}};\\
\text{i.i.d.~samples from }\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{B}^{2}),&\text{if }i\in{I_{\star}}.\end{cases}

5:

Set
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}\leftarrow{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}+{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}^{\top}
.

We generate a random low-rank singular subspace
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
as follows:

Algorithm 4

Generate
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}

1:

: Matrix dimension
n
, matrix rank
r
, incoherence parameter
\mu
.

2:

:
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}

3:

Set
m\leftarrow\lfloor n/\mu\rfloor
. Initialize
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\leftarrow\mathbf{0}_{n\times r}
.

4:

Denote the Stiefel manifold as

\operatorname{St}(n,p)=\left\{\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}:\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\right\}

Fill the top
m
rows of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
with a random orthonormal matrix drawn uniformly from
\operatorname{St}(m,r)
, i.e., according to the Haar measure on the Stiefel manifold.

5:

Fill the remaining rows with another random singular subspace in
\operatorname{St}(n-m,r)
.

6:

Normalize each column of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
to unit
\ell_{2}
norm.

We assume that the node support size
m=|{I_{\star}}|
is known a priori.
For the SDP method, the estimated support
\hat{I}
is obtained by solving the SDP in Equation (
72
) and selecting the
m
rows of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{\mathrm{SDP}}
with the largest
\ell_{1}
-norm.
For the group Lasso method,
\hat{I}
consists of the
m
nodes corresponding to the largest values of
\alpha_{i}
, as defined in Equation (
39
).
Our primary evaluation metric is the
false negative rate (FNR)
, defined as

\mathrm{FNR}:=1-\frac{|\hat{I}\cap{I_{\star}}|}{|{I_{\star}}|}.

All experiments are repeated over 30 independent trials per combination of parameters.
The shaded regions in the figures represent 95% bootstrap confidence intervals computed using the
Seaborn
package
(
Waskom, 2021
)
in Python.

Before presenting the numerical results, we briefly discuss the details of solving the SDP in Equation (
22
).
As noted in Section
4.5
, the original SDP suffers from scalability issues: naïve implementations are inefficient for large-scale problems due to the presence of
\Theta(n^{2})
constraints.
This renders the computation of the Schur complement, which is central to interior-point methods, prohibitively expensive in both time and space
(
Vandenberghe and Boyd, 1996
)
.

From a theoretical perspective, it is known that if an SDP has
q
affine constraints and a compact search space, then it admits a global optimum of rank at most
p_{0}
with
p_{0}(p_{0}+1)/2\leq q

(
Barvinok, 1995
;
Pataki, 1998
)
.
This results motivates a simplification of the original SDP by eliminating the inequality constraints and considering the relaxed problem

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{\mathrm{SDP}}=\underset{\boldsymbol{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}{\arg\min}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{C},\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle

(72)

\displaystyle\text{ s.t. }

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}\succeq 0,\;\langle\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle=K,\;\langle\mathbf{J},\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle=K^{2},

which reduces the number of constraints from
\Theta(n^{2})
to just
2
.
This simplification significantly improves computational efficiency and enables the use of off-the-shelf SDP solvers on moderately large problems.
In our experiments, we are able to handle problem sizes up to
n=7500
using commodity hardware.
Computations were carried out on the UW–Madison Center for High Throughput Computing (CHTC) infrastructure
(
Center for High Throughput Computing, 2006
)
.

Among the available techniques, one popular approach is the Burer-Monteiro factorization
(
Burer and Monteiro, 2003
)
, which reparametrizes the matrix variable as
\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}
, where
\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
is a matrix with
p\ll n
.
This yields a non-convex optimization problem over
\boldsymbol{X}
, but under mild regularity assumptions (which are satisfied by the SDP in Equation (
72
)), it has been shown that all second-order critical points of the reparametrized problem are globally optimal
(
Boumal et al., 2016
, Theorem 2)
.
The resulting non-convex problem can be solved using Riemannian optimization methods, which are efficient and scalable.
In our experiments, we solve Equation (
72
) using the commercial solver
MOSEK

(
MOSEK ApS, 2025
)
, which efficiently handles SDPs of this form.

6.1.1
Effect of Step
4
in Algorithm
1

We first examine the necessity of Step
4
in Algorithm
1
.
Suppose we observe
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}
following Equations (
2
) and (
3
), respectively.
We vary the incoherence parameter
\mu
according to

\mu\in\left\{\log n,\sqrt{n/\log n},\sqrt{n}\log n,n^{3/4}\right\}

and let
n
range from
500
to
7500
in increments of
500
.
We fix the rank at
r=3
and the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
are set to
\lambda^{\star}_{i}=3\sqrt{n}+(3-i)\log n
for
i=1,2,3
.
We estimate
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
using the spectral initializer
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
on
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
, and form the residual matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}=\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
.
We then apply both the SDP and group Lasso methods to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
, with
m=10
.

Figure
1
shows the results of this experiment.
Inspecting the figure, a clear phase transition can be seen to occur near
\mu=\sqrt{n}
.
When
\mu\ll\sqrt{n}
, both the SDP and group Lasso methods successfully recover the support
{I_{\star}}
, and they perform quite similarly, as indicated by the overlapping curves and markers.
On the other hand, when
\mu\gg\sqrt{n}
, the FNRs increase with
n
, indicating a breakdown in support recovery under high incoherence.
This observation is in accodance with Theorem
2
, which states that when
\mu\gg\sqrt{n}
, the rowwise estimation error for high-coherence rows exceeds
\Theta(n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n)
.
In this regime, the signal strength
\sigma_{B}=\Theta(\sigma n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n)
is insufficient for accurate recovery of
{I_{\star}}
.
These results highlight the importance of Step
4
, which removes high-coherence rows prior to applying the spectral initializer.

Figure 1
:
False negative rate of
{I_{\star}}
without Step
4
in Algorithm
1
, across different coherence levels
\mu
.
Each curve corresponds to a different rate of the incoherence parameter
\mu
, with solid lines for SDP and dashed lines for group Lasso.

6.1.2
Identifying the Recovery Threshold

We next investigate the effect of the signal strength constant
C
in the theoretical threshold
\sigma_{B}=Cn^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
.
We first vary
C\in\{0.8,1.2,1.6,2.0,2.4\}
and generate
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
using Algorithm
3
.
To assess recovery under heteroscedastic noise, we sample
W_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{ij}^{2})
with
\sigma_{ij}
uniformly drawn from
[\sigma_{\min},\sigma_{\max}]
.
The node support size is set to
m=10
.

Figure
2
shows the results of this experiment.
We observe a phase transition at
C\approx 1.6
:

1.

When
C\leq 1.2
, the FNR increases with
n
, indicating recovery failure.

2.

When
C\geq 2.0
, the FNR converges to zero.

3.

At the threshold
C=1.6
, the FNR stabilizes around
0.3
.

These observations align closely with the theoretical predictions in Remark
5
, which identifies the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) threshold

\operatorname{SNR}=\Omega(n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n)

as the minimum rate required for exact recovery of the node support
{I_{\star}}
.
Below this threshold, no estimator can succeed uniformly over the class of node-sparse signals.
Our empirical findings support this characterization: when
C
is too small, the effective SNR falls below the exact recovery threshold, and recovery fails.
Both SDP and group Lasso perform similarly in this setting, and are robust to heteroscedasticity when Assumption
2
is satisfied.

Recall from Remark
5
that the partial recovery threshold is given by

\operatorname{SNR}=\Omega\left(n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(\frac{n}{m}\right)\right).

When
m=10
, this threshold has the same asymptotic form as the exact recovery threshold, differing only in the constant factor.
The stablization of the FNR around
0.3
at
C=1.6
provides empirical support for this theoretical prediction, suggesting that
C=1.6
lies in the regime of partial recovery.

To investigate the partial recovery threshold in finer detail, we further vary the signal strength constant over a denser grid:
C\in\{1.3,1.4,1.5,1.6,1.7,1.8\}
.
For each combination of sample size
n
, signal strength
C
, and method, we repeat the experiment
100
times and compute the FNR for each trial.
The results, summarized in Figure
3
, show that as
n
increases, the FNR converges to a stable value for each fixed
C
.
Notably, stronger signals yield better limiting FNRs: for example, when
C=1.3
, the FNR levels off around
0.7
, while for
C=1.8
, it approaches
0.1
.
This trend aligns with the theoretical prediction based on the partial recovery threshold in Remark
5
.

Figure 2
:
False negative rate of
{I_{\star}}
for SDP and group Lasso under varying signal strength constant
C
in
\sigma_{B}=Cn^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
.
Each color corresponds to a different value of the scale parameter
C\in\{0.8,1.2,1.6,2.0,2.4\}
.
As
C
increases, both methods transition from failure to successful recovery.
A phase transition occurs near
C=1.6
: when
C\leq 1.2
, the FNR remains high and increases with
n
; when
C\geq 2.0
, the FNR decays toward zero.
Both SDP and group Lasso perform similarly in this heteroscedastic setting.

Figure 3
:
False negative rate of
{I_{\star}}
for SDP and group Lasso under varying signal strength constant
C
in
\sigma_{B}=Cn^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
.
Each color corresponds to a different value of the scale parameter
C\in\{1.3,1.4,1.5,1.6,1.7,1.8\}
.
As the matrix dimension
n
increases, all curves eventually stablize around some constant FNRs, with larger
C
leading to lower FNRs.
Both SDP and group Lasso perform similarly in this heteroscedastic setting.

6.1.3
Failure Case for Group Lasso

We now demonstrate a failure mode for group Lasso by constructing a signal matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
that satisfies the sparsity assumption but induces misleading group-level structure.
Our construction is similar to Equation (
41
) in Theorem
14
.

We set the number of signal nodes to
m=\lfloor 2\sqrt{n}\rfloor
and define an augmented support set
{I_{\star}}\cup J
of size
m+k
, where
k=\max\{5,\lfloor 0.2m\rfloor\}
.
We randomly select
m+k
nodes without replacement and let
{I_{\star}}
denote the first
m
indices (true signal nodes) and
J
the remaining
k
indices (perturbation nodes).
The signal matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
is constructed as follows:

1.

For each
(i,j)\in{I_{\star}}\times J
(and symmetrically
(j,i)
), set
B^{\star}_{ij}=\beta_{1}
, where
\beta_{1}=\frac{2.5(n\log n)^{1/4}}{\sqrt{m}}
.

2.

For each
i\in{I_{\star}}
and
j\notin{I_{\star}}\cup J
, set
B^{\star}_{ij}=\beta_{2}
, where
\beta_{2}=2n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
.

3.

All other entries of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
are set to zero.

This construction creates two types of signal entries in
{\boldsymbol{B}}
:
a few relatively large entries, corresponding to entries indexed by an element of
{I_{\star}}
and an element of
J
, and many weaker entries corresponding to entries indexed by an index in
{I_{\star}}
and the rest of the nodes.
Under this setting, although nodes in
{I_{\star}}
have signal strength above the exact recovery threshold given in Remark
5
, the group Lasso struggles to separate
J
and
{I_{\star}}
due to its greedy selection mechanism.

We apply both SDP and group Lasso to the observed matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}
to recover the support
{I_{\star}}
.
As shown in Table
1
, the SDP method consistently achieves exact support recovery across all sample sizes considered, since the signal in
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
is well above the exact recovery threshold.
In contrast, group Lasso exhibits persistent recovery failure, with its false negative rate stabilizing around
0.2
.
This value aligns with the fraction
k/(m+k)
of perturbation nodes, suggesting that group Lasso tends to select the larger-magnitude but spurious connections to
J
rather than the true support.
These empirical observations corroborate the theoretical limitations of group Lasso established in Theorem
14
.

:
False negative rate (FNR) in recovering
{I_{\star}}
of SDP and group Lasso across various sample sizes.
Each value is reported as the mean (standard deviation) over
50
repeated trials.

)

6.1.4
Multiple Copies versus Single Copy in SDP

In Section
4.1
, we established that the SDP method requires Assumption
2
to recover the node support
{I_{\star}}
of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
when there is only one observed copy of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
.
We demonstrate that access to multiple copies allows us to relax Assumption
2
as shown in Theorem
11
.
Set the node support size
m=\lceil 2\log n\rceil
and generate
\boldsymbol{Y}_{1}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{2}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}_{2}
, where noise is constructed as follows:

1.

For each row
i
, sample
\sigma_{i}\sim\operatorname{Unif}[\sigma_{\min},\sigma_{\max}]
, with
\sigma_{\min}=0.8
and
\sigma_{\max}=1.3
.

2.

Set the
i
-th row of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
to
\sigma_{i}^{2}
and define
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{\top}
.

3.

Generate a symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{W}_{0}
as before and let
\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{W}_{0}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.

This setup yields symmetric but row-heteroscedastic noise.
We apply two versions of SDP, where for the multiple-copy SDP, the cost matrix is
\boldsymbol{Y}_{1}\circ\boldsymbol{Y}_{2}
, and for the single-copy SDP, the cost matrix is
\boldsymbol{Y}_{\mathrm{avg}}\circ\boldsymbol{Y}_{\mathrm{avg}}
, where
\boldsymbol{Y}_{\mathrm{avg}}=(\boldsymbol{Y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{Y}_{2})/2
.

The results in Figure
4
demonstrate that the single-copy SDP fails under row-heteroscedastic noise, with FNR increasing as
n
grows.
This aligns with the discussion at the beginning of Section
4.1
, confirming that support recovery is ill-posed in the presence of row-wise heteroscedasticity.
By comparison, the multiple-copy SDP accurately recovers
{I_{\star}}
without Assumption
2
, consistent with Theorem
11
.

Figure 4
:
False negative rate for recovering
{I_{\star}}
as a function of sample size
n
(log scale), comparing two SDP-based methods: single-copy SDP (labeled “Single”) and multiple-copy SDP (labeled “Multiple”).

6.1.5
Truncated SDP for Heavy-Tailed Noise

Finally, we consider a heavy-tailed noise setting where the entries of the noise matrix are drawn i.i.d. according to a
t_{4}
distribution rescaled to have unit variance.
The noise matrix
\boldsymbol{W}
and signal matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
are otherwise generated as inthe previous experiments as described in Section
6.1
.
We set the node support size
|{I_{\star}}|
to
m=\lceil 2\log n\rceil
and compare the performance of the vanilla SDP estimator with the truncated version that clips extreme entries, as described in Section
4.2.2
.
As shown in Figure
5
, the truncated SDP reliably recovers
{I_{\star}}
, in agreement with the theoretical guarantees established in Theorem
10
.
Specifically, truncated SDP achieves exact support recovery under heavy-tailed noise when the signal-to-noise ratio exceeds the exact recovery threshold
\Theta(n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n)
defined in Remark
5
.
In contrast, the vanilla SDP fails under this setting, with its FNR diverging to 1 as
n
increases.
This behavior also aligns with Theorem
7
, which indicates that when the noise magnitude
L
significantly exceeds its standard deviation
\sigma
, the vanilla SDP requires a substantially higher SNR to achieve support recovery.

Figure 5
:
False negative rate of recovering
{I_{\star}}
under scaled
t_{4}
-distributed noise as a function of sample size
n
(log scale).
The plot compares two methods: vanilla SDP (blue) and truncated SDP (orange).

6.2
Improved Estimation of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}

We now empirically validate our findings from Section
5
.
Throughout this section, we assume access to four observations
\boldsymbol{Y}_{11},\boldsymbol{Y}_{12},\boldsymbol{Y}_{21},\boldsymbol{Y}_{22}
generated according to a low-rank-plus-noise model with fixed rank
r=3
.
The noise matrices are generated as described in Section
6.1
.
To construct the estimators, we asymmetrically arrange
\boldsymbol{Y}_{i1}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{i2}
to obtain
\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

6.2.1
Varying
\mu
and
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}

We vary
n
over a range of values from
700
to
40000
.
The incoherence parameter
\mu
is set to either
n^{4/5}
or
n^{5/6}
, and the minimum eigenvalue of the low rank signal matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
is chosen as
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\in\{2.05,3\}\sqrt{n}
.
We consider three approaches to estimating
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
and consequently
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
:

1.

Estimate
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
using the bias-corrected eigenvectors
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}
from Equation (
63
) applied to the average matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}_{\mathrm{asym}}
of two assymetrically arranged matrices
\boldsymbol{Y}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{2}
.
Form the estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{1}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}
as in Corollary
4
, where
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
are the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{\mathrm{asym}}
.

2.

Averaging all four matrices
\boldsymbol{Y}_{ij}
for
i,j\in[2]
and compute the top-
r
eigenspace
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
and eigenvalues
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{spec}}
from the spectral decomposition of the average.
Then form
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\mathrm{spec}}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\mathrm{spec}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{spec}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\mathrm{spec}}^{\top}
as the spectral estimator of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.

3.

Use the refined eigenspace estimate
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
from Theorem
18
, and construct
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{2}:=\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}
as in Theorem
19
, where
\boldsymbol{U}
consists of the right-eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{1}
, and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
is the bias-correction factor computed from
\boldsymbol{Y}_{21}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{22}
following Equations (
65
) to (
68
).

The subspace recovery and matrix entrywise recovery results are summarized in Figures
6
and
7
, respectively.
We highlight the following observations:

1.

Estimation of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
:

The error rate of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}
is the worse among all methods, since its error is dominated by the term
\sqrt{\mu/n}/\delta^{\star}_{\min}
as given in Corollary
3
, which is significantly larger compared to that of
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
in Theorem
18
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
in Theorem
21
.
When
\mu
is small,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
achieves the lowest error for smaller matrix dimension
n
.
As
n
increases, the behavior of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
exhibits a clear transition: the error initially decreases with
n
, then flattens, and eventually become worse than that of
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
.
This transition point depends on both
\mu
and
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
.
For instance, when
\mu=n^{4/5}
and
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}=3\sqrt{n}
, the turning point occurs around
n=8000
, beyond which the error of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
levels off.
As
\mu
increases, the transition shifts to smaller
n
, while increasing
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
delays the transition to larger
n
.
According to Theorem
21
, this suggests that for small
n
, the term
\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}/|\lambda^{\star}_{\min}|
initially dominates the estimation error of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
, while the asymptotic term
\sigma\sqrt{\mu r}/|\lambda^{\star}_{\min}|
becomes dominant only for sufficiently large
n
.

2.

Estimation of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
:

We observe a similar trend in the estimation of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
The estimation error of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{1}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}
increases as
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
increases, consistent with Corollary
4
.
Among all methods, the spectral estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
performs best when
\mu
is small and
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
is large, particularly for small to moderate
n
.
As
n
increases, the error of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
increases, while the error of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{2}
remains nearly flat or slightly decreases.
Eventually, for large
n
, the error of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
surpasses that of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{2}
, in line with Theorem
19
, which shows that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{2}
achieves a better error rate than
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
for sufficiently large
n
.

Remark 14
.

The estimators
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{2}
are likely suboptimal in terms of the leading constant in their error bounds.
This inefficiency arises because they do not fully exploit the information available from all four observations: two copies are primarily used for estimation, while the remaining two are incorporated only as a correction term.
It might be possible to improve the efficiency by combining the spectral estimator with
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{2}
, which we leave for the future work.

Figure 6
:
Estimation error of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
under the
\ell_{2,\infty}
norm across varying coherence
\mu
and signal strength
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
.
Each subplot corresponds to a specific pair of
(\mu,\lambda^{\star}_{\min})
, with
\mu\in\{n^{4/5},n^{5/6}\}
(columns, left to right) and
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\in\{2.05\sqrt{n},3\sqrt{n}\}
(rows, top to bottom).
The estimators compared are
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
(blue),
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}
(orange), and the spectral estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
(green).
The
y
-axis shows the
\ell_{2,\infty}
error on a log scale.

:
Estimation error of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
under the
\ell_{\infty}
norm across various regimes of coherence
\mu
and signal strength
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
.
Each subplot corresponds to a different combination of
(\mu,\lambda^{\star}_{\min})
, where
\mu\in\{n^{4/5},n^{5/6}\}
(columns, left to right) and
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\in\{2.05\sqrt{n},3\sqrt{n}\}
(rows, top to bottom).
Three estimators are compared:
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}

\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\mathrm{spec}}
(green).

6.2.2
Varying Eigengap Ratio

We conclude by examining the effect of the ratio
\rho:=\delta^{\star}_{\max}/\delta^{\star}_{\min}
on the estimation error rates of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
when using the estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{2}
.
We fix
\mu=\sqrt{n}\log n
and
\delta^{\star}_{\min}=\log n
in the experiments, and vary
\delta^{\star}_{\max}\in\{\log n,n^{1/3},n^{1/2}\}
.
The result is shown in Figure
8
.
Different values of
\rho
yield overlapping error curves, indicating that changes in
\rho
have negligible impact on the estimation error rates.
This empirical finding suggests that the dependence on
\delta^{\star}_{\max}/\delta^{\star}_{\min}
displayed by the upper bound in Theorem
19
is likely a technical artifact rather than a fundamental limitation.
We leave the pursuit of this improvement to future work.

Figure 8
:
Estimation errors of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}_{2}:=\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}
as a function of
n
under the
\ell_{\infty}
norm.
Each curve corresponds to a different scaling rate for the ratio
\rho:=\delta^{\star}_{\max}/\delta^{\star}_{\min}
.
Each point represents the average error over 30 independent trials.

7
Discussion

In this paper, we proposed a new statistical framework for analyzing multiple weighted networks that share a low-rank structure but differ through sparse, node-specific perturbations. Our model captures a broad class of practical scenarios, extending beyond traditional multilayer network formulations that primarily address global or smooth variations across conditions.

We introduced a suite of estimation procedures that adapt to the number of observations available per group. Our approach combines spectral initialization, semidefinite programming (SDP) for support recovery, and bias-corrected refinement for improved low-rank estimation. Theoretical results establish minimax optimality under various signal and sparsity regimes, and highlight the interplay between observation multiplicity and statistical efficiency.

We further examined the limitations of commonly used alternatives such as Group Lasso, which fails under certain structured perturbation settings. In contrast, our SDP-based methods exhibit robust support recovery even under heteroscedastic and heavy-tailed noise. Comprehensive numerical experiments corroborate the theory and demonstrate key behaviors, including phase transitions, the role of coherence, and the benefits of bias-correction and multiple network observations.

Several directions for future research also emerge from our findings:

Node-sparse structure estimation.

First, a deeper understanding of the computational barriers for node-sparse recovery is needed. It remains open whether the limitations of Group Lasso observed here also apply in broader settings, such as covariance estimation.
Second, while our SDP formulation is based on a least-squares loss, it is not robust to outliers. Incorporating robust loss functions (e.g., Huber or quantile-based objectives) and designing tractable algorithms under such losses would be interesting.
Third, our current framework assumes continuous weights. Extending support recovery guarantees to discrete-weight networks such as binary or count-valued adjacency matrices poses both theoretical and algorithmic challenges.

Low-rank structure estimation.

The low-rank model for the control group may be oversimplified for real-world data. In many applications, the assumption of a clean low-rank plus noise decomposition may not hold.
While our bias-corrected estimator achieves a near-optimal rate under the
\ell_{\infty}
norm, it only outperforms baseline methods when the matrix dimension is sufficiently large. Improving the efficiency of this estimator, especially in terms of its leading constant, would enhance its practical utility in moderate-sized problems.
Moreover, the fundamental limits of low-rank matrix recovery under
\ell_{\infty}
and
\ell_{2,\infty}
norms with only a single observation remain unresolved. In particular, it is unclear whether a coherence-independent estimator exists for subspace recovery in this setting. Developing such an estimator, if it exists, may require substantially more sophisticated techniques.

Our results provide a foundation for future work on structure recovery in high-dimensional network models, and contribute to a broader understanding of statistical estimation under sparsity and low-rank constraints.

Proof.

To analyze the LSE, the high level idea is to make use of the optimality condition of the LSE that the
\hat{I}
is a solution to the optimization problem in Equation (

).
Combining with standard concentration inequalities yields the exact and partial recovery results for the LSE.
Below, we first prove a few concentration inequalities.

The overlap pattern of

and

when

Without loss of generality, we assume that
{I_{\star}}=[m]
.
Let
I\subset[n]
be any set of size
|I|=m
and
|I\cap{I_{\star}}|=m-k
.
A demonstration of the overlapping pattern of the rows and columns indexed by

and

is shown in Figure

.
Let

\mathcal{S}_{I}:=\left\{(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]:i<j,i\in I\right\}

and, for each

, define

N_{k}=k(n-m)+k/2-k^{2}/2,

(73)

which is precisely the cardinality of the set
\mathcal{S}_{I}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}
when
|I\cap{I_{\star}}|=m-k
.
Under the assumption that
n\geq 3m
, we have that
N_{k}=\Omega(kn)
for all
k\in[m]
.

We first fix a
k\in[m]
.
With index set

fixed, we proceed to bound the sum of squares for
W_{ij}
in the purple shaded region shown in Figure

, which has
N_{k}
entries in total.
It follows from Lemma

that for
