,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{I}-\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}}(W_{ij}^{2}-\mathbb{E}W_{ij}^{2})\right|\geq t_{k}\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{t_{k}^{2}}{N_{k}L^{2}\sigma^{2}},\frac{t_{k}}{L^{2}}\right\}\right).

Since there are
\binom{n-m}{k}
choices for
I\cap{{I_{\star}}}^{c}
, an application of the union bound yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset[n]\\
I\cap[m]^{c}=k\end{subarray}}\left|\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{I}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}}\!\!\!\!(W_{ij}^{2}-\mathbb{E}W_{ij}^{2})\right|\geq t_{k}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\binom{n-m}{k}\exp\!\left(-\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{t_{k}^{2}}{N_{k}L^{2}\sigma^{2}},\frac{t_{k}}{L^{2}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{en}{k}\right)^{k}\exp\!\left(-\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{t_{k}^{2}}{N_{k}L^{2}\sigma^{2}},\frac{t_{k}}{L^{2}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\!\left(2k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\min\!\left\{\frac{t_{k}^{2}}{N_{k}L^{2}\sigma^{2}},\frac{t_{k}}{L^{2}}\right\}\right),

where the last inequality holds from the fact that
k\leq m
and our assumption that
n>3m
.
Setting

t_{k}=C\max\left\{L\sigma k\sqrt{n\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)},L^{2}k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)\right\},

for a suitably large constant
C>0
, it holds for for any
k\in[m]
that

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset[n]\\
I\cap[m]^{c}=k\end{subarray}}\left|\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{I}-\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}}(W_{ij}^{2}-\mathbb{E}W_{ij}^{2})\right|\geq C\max\left\{L\sigma k\sqrt{n\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)},L^{2}k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)\right\}

(74)

holds with probability at most
O(\left(k/n\right)^{7k})
, where we have used Equation (
73
) to bound the probability that Equation (
74
) fails to hold.

Observing that

\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{k}{n}\right)^{7k}=O(n^{-7}),

a second union bound over all
k\in[m]
yields that for all suitably large
n
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-7})
, it holds for all
k\in[m]
that

\displaystyle\max_{I\subset[n],I\cap[m]^{c}=k}\left|\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{I}-\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}}(W_{ij}^{2}-\mathbb{E}W_{ij}^{2})\right|\leq C\max\left\{L\sigma k\sqrt{n\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)},L^{2}k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)\right\}.

(75)

By the same argument, this time applied to the sum of squares of
W_{ij}
in the orange shaded region in Figure
9
, it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-7})
that for all
k\in[m]
,

\displaystyle\max_{I\subset[n],I\cap[m]^{c}=k}

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}(W_{ij}^{2}-\mathbb{E}W_{ij}^{2})\right|\leq C\max\left\{L\sigma k\sqrt{n\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)},L^{2}k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)\right\}.

(76)

We now bound the sum of
W_{ij}B^{\star}_{ij}
terms corresponding to entries in the orange shaded region shown in Figure
9
.
Under the condition in Equation (
15
), we have

\frac{\max_{i,j\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}|B^{\star}_{ij}|}{\left(\sum_{i,j\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}B^{\star 2}_{ij}\right)^{1/2}}=O\left(k^{-1/2}\log^{-1/2}\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)\right).

(77)

Applying standard sub-exponential tail bounds
(
Vershynin, 2018
)
, it holds for any
I\subset[n]
with
|I\cap{I_{\star}}|=k\in[m]
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}\!\!B^{\star}_{ij}W_{ij}\right|\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{\sigma^{2}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}B^{\star 2}_{ij}},\frac{t}{L\max_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}|B^{\star}_{ij}|}\right\}\right).

Taking

t=C\max\left\{\sigma\left(\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}B^{\star 2}_{ij}\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)},L\max_{i,j\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}|B^{\star}_{ij}|\cdot k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)\right\}

and applying a union bound over
k\in[m]
and
I\subset[n]
such that
|I\cap{I_{\star}}|=k\in[m]
, it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-7})
, for all
k\in[m]
and
I\subseteq[n]
with

that,

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}B^{\star}_{ij}W_{ij}\right|

(78)

\displaystyle\leq C\max\left\{\sigma\left(\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}B^{\star 2}_{ij}\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)},L\max_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}|B^{\star}_{ij}|\cdot k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C\max\left\{\sigma\left(\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{{I_{\star}}}-\mathcal{S}_{I}}B^{\star 2}_{ij}\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)},o\left(L\sigma k\sqrt{n\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)}\right)\right\}

where the last inequality follows from the condition in Equation (
13
).

For a matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}
and a set
I\subseteq[n]
, we define the matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{I}=(B_{I,ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
according to

B_{I,ij}=\begin{cases}B_{ij}&\mbox{ if }(i,j)\in I\times I,\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(79)

For a matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
, we let
\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{A})
denote the masked matrix whose upper triangle is filled with entries of
\boldsymbol{A}
and the lower triangle is all zero.
To establish exact recovery for the LSE
\hat{I}
, we start with the basic inequality.
By the optimality of LSE, we have

\|\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{Y}_{\hat{I}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}=\|\mathcal{M}({\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\hat{I}^{c}}+\boldsymbol{W}_{\hat{I}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\|\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{Y}_{I_{\star}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}=\|\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{W}_{I_{\star}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}.

Expanding the squared Frobenius norm and rearranging terms, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}({\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\hat{I}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+2\langle\mathcal{M}({\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\hat{I}^{c}}),\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{W}_{\hat{I}^{c}})\rangle\leq\|\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{W}_{I_{\star}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}-\|\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{W}_{\hat{I}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}.

(80)

We will now show that in order for Equation (
80
) to hold, we must have
|\hat{I}\cap{I_{\star}}|=m
with high probability under the condition given in Equation (
14
).
If for some
k\in[m]
,

|\hat{I}\cap{I_{\star}}|=m-k
, combining Equations (
75
)-(
78
) into Equation (
80
) yields that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-7})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}({\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\hat{I}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\|\mathcal{M}({\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\hat{I}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}\sqrt{k\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)}+CL\sigma k\sqrt{n\log\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)}

where the second inequality follows from Equation (
73
). Taking square roots, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}({\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\hat{I}^{c}})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq C(L\sigma k)^{1/2}n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(\frac{n}{k}\right).

Noting that by the condition in Equation (
14
), we have

\|\mathcal{M}({\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\hat{I}^{c}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}\geq\sqrt{k}b

where
b
is defined in Equation (
12
), we have

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\leq C(L\sigma)^{1/2}n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)

Hence, if for some
k_{0}>0
and a sufficiently large constant
c_{0}>0
,

b\geq c_{0}(L\sigma)^{1/2}n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}\left(\frac{n}{k_{0}}\right)

the inequality in Equation (
80
)
cannot hold for any
k\geq k_{0}
with probability at least
1-O(n^{-7})
and therefore, we must have
|\hat{I}\cap{I_{\star}}|\geq m-k_{0}
.
Taking
k_{0}=1
yields exact recovery.

∎

Appendix B
Proof of Minimax Results for Support Recovery

In this section, we provide a proof of Theorem
5
, along with the proof of Corollaries
1
and
6
.
Several supporting technical lemmas are deferred to Section
B.4
.

B.1
Proof of Theorem
5

Proof.

We start with an overview of the proof strategy.
Since the minimax risk is lower-bounded by the Bayes risk under any proper prior over
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)
defined in Equation (
17
),
it suffices to construct a prior for which the lower bound in Equation (
18
) holds.
For details on minimax hypothesis testing, we refer the reader to
Ingster and Suslina, 2003
.
Our argument proceeds in four steps:

•

Step 1.
We construct a hierarchical prior over the parameter set
\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)
, and then replace it with a more tractable product prior.
The Bayes risk under this product prior serves as a lower bound of the minimax risk, and is easier to analyze.

•

Step 2.
We reduce the Bayes risk to sum of expected errors in a sequence of
n
binary hypothesis testing problems, one for each node, allowing a tractable lower bound on the Bayes risk.

•

Step 3.
We derive lower bound on the expected testing risk for each hypothesis by analyzing a Gaussian mixture testing problem, where the risk is defined as a weighted combination of the Type I and Type II errors.

•

Step 4.
Finally, we aggregate the binary hypothesis testing risks to obtain the desired lower bound on the overall Bayes risk.

Step 1: Constructing a prior.

To define a probability measure over
\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)
, we proceed in two steps.
First, we sample the binary support indicator vector
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
from a prior
\pi_{\Theta}
, defined in Lemma
4
, with parameter
m^{\prime}\in(0,m]
. Conditioned on
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, for each
i\in[n]
:

•

If with
\eta_{i}=1
, we sample the vector of entries
\{B_{ii}\}\cup\{B_{ij}|j:\eta_{j}=0\}
uniformly from the sphere
b\mathbb{S}^{n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}}
.
For
B_{ij}
where
i\neq j
and
\eta_{i}=\eta_{j}=1
, we set
B_{ij}
to be a small fixed nonzero value, for example,
B_{ij}=\sigma/n
.

•

If
\eta_{i}=0
, for
j\in[n]
and
\eta_{j}=0
, we set
B_{ij}=0
.

By definition of
\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)
, the prior must exclude the set

\mathcal{E}:=\left\{{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\operatorname{Sym}(n),|I_{\boldsymbol{B}}|\leq m:\|{\boldsymbol{B}}_{i,\cdot}\|_{0}\leq m\text{ for all }i\in I_{\boldsymbol{B}}\right\},

which includes matrices that violate Assumption
3
, thereby making the node support
I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}
potentially non-identifiable.
However, since the event
\mathcal{E}^{c}
occurs almost surely under our construction (since
\|{\boldsymbol{B}}_{i,\cdot}\|_{0}>m
occurs almost surely), including it does not affect the resulting Bayes risk.
Thus, for notational and technical simplicity, we proceed with the sampling distribution defined above.
Under
\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)\setminus\mathcal{E}
, the support
I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}
corresponds to the indices
i
such that
\eta_{i}=1
.

Although the prior
\pi_{\Theta}
defines a valid probability distribution over
\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)
,
computing the Bayes risk under this prior is challenging.
Fortunately, we can construct a simpler distribution that yields a lower bound on the Bayes risk under
\pi_{\Theta}
that is easier to analyze.
Let
\pi
denote the product measure of
n
independent Bernoulli distributions with shared parameter
m^{\prime}/n
.
By Equation (
92
) in Lemma
4
, we have

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\Theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}-4m^{\prime}\exp\left(-\frac{\left(m-m^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{2m}\right),

(81)

which implies that it suffices to analyze the Bayes risk under the simpler product prior
\pi
.

Accordingly, we revise our sampling procedure as follows.
First, sample
\boldsymbol{\eta}
from
\pi
.
Then, conditioned on
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, draw
\{B_{ii}\}\cup\{B_{ij}|j:\eta_{j}=0\}
from the uniform distribution over
b\mathbb{S}^{n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}}
.
For
i,j\in[n]
distinct such that
\eta_{i}=\eta_{j}=1
, set
B_{ij}
to be a small fixed nonzero value such as
\sigma/n
.
In this way, we are guaranteed that
I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}
as well as
\boldsymbol{\eta}
are almost surely identifiable.

Step 2: Bayes risk reduces to testing
n
hypotheses.

To analyze the information available for estimating
\eta_{i}
, define the following subsets of observed entries:

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}=\{\widetilde{Y}_{jk}:j\neq i,k\neq i\}\cup\{\widetilde{Y}_{ik}:\eta_{k}=1,k\neq i\},\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i}=\{\widetilde{Y}_{ik}:\eta_{k}=0\}\cup\{\widetilde{Y}_{ii}\}.

Denote

g(\boldsymbol{\eta},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}):=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left|\hat{\eta}_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}})-\eta_{i}\right|\right|\boldsymbol{\eta},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}\right].

(82)

By definition of the Hamming distance, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\inf_{\hat{\eta}_{i}\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\left|\hat{\eta}_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}})-\eta_{i}\right|

(83)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\inf_{\hat{\eta}_{i}\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\left.\left|\hat{\eta}_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}})-\eta_{i}\right|\right|\boldsymbol{\eta},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}\right]\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\inf_{\hat{\eta}_{i}\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.g(\boldsymbol{\eta},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i})\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}\right].

Recall that under the product measure
\pi
, the indicator
\eta_{i}
is independent of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}
,
and follows a Bernoulli distribution with parameter
m^{\prime}/n
, denoted
\pi_{0}
.
We can rewrite Equation (
83
) as

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\inf_{\hat{\eta}_{i}\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}^{n-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left.g(\boldsymbol{\eta},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i})\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}\right]\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\inf_{\hat{\eta}_{i}\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}^{n-1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left[\left.g(\boldsymbol{\eta},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i})\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}\right]\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}\right].

Interchanging the order of infimum and
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}^{n-1}}\mathbb{E}
in the above display and using the definition of
g
in Equation (
82
) yields that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}

(84)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}^{n-1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\inf_{\hat{\eta}_{i}\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left|\hat{\eta}_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}})-\eta_{i}\right||\boldsymbol{\eta},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}\right]\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}\right\}\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}\right].

Now, since
\eta_{i}
is independent of
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}
,
and since the only part of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
that depends on
\eta_{i}
is
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i}
,
the inner expectation of Equation (
84
) reduces to

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\eta}_{i}\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left[\left.\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\hat{\eta}_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i})-\eta_{i}\right||\boldsymbol{\eta},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}\right]\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}\right]=

\displaystyle\inf_{T_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i})\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left|T_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i})-\eta_{i}\right||\boldsymbol{\eta}\right]|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}\right],

(85)

where the infimum on the right hand side is taken over all test functions
T_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i})\in[0,1]
that is a measurable function of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i}
.
Taking Equation (
85
) into Equation (
84
), we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}^{n-1}}\left[\inf_{T_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i})\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left|T_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i})-\eta_{i}\right||\boldsymbol{\eta}\right]|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}\right]\right].

(86)

Step 3: Lower bound the expected test errors.

Recall the sampling scheme for
\boldsymbol{\eta}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}
described in Step 1.
For a given
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}
, the right hand side of Equation (
85
) corresponds to a hypothesis testing problem under a Gaussian mixture model.
Let

k=n-\sum_{j\neq i}\eta_{j}.

(87)

We consider a binary random variable
\eta\in\{0,1\}
drawn from the prior distribution
\pi_{0}
, and define a random vector
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
according to the following mixture model:

\boldsymbol{y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{w}&\mbox{ if }\eta=0,\\
\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{w}&\mbox{ if }\eta=1,\end{cases}

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})
and
\boldsymbol{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{k})
are independent.
By construction,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i}
has the same distribution as
\boldsymbol{y}
and we have

\inf_{T_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i})\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[\mathbb{E}\left[\left|T_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i})-\eta_{i}\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}\right]|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}]=\inf_{T(\boldsymbol{y})\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\left[\left|T(\boldsymbol{y})-\eta\right|\right],

where the infimum on the right hand side is taken over all test functions
T(\boldsymbol{y})\in[0,1]
that is a measurable function of
\boldsymbol{y}
.
We also use the fact that
\eta
is independent of
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}
under the product measure
\pi
to obtain the above equality.

Combining the above display with Lemma
7
, when we choose a sufficiently small
b
, it follows that

\displaystyle\inf_{T_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i})\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[\mathbb{E}\left[\left|T_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i})-\eta_{i}\right|\boldsymbol{\eta}\right]|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}]

\displaystyle=\inf_{T(\boldsymbol{y})\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\left[\left|T(\boldsymbol{y})-\eta\right|\right]

(88)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m^{\prime}}{n}-\frac{m^{\prime 2}}{4n^{2}}\exp\left(\frac{b^{4}C}{k\sigma^{4}}\right)-\frac{3m^{\prime 2}}{4n^{2}},

where
C>0
is a universal constant.
As a reminder,
k
in Equation (
88
) is a random quantity defined in Equation (
87
).

Step 4: Combining the results.

Combining Equations (
86
) and (
88
),

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq\max_{m^{\prime}\in(0,m]}\left[m^{\prime}-\frac{3m^{\prime 2}}{4n}-\frac{m^{\prime 2}}{4n}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{b^{4}C}{k\sigma^{4}}\right)\right]\right].

Defining
a=Cb^{4}/\sigma^{4}
and applying Lemma
9
, we have for sufficiently large
n
and
3m<n
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{m^{\prime}\in(0,m]}\frac{m^{\prime}}{4}\left[3-\frac{m^{\prime}}{n}\left(\exp\left\{a-Cn\log\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right\}+\exp\left\{\frac{a}{\log n}-Cn\right\}+e^{\frac{Ca}{n}}\right)\right].

Recalling the definition of
f_{n}(a,m^{\prime})
from the statement of Theorem
5
, it follows that

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq\max_{m^{\prime}\in(0,m]}\left(\frac{m^{\prime}}{4}f_{n}(a,m^{\prime})\right).

(89)

Thus, combining Equations (
81
)-(
89
), we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\Theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{T\in[0,1]}\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}-4m^{\prime}\exp\left\{-\frac{\left(m-m^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{2m}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{m^{\prime}\in(0,m]}\left[\frac{m^{\prime}}{4}f_{n}(a,m^{\prime})-4m^{\prime}\exp\left\{-\frac{\left(m-m^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{2m}\right\}\right]

which completes the proof.
∎

B.2
Proof of Corollary
1

Proof.

To show that exact recovery of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is infeasible, it suffices to provide a nontrivial lower bound of the Hamming risk

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}.

Let
a=Cb^{4}/\sigma^{4}
.
For any
m^{\prime}\in[1,m]
, when

a<Cn\log\left(\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right),

we have

\max\left\{a-Cn\log\left(\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right),~\frac{a}{\log n}-Cn,~\frac{Ca}{n}\right\}<\log\left(\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right).

Taking exponentials on both sides and applying Theorem
5
, the following inequality holds:

3-\frac{m^{\prime}}{n}\left(\exp\left\{a-Cn\log\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right\}+\exp\left\{\frac{a}{\log n}-Cn\right\}+\exp\left\{\frac{Ca}{n}\right\}\right)>2.

(90)

Combining this with Equation (
89
), we have that

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}>\frac{m^{\prime}}{2}.

Plugging the above bound into Equation (
81
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\Theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}-4m^{\prime}\exp\left(-\frac{\left(m-m^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{2m}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m^{\prime}}{2}-4m^{\prime}\exp\left(-\frac{\left(m-m^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{2m}\right)

as claimed.
∎

B.3
Proof of Theorem
6

Proof.

Following the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
5
, we can construct a prior over
\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)
and then replace it with a more tractable product prior via Lemma
4
.
We use the same priors for
\boldsymbol{\eta}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}
in the proof of Theorem
5
, but we must consider different observed data.
Thus, as counterparts to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\sim i}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}_{i}
in the proof of Theorem
5
, consider

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{(1:N)}_{\sim i}=\bigcup_{\ell\in[N]}\left\{\widetilde{Y}_{\ell,jk},j\neq i,k\neq i\right\}\cup\left\{\widetilde{Y}_{\ell,ik},:\eta_{k}=1,k\neq i\right\}

and

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1:N)}_{i}=\bigcup_{\ell\in[N]}\left\{\widetilde{Y}_{\ell,ik}:\eta_{k}=0\right\}\cup\left\{\widetilde{Y}_{\ell,ii}\right\},

respectively.
Following the same argument as Step 2 in the proof of Theorem
5
, we can show that

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\inf_{\hat{\eta}_{i}\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\left[\left|\hat{\eta}_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{1},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{2},\cdots,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{N})-\eta_{i}\right|\mid\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{(1:N)}_{\sim i}\right].

By arguments similar to those yielding Equations (
84
) and (
85
), we can once again reduce the Bayes risk to the sum of expected errors in a sequence of
n
binary hypothesis testing problems, one for each node.
To specify the hypotheses, for any
i\in[n]
, given
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sim i}
, we let
k=n-\sum_{j\neq i}\eta_{j}
and

\boldsymbol{y}^{(\ell)}=\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{w}^{(\ell)},\quad\ell\in[N],

where
\boldsymbol{w}^{(\ell)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{k})
for all
\ell\in[N]
.
Under the null hypothesis, we have
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{0}
, while under the alternative hypothesis,
\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})
.
Then the binary hypothesis testing risk associated with the
i
-th node is

\inf_{T\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\left[\left|T(\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{y}^{(2)},\dots,\boldsymbol{y}^{(N)})-\eta\right|\right],

where
\eta\in\{0,1\}
is a binary selector drawn from a Bernoulli distribution with parameter
m^{\prime}/n
, denoted as
\pi_{0}
.

As seen in the proof of Lemma
7
, the testing risk above can be bounded by the expectation of the likelihood ratio under the null hypothesis.
This likelihood ratio is given by

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{y}^{(2)},\cdots,\boldsymbol{y}^{(N)}\right)}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}f_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{y}^{(\ell)}\right)}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{y}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|^{2}_{2}\right\}}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{y}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right\}}

(91)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\exp\left\{-\frac{N}{2\sigma^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{y}^{(\ell)\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\}.

Setting
\bar{\boldsymbol{y}}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{y}^{(\ell)}
, we have

L=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\exp\left\{-\frac{N}{2\sigma^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{N}{\sigma^{2}}\bar{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\}.

Thus, replacing
\sigma^{2}
with
\sigma^{2}/N
and
\boldsymbol{y}
with
\bar{\boldsymbol{y}}
in the proof of Lemma
5
, we obtain that

\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}\leq\exp\left\{\frac{Cb^{4}}{k(\sigma/\sqrt{N})^{4}}\right\}.

The remainder of the proof directly follows the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
5
.
∎

B.4
Technical Lemmas

Lemma 4
.

Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\ell}={\boldsymbol{B}}+\boldsymbol{W}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[N]
,
where
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}(b,n,m)
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{\ell}
are symmetric matrices with
W_{\ell,ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
for all
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
and
\ell\in[N]
.
Define the set of sparse binary vectors with at most
m
non-zero entries as

\Theta(n,m)=\left\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\{0,1\}^{n}:\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|_{0}\leq m\right\}.

Let
\pi
denote the product measure of
n
independent Bernoulli distributions with shared parameter
m^{\prime}/n
, for some real number
m^{\prime}\in(0,m]
,
and let
\pi_{\Theta}
be the conditional distribution of
\pi
restricted to
\Theta(n,m)
, i.e., for any subset
E\subset\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\pi_{\Theta}(E)=\frac{\pi(E\cap\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\Theta(n,m)\})}{\pi(\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\Theta(n,m))}.

Let
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
be the binary indicator vector defined as

\eta_{i}=\mathbbm{1}\{i\in I_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}\},\quad i\in[n].

Then for any
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that is a measurable function of
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{1},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{2},\dots,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{N})
,

\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\Theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathrm{H}}\geq\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in[0,1]^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{\mathrm{H}}-4m^{\prime}\exp\left(-\frac{(m-m^{\prime})^{2}}{2m}\right).

(92)

Proof.

The result follows from a minor adaptation of Theorem 1.1 in
Butucea et al., 2018
.
Details are omitted for the sake of space.
∎

Lemma 5
.

Consider
\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{w}
, where
\boldsymbol{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{k})
and we test the two hypotheses

H_{0}:\boldsymbol{\theta}=\mathbf{0}\quad\text{ v.s. }\quad H_{1}:\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}\left(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1}\right).

Let
f_{i}
be the probability density function of
\boldsymbol{y}
under hypothesis
H_{i}
for
i=0,1
and let
L(\boldsymbol{y})=f_{1}(\boldsymbol{y})/f_{0}(\boldsymbol{y})
denote the likelihood ratio. Then for some universal constant
C
,

\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}\leq\exp\left\{\frac{Cb^{4}}{k\sigma^{4}}\right\}.

Proof.

The likelihood ratio is given by

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{1}(\boldsymbol{y})}{f_{0}(\boldsymbol{y})}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|^{2}\right\}}{\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|^{2}\right\}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{b^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\exp\left\{\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\}.

To compute the second moment of
L
under the null hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{0}\left[\exp\left\{-\frac{b^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\left(\exp\left\{\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\left(\exp\left\{\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right\}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{b^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\mathbb{E}_{0}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\exp\left\{\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\theta}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right\}\right].

By Fubini’s Theorem and properties of the Gaussian moment generating function, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{b^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{0}\exp\left\{\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\theta}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{b^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{b^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{b^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{v},\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{b^{2}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\right].

Denote
\boldsymbol{x}=\sqrt{k}\boldsymbol{v}
, where
\boldsymbol{v}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{k-1})
.
By Theorem 3.4.6 in
Vershynin, 2018
,
\boldsymbol{x}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\sqrt{k}\mathbb{S}^{k-1})
is a sub-Gaussian random vector with sub-Gaussian norm bounded by a constant.
Hence, fixing
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{S}^{k-1}
, by the property of the moment generating functions of sub-Gaussian random variables, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\sqrt{k}\mathbb{S}^{k-1})}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{b^{2}\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}}{\sqrt{k}\sigma^{2}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{\frac{b^{4}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\|^{2}_{2}C^{2}}{2k\sigma^{4}}\right\}=\exp\left\{\frac{Cb^{4}}{k\sigma^{4}}\right\},

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\|^{2}_{2}=1
since
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{S}^{k-1}
.
Noting the right hand side does not depend on
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}\leq\exp\left\{\frac{Cb^{4}}{k\sigma^{4}}\right\},

as we set out to show.
∎

Lemma 6
.

Consider
\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{w}
, where
\boldsymbol{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{k})
and we test the two hypotheses

H_{0}:\boldsymbol{\theta}=\mathbf{0}\quad\text{ v.s. }\quad H_{1}:\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\beta\operatorname{Unif}\left(\{\pm 1\}^{k}\right).

Let
f_{i}
be the probability density of
\boldsymbol{y}
under hypothesis
H_{i}
for
i=0,1
and let
L(\boldsymbol{y})=f_{1}(\boldsymbol{y})/f_{0}(\boldsymbol{y})
denote the likelihood ratio. Then for some universal constant
C
,

\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}\leq\exp\left\{\frac{\beta^{4}C^{2}}{2k\sigma^{4}}\right\}.

Proof.

Following the same proof of Lemma
5
, we have that

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\beta\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{k})}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}\}}{\exp\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|^{2}\}}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{k\beta^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\beta\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{k})}\exp\left\{\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}=\exp\left\{-\frac{k\beta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\mathbb{E}_{0}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sim\beta\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{k})}\exp\left\{\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\theta}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right\}\right].

Interchanging the order of the expectation and using properties of the Gaussian moment generating function, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{k\beta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sim\beta\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{k})}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sim\beta\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{k})}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\right].

By Lemma 3.4.2 in
Vershynin, 2018
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is a sub-Gaussian vector with sub-Gaussian norm bounded by
C\beta
for some constant
C>0
. Fixing
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, we have that the sub-Gaussian norm of
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is bounded by
C\beta^{2}\sqrt{k}
. By the property of the moment generating functions of sub-Gaussian random variables, it follows that

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\beta\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{k})}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{C\beta^{4}k}{\sigma^{4}}\right).

Noting that the right hand side does not depend on
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}\leq\exp\left(\frac{C\beta^{4}k}{\sigma^{4}}\right),

completing the proof.
∎

Lemma 7
.

Consider a random vector
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and a random variable
\eta\in\{0,1\}
such that
\eta\sim\pi_{0}
, where
\pi_{0}
is a Bernoulli distributions with parameter
m^{\prime}/n
.
Assume that when
\eta=0
,
\boldsymbol{y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{k})

and when
\eta=1
,
\boldsymbol{y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{w}
, where
\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1})
and
\boldsymbol{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{k})
.
Suppose that

\frac{4m^{\prime}}{n}-\frac{{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{n^{2}}\exp\left\{\frac{b^{4}C^{2}}{k\sigma^{4}}\right\}-\frac{3{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{n^{2}}\in[0,1],

(93)

then

\inf_{T\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\left[\left|T(\boldsymbol{y})-\eta\right|\right]\geq\frac{m^{\prime}}{n}-\frac{{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{4n^{2}}\exp\left\{\frac{b^{4}C^{2}}{k\sigma^{4}}\right\}-\frac{3{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{4n^{2}},

where the infimum is taken over all test functions
T(\boldsymbol{y})\in[0,1]
that is a
measurable function of
\boldsymbol{y}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\pi_{0}
, we can write the risk of
T
as

\displaystyle R(T)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}|T(\boldsymbol{y})-\eta|

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{\prime}}{n}\mathbb{E}_{1}(1-T(\boldsymbol{y}))+\frac{n-m^{\prime}}{n}\mathbb{E}_{0}T(\boldsymbol{y}).

Consider the simple versus simple hypothesis testing problem

H_{0}:\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{0}\quad\text{ vs. }\quad H_{1}:\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(b\mathbb{S}^{k-1}),

with a prior distribution
\mathbb{P}[H_{0}]=1-m^{\prime}/n
and
\mathbb{P}[H_{1}]=m^{\prime}/n
.
From the Bayesian version of the Neyman-Pearson Lemma, the optimal test
T^{\star}
is given by

T^{\star}(\boldsymbol{y})=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\frac{(m^{\prime}/n)f_{1}(\boldsymbol{y})}{\left(1-m^{\prime}/n\right)f_{0}(\boldsymbol{y})}>1\right\},

where
f_{i}
is the probability density function of
\boldsymbol{y}
under hypothesis
H_{i}
for
i=0,1
.
Let
\alpha=m^{\prime}/n
and denote the likelihood ratio as
L(\boldsymbol{y})=f_{1}(\boldsymbol{y})/f_{0}(\boldsymbol{y})
.
Then the minimum risk can be written as

\displaystyle R(T^{\star})

\displaystyle=\alpha+(1-\alpha)\mathbb{E}_{0}T^{\star}-\alpha\mathbb{E}_{1}T^{\star}

\displaystyle=\alpha-\int_{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}L>1}[\alpha f_{1}-(1-\alpha)f_{0}]d\boldsymbol{y}

\displaystyle=\alpha-\int_{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}L>1}\frac{\alpha f_{1}-(1-\alpha)f_{0}}{(1-\alpha)f_{0}}\cdot(1-\alpha)f_{0}d\boldsymbol{y}

\displaystyle=\alpha-\int_{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}L>1}\left(\alpha L-(1-\alpha)\right)\cdot f_{0}d\boldsymbol{y}

Noting that

\displaystyle\int\alpha f_{1}-(1-\alpha)f_{0}d\boldsymbol{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}L>1}\alpha f_{1}-(1-\alpha)f_{0}d\boldsymbol{y}+\int_{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}L<1}\alpha f_{1}-(1-\alpha)f_{0}d\boldsymbol{y}

\displaystyle=2\alpha-1,

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}\left|\alpha L-(1-\alpha)\right|

\displaystyle=\int_{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}L>1}\alpha f_{1}-(1-\alpha)f_{0}d\boldsymbol{y}-\int_{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}L<1}\alpha f_{1}-(1-\alpha)f_{0}d\boldsymbol{y},

it follows that

\displaystyle R(T^{\star})

\displaystyle=\alpha-\frac{1}{2}(2\alpha-1+\mathbb{E}_{0}\left|\alpha L-(1-\alpha)\right|)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{0}\left|\alpha L-(1-\alpha)\right|.

Applying Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle R(T^{\star})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{0}\left(\alpha L-(1-\alpha)\right)^{2}}

(94)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}_{0}L^{2}-2(1-\alpha)\alpha\mathbb{E}_{0}L+(1-\alpha)^{2}}.

Combining Equation (
94
) with Lemma
5
, we have

\displaystyle R(T^{\star})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\alpha^{2}\exp\left(\frac{b^{4}C^{2}}{k\sigma^{4}}\right)-2(1-\alpha)\alpha+(1-\alpha)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1+\frac{{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{n^{2}}\exp\left\{\frac{b^{4}C^{2}}{k\sigma^{4}}\right\}-\frac{4{m^{\prime}}}{n}+\frac{3{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{n^{2}}}

Since
1-\sqrt{1-x}\geq x/2
for
x\in[0,1]
, under Equation (
93
)
we have

\inf_{T\in[0,1]}R(T)\geq R(T^{\star})\geq\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{4m^{\prime}}{n}-\frac{{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{n^{2}}\exp\left(\frac{b^{4}C^{2}}{k\sigma^{4}}\right)-\frac{3{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{n^{2}}\right),

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8
.

Consider a random vector
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and a random variable
\eta\in\{0,1\}
such that
\eta\sim\pi_{0}
, where
\pi_{0}
is a Bernoulli distributions with parameter
m^{\prime}/n
.
Assume that we have
\boldsymbol{y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{k})
when
\eta=0
and when
\eta=1
we have
\boldsymbol{y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{w}
, where
\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\beta\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{k})
and
\boldsymbol{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{k})
.
Suppose that

\frac{4m^{\prime}}{n}-\frac{{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{n^{2}}\exp\left\{\frac{C\beta^{4}k}{\sigma^{4}}\right\}-\frac{3{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{n^{2}}\in[0,1],

then

\inf_{T\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\left[\left|T(\boldsymbol{y})-\eta\right|\right]\geq\frac{m^{\prime}}{n}-\frac{{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{4n^{2}}\exp\left\{\frac{C\beta^{4}k}{\sigma^{4}}\right\}-\frac{3{m^{\prime}}^{2}}{4n^{2}}.

Proof.

The result follows from the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
7
, and thus we omit the details.
∎

Lemma 9
.

For
a>0
and a random variable
k
such that
k-1\sim\operatorname{Binom}(n-1,m^{\prime}/n)
for
3m^{\prime}<n
, it follows that for
n
sufficiently large,

\mathbb{E}e^{\frac{a}{k}}\leq\exp\left\{a-Cn\log\left(\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right)\right\}+\exp\left\{\frac{a}{\log n}-Cn\right\}+e^{\frac{Ca}{n}},

where
C>0
is a universal constant that does not depend on
a,n
or
m^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Letting
\mu_{k}=\mathbb{E}(k-1)
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{a}{k}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{a}{k}\right\}\mathbbm{1}\{k-1\leq\log n\}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{a}{k}\right\}\mathbbm{1}\{\log n\leq k-1\leq(1-\delta)\mu_{k}\}\right]

(95)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left\{\frac{a}{k}\right\}\mathbbm{1}\{k-1\geq(1-\delta)\mu_{k}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{a}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\left(k-1\leq\log n\right)}_{P_{1}}+e^{\frac{a}{1+\log n}}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\left(k-1\leq(1-\delta)\mu_{k}\right)}_{P_{2}}+e^{\frac{a}{(1-\delta)\mu_{k}+1}}.

To bound
P_{1}
, let
X=\sum_{j\neq i}\eta_{j}
and observe that
X\sim\operatorname{Binom}(n-1,m^{\prime}/n)
.
It follows that

\displaystyle P_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(X\geq n-1-\log n\right)\leq e^{-\frac{n-1}{n}m^{\prime}}\left(\frac{e(n-1)m^{\prime}/n}{n-1-\log n}\right)^{n-1-\log n},

where the inequality follows from Chernoff’s inequality for large deviations
(
Vershynin, 2018
, see Theorem 2.3.1 in)
.
For suitably large
n
, we have
n-1-\log n>en/3
, and it follows that

\displaystyle P_{1}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{n-1}{n}m^{\prime}}\left(\frac{em^{\prime}}{n-1-\log n}\right)^{n-1-\log n}\leq\left(\frac{3m^{\prime}}{n}\right)^{2n/3}=\exp\left\{-\frac{2n}{3}\log\left(\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right)\right\}.

(96)

To bound
P_{2}
, since
k-1\sim\operatorname{Binom}(n-1,1-m^{\prime}/n)
, Chernoff’s inequality for small deviations
(
Vershynin, 2018
, see Exercise 2.3.5 in)
yields that

\displaystyle P_{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{\mu_{k}\delta^{2}}{2}}=\exp\left\{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}\left(n-1-\frac{(n-1)m^{\prime}}{n}\right)\right\}\leq e^{-\delta^{2}n/6},

(97)

where the second inequality holds for
n>2
by our assumption that
m^{\prime}<n/3
.

Applying Equations (
96
) and (
97
) to Equation (
95
) and taking
\delta=1/2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{a}{k}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{a-\frac{2n}{3}\log\left(\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right)\right\}+\exp\left\{\frac{a}{1+\log n}-Cn\right\}+e^{\frac{2a}{n-(n-1)m^{\prime}/n}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{a-Cn\log\left(\frac{n}{3m^{\prime}}\right)\right\}+\exp\left\{\frac{a}{\log n}-Cn\right\}+e^{\frac{Ca}{n}},

where
C>0
is a universal constant that does not depend on
n
and
m^{\prime}
as long as
n
is sufficiently large (and again using our assumption that
n>3m^{\prime}
).
∎

Appendix C
Localized Error Bound for Initial Estimator

We begin by stating a refined bound on the row-wise estimation error of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
.
For any matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
with singular value decomposition
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{X}_{2}^{\top}
, we define the generalized sign matrix as
\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{A}):=\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\boldsymbol{X}_{2}^{\top}
.
Adapting the proof of Theorem 4.2 in
Chen et al., 2021b
, we obtain the following refined bound on the row-wise estimation error of
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
.

Theorem 21
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
is given by Equation (
1
), that is,
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
, where the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
satisfies Assumption
1
and Equation (
4
).
Assume that

|\lambda^{\star}_{r}|\geq C\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n\log n}

(98)

for some sufficiently large constant
C
, and that the condition number
\kappa=O(1)
.
Then with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, the following bound holds uniformly for all
\ell\in[n]
:

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}}{|\lambda^{\star}_{r}|}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{|\lambda^{\star}_{r}|}

where

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}:=\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)\top}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}.

(99)

C.1
Proof of Theorem
21

Proof.

For any
\ell\in[n]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\right\|_{2}.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\lambda^{\star}_{r}>0
.
Using Lemma
11
to bound the first term,

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}.

(100)

Applying submultiplicativity of the norm followed by Lemma
10
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\|\leq\frac{2\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\right\|^{2}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}.

Since

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{-1}\|\leq 2\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{2},

where the last inequality follows from Lemma
10
.
By the above display and the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\right\|^{2}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\right\|^{2}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\left(\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right),

where the last inequality follows from
\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\|\leq C\sigma\sqrt{n}
holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
for some constant
C>0
(see for example, Theorem 3.4 in
Chen et al., 2021b
).
Thus, applying the above bound to Equation (
100
) under the assumption in Equation (
98
) gives

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}

where the last inequality follows from Lemma
11
.
∎

Lemma 10
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
21
,
if
|\lambda^{\star}_{r}|>2\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\|
, then

\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\right\|\leq\frac{2\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\|^{2}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{-1}\|\leq 2,

where
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}=\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)\top}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
is the matrix defined in Theorem
21
.
The same bounds hold when we replace
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}
by its leave-one-out version
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}
, for any
\ell\in[n]
.

Proof.

Denote the SVD of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}
as
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}=\boldsymbol{X}\left(\cos\boldsymbol{\Theta}\right)\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}
and define the matrix
\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}
.
We write the singular values of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}
as a diagonal matrix
\cos(\boldsymbol{\Theta})=\operatorname{diag}(\cos(\theta_{1}),\cdots,\cos(\theta_{r}))
, since the singular values of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}
are the cosines of the principal angles between the subspaces spanned by the columns of
\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}
and
{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{X}(\cos\boldsymbol{\Theta}-\boldsymbol{I})\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}\right\|\leq\|\boldsymbol{I}-\cos\boldsymbol{\Theta}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{I}-\cos^{2}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\|=\|\sin^{2}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\right\|^{2}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}},

where the second inequality follows from the fact that
1-\cos\theta_{i}\geq 1-\cos^{2}\theta_{i}
for all
i\in[r]
, and the last inequality follows from the Davis-Kahan
\sin\Theta
theorem
(see Theorem 2.7 in
Chen et al., 2021b
, for a reference)
.
Thus, we have

\sigma_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\geq\|\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\|-\|\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\operatorname{sgn}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})\|\geq 1-\frac{2\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\|^{2}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\geq 1/2,

as long as
|\lambda_{r}^{\star}|>2\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}\|
.
Noting that

\|\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{-1}\|=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}})}\leq 2

completes the proof.
∎

With the help of Theorem
21
, we can now prove the main result of this section, which provides a bound on the row-wise estimation error of
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
relative to
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
Some supporting technical results are deferred to Section
C.3
below.

C.2
Proof of Theorem
2

Proof.

Recall from Equation (
99
) that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}:=\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)\top}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
.
For notational simplicity, we will drop the superscript
(0)
in
\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}
when it is clear from context in this section.
We caution the reader not to confuse this with the matrix
\boldsymbol{U}
that appears in Section
5
, which serves a distinct role.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\right)\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\right\|_{2}}_{\gamma_{1}}+\underbrace{\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{2}}_{\gamma_{2}}.

(101)

and, similarly, for any
k\in[n]
,

\left|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell k}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}_{\ell k}\right|\leq\underbrace{\left|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\right)\boldsymbol{U}_{k,\cdot}^{\top}\right|}_{\gamma^{(\ell,k)}_{1}}+\underbrace{\left|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}_{k,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}_{k,\cdot}\right|}_{\gamma^{(\ell,k)}_{2}}.

(102)

Throughout the proof that follows, we will repeatedly use the inequalities

\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{y}\right|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{y}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{x}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{y}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\right\|

and

\|\boldsymbol{A}{\boldsymbol{B}}\|\leq\|\boldsymbol{A}\|\|{\boldsymbol{B}}\|

without explicit referencing, where
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}
are real vectors and
\boldsymbol{A},{\boldsymbol{B}}
are real matrices and they have compatible dimensions.

Step 1: Bounding
\gamma_{1}
.

By definition of
\gamma_{1}
above, we have

\gamma_{1}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\right\|\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\right\|.

(103)

and, similarly for
\gamma^{(\ell,k)}_{1}
,
writing
\nu_{k}=\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{k,\cdot}\right\|_{2}
for
k\in[n]
,

\gamma^{(\ell,k)}_{1}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{k,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\right\|=\nu_{\ell}\nu_{k}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\right\|.

(104)

By Equation (4.139) in
Chen et al., 2021b
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{(0)}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\kappa\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda_{r}^{\star}}+\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}

holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
by choosing sufficiently large constants in the bound.
Substituting the above bound into Equations (
103
) and (
104
) yields that

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\nu_{\ell}\left(\frac{\kappa\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}\right)

(105)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\kappa\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda_{r}^{\star}}+\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}\right)\left(\left\|(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}})_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right)

and

\displaystyle\gamma^{(\ell,k)}_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\nu_{\ell}\nu_{k}\left(\frac{\kappa\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda_{r}^{\star}}+\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\kappa\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda_{r}^{\star}}+\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}\right)\left(\left\|(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}})_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right)\left(\left\|(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}})_{k,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{k,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right).

Let

\delta_{k}=\left\|(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}})_{k,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\text{ and }\nu^{\star}_{k}=\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{k,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\text{ for all }k\in[n].

Then we can rewrite the above bound as

\displaystyle\gamma^{(\ell,k)}_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\kappa\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda_{r}^{\star}}+\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}\right)\left(\delta_{\ell}+\nu^{\star}_{\ell}\right)\left(\delta_{k}+\nu^{\star}_{k}\right).

(106)

Step 2: Bounding
\gamma_{2}
.

Since the difference
\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}
can be decomposed into

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}+\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)^{\top}+{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)^{\top}.

By triangle inequality, one can upper bound
\gamma_{2}
by

\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right)\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right)\right\|_{2},

which is further bounded by

\lambda^{\star}_{1}\delta_{\ell}+\lambda^{\star}_{1}\left(\delta_{\ell}+\nu^{\star}_{\ell}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|\lesssim\lambda^{\star}_{1}\left(\delta_{\ell}+\nu^{\star}_{\ell}\right)\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\lambda^{\star}_{1}\delta_{\ell}\lesssim\kappa\sigma\sqrt{n}\nu^{\star}_{\ell}+\lambda^{\star}_{1}\delta_{\ell}.

up to some constant factor.
Similarly, one can bound
\gamma^{(\ell,k)}_{2}
by

\left|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}_{k,\cdot}\right|+\left|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)^{\top}_{k,\cdot}\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)^{\top}_{k,\cdot}\right|,

and it is further bounded by

\lambda^{\star}_{1}\delta_{\ell}\nu^{\star}_{k}+\lambda^{\star}_{1}\delta_{\ell}\delta_{k}+\lambda^{\star}_{1}\delta_{k}\nu^{\star}_{\ell}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\kappa\sigma\sqrt{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\lambda^{\star}_{1}\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\sigma\kappa^{2}\sqrt{n}\right)\nu^{\star}_{\ell}+\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n},

(107)

where the second inequality follows from Equation (
108
) in Lemma
11
. Similarly, one has

\gamma^{(\ell,k)}_{2}\lesssim\left(\sigma\kappa^{2}\sqrt{n}\right)\nu^{\star}_{k}\nu^{\star}_{\ell}+\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}\left(\nu^{\star}_{k}+\nu^{\star}_{\ell}\right)+\frac{\sigma\kappa^{3}n\nu^{\star}_{\ell}\nu^{\star}_{k}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\kappa r\log n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}.

Step 3: Putting everything together.

Combining Equations
105
and
107
with Equation (
101
), we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\sigma\kappa^{2}\sqrt{n}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n},

as desired.
∎

C.3
Supporting technical results

Lemma 11
.

Under the setting of Theorem
2
,
with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, it holds for all
\ell\in[n]
that

\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{\left(\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n}\right)\nu^{\star}_{\ell}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}},

(108)

where
\nu^{\star}_{\ell}=\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}
and we abbreviate
\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}
to
\boldsymbol{U}
for notational simplicity.

Proof.

By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}.

By standard concentration inequalities
(
Vershynin, 2018
)
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, it holds uniformly over all
\ell\in[n]
that

\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}+L\log n\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}.

Applying Lemma
12
and the assumptions in Equations (
4
) and (
98
),

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\left(\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma L\sqrt{n\log^{2}n}}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{r}\right)^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|_{2,\infty}.

Taking maximum over
\ell\in[n]
on both sides in the above display and rearranging terms, one has

\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\frac{\left(\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}},

which implies that

\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{\left(\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}},

as we set out to show.
∎

Lemma 12
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
2
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, for all
\ell\in[n]
, the difference

\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}\|_{2}
is bounded by

\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{r}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma L\sqrt{n\log^{2}n}}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{r}\right)^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|_{2,\infty}+\frac{\sigma^{3}n^{3/2}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{3}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}.

Proof.

Following essentially the same proof as Equations (4.124)-(4.127) in
Chen et al., 2021b
, one arrives at the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}+\lambda^{\star}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2}\right).

Applying standard concentration inequalities
(
Vershynin, 2018
)
to control the
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}
term, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, it holds uniformly over all
\ell\in[n]
that

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}.

(109)

By the triangle inequality, one has

\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}.

(110)

The first term on the right hand side can be bounded directly by

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\|\|\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{I}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\kappa\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2},

where the last inequality follows from the Davis-Kahan
\sin\Theta
theorem.
Applying this bound to Equation (
110
),

\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{\kappa\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}.

Combined the above bound and Lemma
13
with Equation (
110
) yields that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+(L\log n)\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2},

where we use the fact that
\kappa\geq 1
to collect terms.
Taking the above bound into the right hand side of Equation (
109
) gives

\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{r}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma L\sqrt{n\log^{2}n}}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{r}\right)^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|_{2,\infty}+\frac{\sigma^{3}n^{3/2}}{(\lambda^{\star}_{r})^{3}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}

as desired.
∎

Lemma 13
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
2
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, for all
\ell\in[n]
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}\lesssim(L\log n)\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|_{2,\infty}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right).

Proof.

By the triangle inequality and basic properties of the norm,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

Controlling the
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}
term using basic concentration inequalities
(
Vershynin, 2018
)
, it holds with high probability that, uniformly over all
\ell\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}+\sigma\sqrt{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

(111)

where the last inequality holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
uniformly over all
\ell\in[n]
by choosing sufficiently large constants in the bound.
The first term in Equation (
111
) can be directly bounded by

(L\log n)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\sigma\sqrt{\log n}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

Applying triangle inequality, the above is further bounded by

\displaystyle(L\log n)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\sigma\sqrt{\log n}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\sigma\sqrt{\log n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

(112)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(L\log n)\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\right)+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}},

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
L\log n>\sigma\sqrt{\log n}
and triangle inequality.
Since
L\log n\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{n}
under the assumption in Equation (
4
), the main contribution of Equation (
112
) is

(L\log n)\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}.

(113)

It remains to control the second term in Equation (
111
).
By Equation (4.105) in
Chen et al., 2021b
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim\frac{\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0,\ell)}\right)\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}.

(114)

By construction of
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0,\ell)}
, we bound the numerator in Equation (
114
) by

\left(\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0,\ell)}\right)\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}=\boldsymbol{e}_{\ell}\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}+\left(\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\cdot,\ell}-W_{\ell\ell}\boldsymbol{e}_{l}\right)\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}_{\ell,\cdot}.

Together with the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0,\ell)}\right)\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\cdot,\ell}-W^{(0)}_{\ell\ell}\boldsymbol{e}_{\ell}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}

(115)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{2}+\sigma\sqrt{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\log n}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}+L\log n\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{2,\infty}

(116)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}+L\log n\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}\right)

where the first inequality holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
uniformly over all
\ell\in[n]
by choosing sufficiently large constants in the bound.
To control
\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}
, we apply Lemma
10
and use triangle inequality to obtain

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}

Taking the above bound and Equations (
115
) and (
116
) into Equation (
114
) yields

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{L\log n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right)

which after reorganizing, yields

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{L\log n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right)

By the assumption in Equation (
4
), the above bound for
\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}
can be simplified as

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}^{(\ell)}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right)

(117)

Taking Equations (
117
) and (
113
) into Equation (
111
) yields

\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}_{\ell,\cdot}\left(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)\right\|_{2}\lesssim(L\log n)\|\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|_{2,\infty}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda^{\star}_{r}}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{O}}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}\right)

as desired.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of SDP

Here we collect proof details related to SDP.
Throughout this section, we treat the error matrix
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
, given by Equation (
8
), as fixed, since it comes from
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(0)}
and is independent of
\boldsymbol{Y}^{(1)}
.
Appendices
D.1
-
D.6
gives a detailed proof of the SDP formulated in Equation (
22
), along with necessary technical lemmas.
Specifically, the main result Theorem
7
, is shown in Appendix
D.5
, and Corollary
2
is proved in Appendix
D.6
.
Appendix
D.7
provides the proof of Theorem
8
, which establishes the minimax optimality of SDP.
Appendices
D.8
and
D.9
establishes results related to the truncated SDP.
Appendix
D.10
handles results related to the SDP when multiple observations are available, specifically Theorem
11
.
Finally, Appendix
D.11
contains technical results that are used in the proofs in this section.

D.1
Proof of Lemma
2

Proof.

Recall that the cost matrix
\boldsymbol{C}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
.
By the optimality of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}
, we have

\langle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star},\boldsymbol{C}\rangle\leq 0.

(118)

Recall that the matrix
\boldsymbol{C}^{(1)}
defined in Equation (
26
) removes
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}
from the cost matrix
\boldsymbol{C}
, where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}
denotes the estimation error matrix of the low rank component
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
.
Since we can relabel the indices in
{I_{\star}}
, without loss of generality, we assume that
