The remaining terms in
\widetilde{S}_{1}
follow similarly to Lemma
15
, leading to

|\widetilde{S}_{1}|\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\left(\sigma^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n^{2}/\varepsilon_{0})}{n}}+\frac{L^{2}}{n}\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)+\sigma\beta\sqrt{\frac{r(\widetilde{\mu}\vee\log n)}{n}}\right).

Under the setting where
\widetilde{\mu}r^{3/2}\leq cn\beta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
,
it follows that

\beta^{2}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right\|_{1}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\left(\sigma^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n^{2}/\varepsilon_{0})}{n}}+\frac{L^{2}}{n}\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)\right),

which implies that

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right\|_{1}\lesssim n^{2}\max\left\{\exp\left(-\frac{n\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}\right),\exp\left(-\frac{n\beta^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\right)\right\}.

Step 4: Extending Corollary
2
.

Our final step is to extend the previous results to the case where
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
follows Equation (
25
).
For all
i,j\in[n]
,
let

\widetilde{C}_{ij}=\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{1,ij}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{ij}+B^{\star}_{ij}\right)\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{2,ij}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{ij}+B^{\star}_{ij}\right)

and

\widetilde{C}^{(0)}_{ij}=\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{1,ij}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{ij}+B^{(0)}_{ij}\right)\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{2,ij}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{ij}+B^{(0)}_{ij}\right),

where
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{(0)}
follows Equation (
24
).
By construction of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
, we have

\widetilde{\Delta}_{ij}+B^{\star}_{ij}\geq\widetilde{\Delta}_{ij}+B^{(0)}_{ij}

(185)

for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
.
Let

\Theta_{ij}:=\widetilde{\Delta}_{ij}+B^{\star}_{ij}\quad\text{and}\quad\Theta_{ij}^{(0)}:=\widetilde{\Delta}_{ij}+B^{(0)}_{ij}.

Our goal is then to show that for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{1,ij}+\Theta_{ij}\right)\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{2,ij}+\Theta_{ij}\right)\geq t\right]\geq\mathbb{P}\left[\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{1,ij}+\Theta^{(0)}_{ij}\right)\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{2,ij}+\Theta^{(0)}_{ij}\right)\geq t\right].

Let
B_{k,ij}:=\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{k,ij}+\Theta_{ij}
and
A_{k,ij}:=\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{k,ij}+\Theta^{(0)}_{ij}
.
By Equation (
185
), we have
A_{k,ij}\preceq B_{k,ij}
for
k=1,2
.
By Lemma
39
, it follows that
A_{1,ij}A_{2,ij}\preceq B_{1,ij}B_{2,ij}
.
The remainder of the argument follows the same argument as in the proof of Corollary
2
, and so details are omitted.
∎

D.11
Auxilary Lemmas for SDP Proofs

Lemma 20
.

Under the same assumptions as in Lemma
2
,

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right\|_{1}<5\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij}

Proof.

Let

\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{1}=\left(\widehat{Z}_{ij}\right)_{i,j\in{I_{\star}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{2}=\left(\widehat{Z}_{ij}\right)_{i,j\in I_{\star}^{c}}.

(186)

Without loss of generality, we assume that
{I_{\star}}=[m]
as we can always relabel the indices.
Let
\alpha:=\operatorname{tr}\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{1}
.
By the constrain
\widehat{Z}_{ii}\leq 1
for all
i\in[n]
in Equation (
22
), we have

\alpha=\operatorname{tr}\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{Z}_{ii}\leq m\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{tr}\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{2}=\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ii}=K-\alpha.

(187)

We note that if

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{Z}_{ij},

(188)

then expanding the inner product between
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}
and
\boldsymbol{J}
yields

\displaystyle\left\langle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}},\boldsymbol{J}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{Z}_{ij}+2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\widehat{Z}_{ii}+\widehat{Z}_{jj}}{2}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\frac{\widehat{Z}_{ii}+\widehat{Z}_{jj}}{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}2m\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{1})+K\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}2m\alpha+K(K-\alpha)=K^{2}-\alpha(n-3m),

where the inequality follows from the semidefiniteness of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}
that
2\widehat{Z}_{ij}\leq\widehat{Z}_{ii}+\widehat{Z}_{jj}
for all
i,j\in[n]
and Equation (
188
), equality
(i)
follows from the definition of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{2}
in Equation (
186
) and equality
(ii)
follows from Equation (
187
).
Hence, under the constraint that
\langle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}},\boldsymbol{J}\rangle=K^{2}
and the assumption that
3m<n
, we must have

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij}>\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{Z}_{ij}\right).

(189)

It follows that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{Z}_{ij}+2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}(1-\widehat{Z}_{ij})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{Z}_{ij}+2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij}+K^{2}-\left(K^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{Z}_{ij}-2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{Z}_{ij}+4\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij}<5\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\widehat{Z}_{ij},

where the inequality follows from Equation (
189
).
∎

Lemma 21
.

Under the same assumptions as in Lemma
14
,

\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}})\right)\leq\frac{1}{K}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1}.

Proof.

Noting that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}\succeq 0
, we have

\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}})=\left(\boldsymbol{I}-\frac{1}{K}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right)\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{I}-\frac{1}{K}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right)\succeq 0.

Since
\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star})=0
by definition of
\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}
in Equation (
33
), we have

\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}})\right)=\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star})\right)=\operatorname{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{I}-\frac{1}{K}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right)(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star})\left(\boldsymbol{I}-\frac{1}{K}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right).

By cyclicity of the trace and the fact that
\boldsymbol{I}-\frac{1}{K}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
is a projection matrix, it follows that

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{I}-\frac{1}{K}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\right)(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\operatorname{tr}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}})\right].

where the last equality holds since
\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star})=\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}})=K
.
Since
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}_{ik}=1
for
i,k\in I_{\star}^{c}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}_{ik}=0
otherwise, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}\mathcal{P}_{T^{\perp}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z^{\star}_{ik}(Z^{\star}_{ik}-\widehat{Z}_{ik})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}(Z^{\star}_{ik}-\widehat{Z}_{ik})\leq\frac{1}{K}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Z}}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\|_{1},

as we set out to show.
∎

Lemma 22
.

For any matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and any matrix
\boldsymbol{\Xi}
, we have

\frac{1}{K}\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Xi}\right\rangle\right|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\lceil\varepsilon_{0}/K\rceil}X_{(j)}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{K}\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{A}\frac{\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}}{K},\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Xi}\right\rangle\right|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\lceil\varepsilon_{0}/K\rceil}X_{(j)},

where
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
is given in Equation (
23
),
K=n-m
,
X_{j}:=\left|\sum_{k\in I^{c}_{\star}}\Xi_{kj}\right|
for
j\in[n]
and
X_{(j)}
denotes the
j
-th order statistic of the random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
{I_{\star}}=[m]
.
By definition of
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}
, we have
Z^{\star}_{ik}=0
if either
i\in{I_{\star}}=[m]
or
k\in[m]
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\langle\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Xi}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}A_{ij}\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z^{\star}_{ik}\Xi_{kj}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\frac{A_{ij}}{K}\right)\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}\Xi_{kj}.

Let
a_{j}:=\left|\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}A_{ij}\right|/K
for
j\in[n]
.
By the assumption that
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, we have
a_{j}\leq 1
for all
j\in[n]
.
It follows from the above display and the definition of
X_{j}
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\langle\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Xi}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}X_{j}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\lceil\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\rceil}X_{(j)}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\lceil\varepsilon_{0}/K\rceil}X_{(j)},

(190)

where the second inequality follows from
a_{j}\leq 1
for all
j\in[n]
and properties of order statistics, while the last inequality follows from the assumption that
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.
We also have that

\left(\frac{1}{K}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{A}\right)_{ij}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z^{\star}_{ik}A_{kj}\leq\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 1,

by assumption on
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\infty}
, and

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1}{K}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{A}\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z^{\star}_{ik}A_{kj}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kj}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{Z^{\star}_{ik}}{K}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{0},

from which, respectively,
\|\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{A}/K\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\|\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{A}/K\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.
Hence, the matrix
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{A}/K
satisfies the same assumptions as
\boldsymbol{A}
.
Replacing
\boldsymbol{A}
by
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{A}/K
in Equation (
190
) yields that

\frac{1}{K}\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{A}\frac{\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}}{K},\boldsymbol{Z}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Xi}\right\rangle\right|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\lceil\varepsilon_{0}/K\rceil}X_{(j)},

as we set out to show.
∎

Lemma 23
.

Under Assumption
5
and assuming that the distribution of
W_{ij}
is symmetric about
0
for all
i,j\in[n]
, for any
a,\delta>0
, for any
1\leq i,j\leq n
, both
(W_{ij}+a+\delta)^{2}
and
(W_{ij}-a-\delta)^{2}
have first-order stochastic dominance over
(W_{ij}-a)^{2}
and
(W_{ij}+a)^{2}
, respectively.

Proof.

Denote the cumulative distribution function of
W_{ij}
by
F(x)=\mathbb{P}(W_{ij}\leq x)
for a given pair of
i,j\in[n]
.
We first show that

F(x+a+\delta)+F(x-a-\delta)\leq F(x+a)+F(x-a).

(191)

Let
(v)_{+}:=\max\{0,v\}
.
If
x\geq a
, then using the symmetry about zero assumption of
W_{ij}
, we have

\displaystyle F(x+a+\delta)-F(x+a)

\displaystyle=\int_{x+a}^{x+a+\delta}f(u)du

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(x-a-\delta)_{+}}^{x-a}f(u)du+\int_{0}^{(a+\delta-x)_{+}}f(u)du

\displaystyle=\int_{x-a-\delta}^{x-a}f(u)du=F(x-a)-F(x-a-\delta).

Rearranging the above equation yields that Equation (
191
) holds when
x\geq a
.
Similarly, if
x<a
, we have

\displaystyle F(x-a)-F(x-a-\delta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(x-a-\delta\leq W_{ij}\leq x-a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(a-x\leq W_{ij}\leq a+\delta-x)=\int_{a-x}^{a+\delta-x}f(u)du

\displaystyle\geq\int_{a+x}^{a+x+\delta}f(u)du=F(x+a+\delta)-F(x+a),

which establishes that Equation (
191
) also holds for
x<a
.

Noting that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left(W_{ij}+a\right)^{2}\geq x^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(W_{ij}\geq x-a\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(W_{ij}\leq-a-x\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(W_{ij}\geq x-a\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(W_{ij}\geq x+a\right)=2-(F(x-a)+F(x+a))

and

\mathbb{P}\left[\left(W_{ij}+a+\delta\right)^{2}\geq x^{2}\right]=2-(F(x-a-\delta)+F(x+a+\delta))

It follows from Equation (
191
) that

\mathbb{P}\left[\left(W_{ij}+a\right)^{2}\geq x^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left(W_{ij}+a+\delta\right)^{2}\geq x^{2}\right].

Since

\mathbb{P}\left[\left(W_{ij}-a\right)^{2}\geq x^{2}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\left(W_{ij}+a\right)^{2}\geq x^{2}\right]

and

\mathbb{P}\left[\left(W_{ij}-a-\delta\right)^{2}\geq x^{2}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\left(W_{ij}+a+\delta\right)^{2}\geq x^{2}\right],

the proof is now complete.
∎

Appendix E
The Solution Path of Group Lasso

Let
\boldsymbol{Y}\in\operatorname{Sym}(n)
denote an observed symmetric matrix.
In this section, our goal is to analyze the theoretical properties of applying the overlapping group lasso estimator to
\boldsymbol{Y}
, with a particular focus on its solution path.
We defer the analysis of support recovery properties to Section
F
.
Specifically, we analyze the solution path of the overlapping group lasso optimization problem

\displaystyle\min_{{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\operatorname{Sym}(n)}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}(y_{ij}-B_{ij})^{2}+\lambda\Omega({\boldsymbol{B}})

\displaystyle=\min_{\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}(y_{ij}-v_{ij}-v_{ji})^{2}+\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{ij}^{2}},

where
\boldsymbol{Y}=[y_{ij}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
denotes the observed matrix,
and the penalty term
\Omega({\boldsymbol{B}})
enforces node-level group sparsity via the overlapping group Lasso, where
{\boldsymbol{B}}=\boldsymbol{V}+\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}
:

\Omega({\boldsymbol{B}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{ij}^{2}}

To efficiently solve this optimization problem, we employ the Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers
(ADMM; see
Boyd et al., 2011
, for an overview)
.
The corresponding iterative procedure is detailed in Algorithm
5
.
The operator
\mathscr{T}_{2}
denotes the row-wise soft-thresholding operator, defined for each row
i\in[n]
by

\mathscr{T}_{2}(\boldsymbol{A},q)_{i,\cdot}=\left(1-\frac{q}{\left\|\boldsymbol{A}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}}\right)_{+}\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{i,\cdot}\right)

where
(x)_{+}=\min\{x,0\}
and
q
is a positive scalar parameter.

Algorithm 5

ADMM for Group Lasso

1:

:
\boldsymbol{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
\lambda>0
,
\rho>0
,
T>0
;
Initialize
\boldsymbol{V}^{(0)}
,
\boldsymbol{Z}^{(0)}
,
\boldsymbol{U}^{(0)}
randomly.

2:

:
\boldsymbol{V}^{(T)}
.

3:

for

t=0,\cdots,T-1

do

4:

\boldsymbol{V}
-step
:
\boldsymbol{V}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\frac{1}{\rho+2}\left(\boldsymbol{Y}-(\boldsymbol{Z}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)})^{\top}\right)+\frac{\rho+1}{\rho+2}(\boldsymbol{Z}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)})

5:

\boldsymbol{Z}
-step
:
\boldsymbol{Z}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathscr{T}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{(t+1)}+\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)},\frac{\lambda}{\rho}\right)
,

6:

\boldsymbol{U}
-step
:
\boldsymbol{U}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)}+\boldsymbol{V}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{Z}^{(t+1)}

7:

end

for

E.1
KKT Condition and Reformulation

Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}=(v_{i1},v_{i2},\ldots,v_{in})^{\top}
denote the
i
-th row of
\boldsymbol{V}
.
Suppose that, for a given index set
I\subset[n]
, we obtain a solution with the property that
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
for every
i\in I
, while
\boldsymbol{v}_{j}=\boldsymbol{0}
for all
j\notin I
.
For such a solution to be optimal for the overlapping group lasso problem,
it must satisfy the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions.
Specifically, these conditions can be stated as follows:

\displaystyle v_{ij}+v_{ji}+\lambda\frac{v_{ij}}{\|\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=y_{ij},~~~~~~\text{ if }i,j\in I,i\neq j;

(192)

\displaystyle v_{ij}+\lambda\frac{v_{ij}}{\|\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=y_{ij},~~~~~~\text{ if }i\in I,j\in I^{c}\text{ or }i=j;

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}(y_{\ell i}-v_{i\ell})^{2}+\sum_{i\in I^{c}}y_{\ell i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{2},~~~~~~~\text{if }j\in I^{c}.

We remind the reader that
\lambda
is a nonnegative regularization parameter.
The KKT reformulation offers valuable structural insight into the solution of the overlapping group lasso problem.
Let us denote

\alpha_{i}=\|\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\|_{2}\quad\text{ for }i\in[n],

(193)

and by the property of
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}
and
I
, we have
\alpha_{i}>0
for
i\in I
and
\alpha_{i}=0
for
i\in I^{c}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{n})^{\top}
.

From the first equality in Equation (
192
) and the symmetry of the observation matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}
, we have

\frac{v_{ij}}{\alpha_{i}}=\frac{v_{ji}}{\alpha_{j}}\quad\text{for }i,j\in I.

Using this identity, we can express the KKT conditions more explicitly in terms of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and the observed data:

\displaystyle v_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}+\lambda}~y_{ij}

\displaystyle\text{ if }i,j\in I,i\neq j;

(194)

\displaystyle v_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{i}+\lambda}~y_{ij}

\displaystyle\text{ if }i\in I,j\in I^{c},\text{ or }i=j;

and

\sum_{k\in I}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\alpha_{k}+\lambda}\right)^{2}y_{kj}^{2}+\sum_{k\in I^{c}}y_{kj}^{2}\leq\lambda^{2},~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\text{ if }j\in I^{c}.

(195)

Combining the equalities in Equation (
194
) with the definition
\alpha_{i}=\|\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\|_{2}
yields that

\frac{y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{j\in I^{c}}y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}+\sum_{j\in I,j\neq i}\frac{y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}+\lambda)^{2}}=1,~~~~~~~\text{ for }i\in I.

(196)

This non-linear system characterizes the
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
values as implicit functions of
\lambda
and the data
\boldsymbol{Y}
.
Importantly, the set of equations (
196
) and inequalities (
195
) always has at least one solution,
due to the continuity and coercivity of the objective function in Equation (
38
).
This ensures the existence of a valid minimizer for the group lasso problem.

E.2
Local Analysis of the Solution Path

To further understand how the solution evolves with
\lambda
,
for any fixed
\lambda\geq 0
, we may partition the index set
[n]
into three disjoint subsets
E_{1}(\lambda),E_{2}(\lambda),J(\lambda)
such that:

•

\alpha_{i}>0
for
i\in E_{1}(\lambda)
;

•

\alpha_{i}=0
but the KKT equality holds for
i\in E_{2}(\lambda)
;

•

the KKT inequality is strict for
i\in J(\lambda)
.

More precisely, this partition gives rise to a set of equations and inequalities:

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in E_{1}(\lambda),i\neq j}\frac{y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}+\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{j\in E_{1}(\lambda)^{c}}y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}

\displaystyle=1,~~~~~~~\text{ if }i\in E_{1}(\lambda);

(197)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in E_{1}(\lambda)}\frac{y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{\sum_{i\in E_{1}(\lambda)^{c}}y_{ij}^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle=1,~~~~~~~~\text{ if }i\in E_{2}(\lambda);

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in E_{1}(\lambda)}\frac{y_{\ell j}^{2}}{(\alpha_{\ell}+\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{\sum_{j\in E_{1}(\lambda)^{c}}y_{\ell j}^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle<1,~~~~~~~~\text{ if }\ell\in J(\lambda).

For indices
i\in E(\lambda)=E_{1}(\lambda)\cup E_{2}(\lambda)
, we can compactly express the conditions as:

\sum_{j\in E(\lambda),j\neq i}\frac{y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}+\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{j\in E(\lambda)^{c}}y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{j}+\lambda)^{2}}=1,~~~~~~~\text{ if }i\in E(\lambda).

(198)

We next show that under mild conditions, each
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)
evolves smoothly and monotonically in
\lambda
.

Lemma 24
.

Suppose that for some
\lambda_{0}>0
, Equation (
198
) has a solution
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}
with
m_{0}:=|E(\lambda_{0})|<n
.
Suppose that the observed matrix
y_{ij}>0
holds for all
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
.
Then there exists a neighborhood
(\lambda_{0}-\delta,\lambda_{0}+\delta)
for some
\delta>0
, such that:

1.

A unique solution
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
exists in this neighborhood and continuously extends
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}
;

2.

For every
i\in E(\lambda_{0})
, the function
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)
is continuous and strictly decreasing in
\lambda
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume
E(\lambda_{0})=[m_{0}]
.
We denote by
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{m_{0}})^{\top}
the vector of active group norms, but we will drop the subscript
E
for simplicity in the remainder of the proof.
For each
i\in[m_{0}]
, define the function

F_{i}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda)=\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{m_{0}}\frac{y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}+\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{j=m_{0}+1}^{n}y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}-1.

(199)

Let
F(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda)=(F_{1},F_{2},\dots,F_{m_{0}})^{\top}
.
We compute the Jacobian matrix
\partial F/\partial\boldsymbol{\alpha}
according to

\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial\alpha_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda)=\begin{cases}-\frac{2y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}+\lambda)^{3}}=-2a_{ij}&\text{ if }j\neq i;\\
-\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{m_{0}}\frac{2y_{i\ell}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{\ell}+\lambda)^{3}}-\frac{2y_{ii}^{2}+2\sum_{\ell=m_{0}+1}^{n}y_{i\ell}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\lambda)^{3}}=-2\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{m_{0}}a_{i\ell}-2a_{ii},&\text{ if }j=i,\end{cases}

where we have set, for
i\neq j
,

a_{ij}=\frac{y_{ij}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}+\lambda)^{3}}~\text{ and }~a_{ii}=\frac{y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=m_{0}+1}^{n}y_{i\ell}^{2}}{(\alpha_{i}+\lambda)^{3}}.

Therefore, the Jacobian matrix has the form

\frac{\partial F}{\partial\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda\right)=-2\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{0}}a_{1j}&a_{12}&\ldots&a_{1m_{0}}\\
a_{21}&\sum_{j=1}^{m_{0}}a_{2j}&\ldots&a_{2m_{0}}\\
&\ldots&&\\
a_{m_{0}1}&a_{m_{0}2}&\ldots&\sum_{j=1}^{m_{0}}a_{2j}\end{array}\right).

(200)

Since the observed matrix satisfies
y_{ij}>0
for all
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
and
m_{0}<n
, we have
a_{ii}>0
holds almost surely for all
i\in[m_{0}]
.
It follows that the Jacobian matrix is strictly diagonally dominant, and thus
\partial F/\partial\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is almost surely invertible at all
(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda)
such that
\lambda>0
and
\alpha_{j}\geq 0
for
j\in[m_{0}]
.
By the Implicit Function Theorem, since
F(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0},\lambda_{0})=0
and the Jacobian is nonsingular at
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0},\lambda_{0})
, there exists
\delta>0
and a unique continuously differentiable function
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
on the interval
(\lambda_{0}-\delta,\lambda_{0}+\delta)
such that
F(\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda),\lambda)=0
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{0})=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}
.

To establish the monotonicity of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
, compute

\frac{d\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{d\lambda}=-\left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda),\lambda\right)\right)^{-1}\frac{\partial F}{\partial\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda),\lambda\right).

(201)

Recalling the definition of
F_{i}
from Equation (
199
), taking the partial derivative with respect to
\lambda
, we have

\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial\lambda}=\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial\alpha_{i}}=-2\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_{0}}a_{i\ell},\quad\text{for }i\in[m_{0}].

(202)

Let
\boldsymbol{D}=\operatorname{diag}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m_{0}}a_{1j},\sum_{j=1}^{m_{0}}a_{2j},\ldots,\sum_{j=1}^{m_{0}}a_{2j}\right)
and define the matrix
\boldsymbol{S}
with entries

S_{ij}=\begin{cases}\frac{a_{ij}}{\sum_{k=1}^{m_{0}}a_{ik}}&\text{ if }i\neq j;\\
0&\text{ if }i=j.\end{cases}

Then, by Equation (
200
) we have

\frac{\partial F}{\partial\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=-2\boldsymbol{D}\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{m_{0}}+\boldsymbol{S}\right).

Combining this with Equations (
201
) and (
202
), we have

\frac{d\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{d\lambda}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{I}_{m_{0}}+\boldsymbol{S})^{-1}\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\frac{\partial F}{\partial\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda),\lambda)=-(\boldsymbol{I}_{m_{0}}+\boldsymbol{S})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}}

Because
\boldsymbol{S}\geq 0
elementwise and has row sums less than
1
, its spectral radius is strictly less than
1
by Gershgorin circle theorem.
Thus, the matrix
\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{S}
is invertible and

\displaystyle\frac{d\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{d\lambda}=-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{S}^{2k}(\boldsymbol{I}_{m_{0}}-\mathbf{S})\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}}<0,

(203)

where the last inequality holds elementwise.
This shows that each
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)
is strictly decreasing in
\lambda
for
i\in E(\lambda_{0})
, completing the proof.
∎

The preceding lemma establishes that the group norm
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)
evolves continuously and strictly decreases as the regularization strength
\lambda
increases.
The following lemma quantifies the rate at which
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)
decreases with
\lambda
, providing both upper and lower bounds on the derivative
d\alpha_{i}/d\lambda
.

Lemma 25
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
be a solution to Equation (
198
) and let
m_{0}=|E(\lambda)|
as defined in Equation (
198
).
Under the same assumptions as in Lemma
24
, for all
i\in E(\lambda)
, the derivative of
\alpha_{i}
with respect to
\lambda
satisfies

\frac{\sum_{\ell\in E(\lambda)^{c}}y_{i\ell}^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}y_{i\ell}^{2}}\leq\left|\frac{d\alpha_{i}}{d\lambda}\right|\leq 1.

(204)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
E(\lambda)=[m_{0}]
and denote
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E}=(\alpha_{i})_{i\in[m_{0}]}
.
For notational convenience, we omit the subscript and simply write
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E}
throughout this proof.
From the expression derived in Equation (
203
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{d\lambda}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{S}^{2k}(\boldsymbol{I}_{m_{0}}-\boldsymbol{S})\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}}=\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}}-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{S}^{2k+1}(\boldsymbol{I}_{m_{0}}-\boldsymbol{S})\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}}.

Since
\boldsymbol{S}\geq 0
elementwise and has row sums strictly less than
1
,
the term subtracted on the right-hand side is nonnegative.
This immediately yields the upper bound:

\left|\frac{d\alpha_{i}}{d\lambda}\right|\leq 1\quad\text{for all }i\in[m_{0}].

To obtain the lower bound, observe that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{d\lambda}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}}-\boldsymbol{S}\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{S}^{2k+2}(\boldsymbol{I}_{m_{0}}-\boldsymbol{S})\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}}\geq\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}}-\boldsymbol{S}\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}},

again holding elementwise.
For each
i\in[m_{0}]
,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{S}\mathbf{1}_{m_{0}})_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{m_{0}}\frac{a_{i\ell}}{\sum_{j=1}^{m_{0}}a_{ij}}=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{m_{0}}a_{i\ell}}{a_{ii}+\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{m_{0}}a_{ij}}=1-\frac{a_{ii}}{\sum_{j=1}^{m_{0}}a_{ij}},

which implies

\left|\frac{d\alpha_{i}}{d\lambda}\right|\geq\frac{a_{ii}}{\sum_{j=1}^{m_{0}}a_{ij}}.

(205)

Plugging in the definition of
a
into the right-hand of Equation (
205
), where

a_{ii}=\frac{y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=m_{0}+1}^{n}y_{i\ell}^{2}}{\left(\alpha_{i}+\lambda\right)^{3}},\quad a_{i\ell}=\frac{y_{i\ell}^{2}}{\left(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{\ell}+\lambda\right)^{3}},

we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\alpha_{i}}{d\lambda}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\left(y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=m_{0}+1}^{n}y_{i\ell}^{2}\right)/\left(\alpha_{i}+\lambda\right)^{3}}{\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{m_{0}}y_{i\ell}^{2}/\left(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{\ell}+\lambda\right)^{3}+(y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=m_{0}+1}^{n}y_{i\ell}^{2})/\left(\alpha_{i}+\lambda\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=m_{0}+1}^{n}y_{i\ell}^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{m_{0}}\frac{\left(\alpha_{i}+\lambda\right)^{3}}{\left(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{\ell}+\lambda\right)^{3}}y_{i\ell}^{2}+y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=m_{0}+1}^{n}y_{ii\ell}^{2}}.

Observing that all coefficients multiplying
y_{i\ell}^{2}
in the denominator are bounded by
1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\alpha_{i}}{d\lambda}\right|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\geq\frac{y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=m_{0}+1}^{n}y_{i\ell}^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}y_{i\ell}^{2}},

which yields our desired lower bound.
∎

With Lemmas
24
and
25
, we have shown that when the active set
E(\lambda)
contains fewer than
n
indices, the group norm vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
evolves smoothly ad strictly decreases with respect to the regularization parameter
\lambda
.
A natural next step is to examine what happens when the active set saturates the entire index set, i.e., when
E(\lambda)=[n]
.
The following lemma establishes that the monotonicity of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
persists in this regime as well, and even admits a closed-form derivative when the diagonal entries of
\boldsymbol{Y}
vanish.

Lemma 26
.

Assume that
y_{ij}\neq 0
for
i,j\in[n],i\neq j
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{J})
be a solution to Equation (
197
) such that
J(\lambda_{J})=\emptyset
.
Then there exists a unique solution path
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, continuous on the interval
[0,\lambda_{J}]
, such that

\frac{d\alpha_{i}(\lambda)}{d\lambda}<0,\quad\text{ for all }i\in[n].

Moreover, if
y_{ii}=0
for all
i\in[n]
, then this derivative is constant and satisfies

\frac{d\alpha_{i}(\lambda)}{d\lambda}=-\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{ for all }i\in[n].

Proof.

We follow the setup of Lemmas
24
and
25
.
Define the matrix
\boldsymbol{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
by

S_{ij}=\begin{cases}\frac{a_{ij}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{ik}},&\mbox{ if }i\neq j,\\
0,&\mbox{ if }i=j\end{cases}\\

where

a_{ij}=\frac{y_{ij}^{2}}{\left(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}+\lambda_{J}\right)^{3}},\quad\text{ for }i\neq j,\quad a_{ii}=\frac{y_{ii}^{2}}{\left(\alpha_{i}+\lambda_{J}\right)^{3}}

Under the assumption that
y_{ij}\neq 0
for all
i\neq j
, we have
S_{ij}>0
for all off-diagonal entries.
If not all
y_{ii}=0
for
i\in[n]
, then there exists at least one row (say, the first) such that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}S_{1j}<1.

It follows that for any
k\in[n]
,

(\boldsymbol{S}^{2}\mathbf{1}_{n})_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}S_{kj}S_{ji}=S_{k1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}S_{1i}+\sum_{j\neq 1}S_{kj}\sum_{i=1}^{n}S_{ji}<S_{k1}+\sum_{j\neq 1}S_{kj}\leq 1,

which indicates that the row sums of
\boldsymbol{S}^{2}
are all strictly less than 1.
Hence, by Gershgorin circle theorem, all eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{S}^{2}
lie strictly within the unit circle.
Consequently,
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{S}^{k}=0
, and the Neumann series expansion of
(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{S})^{-1}
is valid.
By the same argument as in Lemma
24
, this implies that for some
\delta>0
, the solution path
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
is well-defined and differentiable on the interval
(\lambda_{J}-\delta,\lambda_{J}]
, and satisfies

-1\leq\frac{d\alpha_{i}(\lambda)}{d\lambda}<0,\quad\text{ for all }i\in[n].

Because
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
is continuous and its derivative is uniformly bounded, we may take the right-handed limit

\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{J}-\delta)=\lim_{\lambda\to(\lambda_{J}-\delta)^{+}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda),

which exists and satisfies Equation (
198
) by continuity.
Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{J}-\delta)
is also a solution to Equation (
198
) with
\lambda=\lambda_{J}-\delta
.
Moreover,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{J}-\delta)
is the unique extension of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{J})
to the interval
[\lambda_{J}-\delta,\lambda_{J}]
by the implicit function theorem.
By repeating this argument, we can uniquely extend
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
to the entire interval
[0,\lambda_{J}]
.

For the special case where
y_{ii}=0
for all
i
, the diagonal entries
a_{ii}=0
, and
\boldsymbol{S}
becomes row-stochastic with strictly positive off-diagonal entries.
Therefore,
\boldsymbol{S}^{2}>0
and
\boldsymbol{I}_{n}+\boldsymbol{S}
is invertible by Lemma
40
.
Applying Lemma
41
yields

\frac{d\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{d\lambda}=-(\boldsymbol{I}_{n}+\boldsymbol{S})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{n}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{n},

completing the proof.
∎

E.3
Global Extension of the Solution Path

Building on the local continuity and monotonicity results established in Lemmas
24
through
26
, we now turn to the global behavior of the group lasso solution path.
The preceding lemmas guarantee that for any set of active indices
E(\lambda_{0})\subseteq[n]
, the corresponding solution vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E(\lambda_{0})}(\lambda)
evolves continuously and strictly decreases as
\lambda
decreases, at least within a neighborhood of any given
\lambda_{0}
.
Moreover, Lemma
26
ensures that this solution path remains well-defined even when all indices are active.

We now show that starting from any valid solution
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{0})
, this local behavior can be extended inductively to yield a continuous and elementwise decreasing solution path
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\geq 0
.
This result provides a global structural characterization of the overlapping group lasso estimator and underlies its interpretation as a greedy algorithm.

Lemma 27
.

Assume that
y_{ij}\neq 0
for
i,j\in[n],i\neq j
.
Given a solution
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{0})
to the group lasso at
\lambda=\lambda_{0}>0
,
there exists a continuous,
elementwise decreasing function
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
defined for all
\lambda\geq 0
,
such that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
solves the group lasso problem for each
\lambda
.

Proof.

First, observe that for any
j\in[n]
,
the left-hand side of the KKT inequality condition in Equation (
195
) is upper bounded by
\|\boldsymbol{y}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
.
Thus, for
\lambda>\lambda_{\max}:=\max_{j\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{j}\|_{2}
, the inequality cannot be satisfied with
\alpha_{j}>0
,
and hence the only solution is the all-zero vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)=0
.
Therefore, we only need to construct a valid solution path over the compact interval
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{\max}]
.

Fix
\lambda_{0}\in(0,\lambda_{\max}]
and assume we are given a solution
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{0})
.
We aim to extend this solution path continuously and monotonically to the left over the interval
[0,\lambda_{0}]
.
Recall that we partition
[n]
into
E_{1}(\lambda)
,
E_{2}(\lambda)
and
J(\lambda)
according to Equation (
197
).
We call the indices
E(\lambda_{0})=E_{1}(\lambda_{0})\cup E_{2}(\lambda_{0})
the set of
active groups
at
\lambda_{0}
.
We let
J(\lambda_{0})
be the complement of
E(\lambda_{0})
.
We distinguish two cases based on whether
J(\lambda_{0})
is empty.

If
J(\lambda_{0})=\emptyset
, then all groups are already active, and the entire index set participates in the solution.
In this case, we invoke Lemma
26
to construct the full solution path on
[0,\lambda_{0}]
.
Otherwise, as we decrease
\lambda
from
\lambda_{0}
, eventually some inequality constraints in Equation (
195
) become active, and new group of coordinates may enter the support.
Let
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{0}
be the first such value where this occurs.
We refer to
\lambda_{1}
as a
breaking point
.

We now proceed to extend the solution from
\lambda_{0}
to
\lambda_{1}
so that the solution path is continuous over the interval
[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{0}]
.
We consider the solution
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E(\lambda_{0})}(\lambda)
defined on the active set
E(\lambda_{0})
.
By Lemma
24
, there exists a neighborhood of
\lambda_{0}
over which
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E(\lambda_{0})}(\lambda)
is unique, continuous, and strictly decreasing.
Moreover, Lemma
25
ensures that this solution path has bounded derivatives, so the limit

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E(\lambda_{0})}(\lambda_{0}-\delta):=\lim_{\lambda\to(\lambda_{0}-\delta)^{+}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E(\lambda_{0})}(\lambda)

exists and satisfies the partition KKT conditions at
\lambda=\lambda_{0}-\delta
.
Thus, the solution can be further extended.
By repeating this argument, we can continue to extend
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E(\lambda_{0})}(\lambda)
leftward until reaching
\lambda_{1}
, the first point at which a new group of coordinates is activated.
By setting
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{E(\lambda_{0})^{c}}(\lambda)=0
for
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{0}]
, we obtain a complete solution
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
over
[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{0}]
that is continuous and decreasing in each coordinate.

Now, we aim to iterate this process to continue the solution path to values smaller than
\lambda_{1}
.
At
\lambda=\lambda_{1}
, new group become active, corresponding to
E_{2}(\lambda_{1})\neq\emptyset
.
We now recursively apply the argument just given above with
\lambda_{1}
in place of
\lambda_{0}
.
Each such application of this argument adds new group into the active set, and we continue until eventually
J(\lambda)=\emptyset
.

Once
J(\lambda)=\emptyset
, the entire index set participates in the solution.
In this regime, Lemma
26
guarantees the existence of a globally defined, strictly decreasing solution path
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
over
[0,\lambda]
.
This completes the extension over the entire interval
[0,\lambda_{0}]
.
∎

To summarize, we have shown that for the overlapping group lasso estimator, the solution path
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
is continuous and strictly decreasing in
\lambda
, starting from the maximal regularization level
\lambda_{\max}:=\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2}
.
At this level, the unique solution is
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda_{\max})=\mathbf{0}
, and Lemma
27
guarantees that this solution can be extended to all
\lambda\geq 0
as a continuous and elementwise decreasing function.

For theoretical purposes, we focus on this canonical solution path initialized at
\lambda_{\max}
and interpret group lasso as a selection procedure: once a group becomes active at some
\lambda=\bar{\lambda}
, it remains active for all smaller values of
\lambda
.
This viewpoint allows us to formalize support recovery guarantees based on the structure of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
.

E.4
Empirical Results of the Solution Path

To validate the theoretical results developed in Section
E
, we conduct a simple simulation study illustrating the behavior of the group lasso solution path as a function of the regularization parameter
\lambda
.
We fix
n=500
and generate a symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}\in\operatorname{Sym}(n)
according to the additive model
\boldsymbol{Y}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}
, where
\boldsymbol{W}
is a symmetric noise matrix with i.i.d. entries from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
on and above the diagonal.
The signal matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
is constructed as
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}+{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}^{\top}
, where
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}
contains
m=5
randomly selected nonzero rows, each with entries independently drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{B}^{2})
and
\sigma_{B}=1.9\,n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
.
Let
{I_{\star}}
denote the indices of the nonzero rows of
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}
.
By construction,
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}
is a node-sparse symmetric matrix, and with high probability, the row norms corresponding to indices in
{I_{\star}}
are of order
n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n
, matching the minimax signal strength threshold (see Remark
5
).

We compute the group lasso estimator over a grid of
T=60
regularization parameters
\lambda
spanning the interval

\lambda_{\min}:=0.85\cdot\min_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2},\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{\max}:=\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2},

where
\boldsymbol{y}_{i}
denotes the
i
-th row of
\boldsymbol{Y}
.
For each value of
\lambda
, we solve the optimization problem in Equation (
38
) using the ADMM algorithm described in Algorithm
5
.
We then compute
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)
for each
i\in[n]
, where
\alpha_{i}
is defined as the
\ell_{2}
norm of the
i
-th row of the estimated matrix
\boldsymbol{V}(\lambda)
(see Equation (
193
)).
This yields the full solution path
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
, which characterizes how the magnitude and support of the estimated rows evolve with the penalty parameter
\lambda
.

Figure
10
displays the resulting solution paths.
Each curve represents
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)
for a fixed row
i\in[n]
.
Orange curves correspond to signal nodes (
i\in I^{\star}
), while blue curves correspond to non-signal nodes (
i\notin I^{\star}
).
For visual reference, we include two guide lines: a dashed red line with slope
-1
and a dotted red line with slope
-\frac{1}{2}
.
The
x
-axis represents the regularization parameter
\lambda
, while the
y
-axis represents the
\ell_{2}
norm
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)
of the estimated rows
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}
.
The empirical findings are in strong agreement with our theoretical analysis:

1.

The solution paths are continuous and strictly decreasing in
\lambda
, as evidenced by the monotonic decay of all curves.
This behavior reflects the selection mechanism of the group lasso estimator: once a group becomes active at some threshold
\lambda=\bar{\lambda}
, it remains active for all smaller values of
\lambda
along a solution path.
This observation is consistent with the global monotonicity result established in Lemma
27
.

2.

For small values of
\lambda
, the solution paths tend to follow a slope of
-\frac{1}{2}
, closely tracking the dotted reference line in Figure
10
.
This matches the theoretical prediction in Lemma
26
, which shows that when all groups are active and the diagonal entries vanish, the group norms decay linearly with slope
-\frac{1}{2}
.
For large values of
\lambda
, the paths approximately follow a slope of
-1
, as indicated by the dashed line, which agrees with Lemma
25
.
This is expected since, with high probability, the left-hand side of Equation (
204
) concentrates near one.

3.

Finally, we observe that the curves corresponding to rows in the true support set
{I_{\star}}
are the first to become active as
\lambda
decreases.
This supports the conclusion that, when the signal strength is sufficiently large, the group lasso estimator can correctly recover the ground truth node support
{I_{\star}}
.
In particular, the empirical behavior suggests that recovery is achievable at the minimax signal strength threshold, in line with the sufficient conditions established in Theorem
12
.
We provide a detailed analysis of support recovery guarantees in Section
F
.

In summary, these findings provide compelling empirical support for the theoretical results derived in Section
E
, and set the stage for our subsequent investigation into the theoretical guarantees for support recovery in Section
F
.

Figure 10
:
Solution path of the overlapping group lasso estimator. Each curve corresponds to a group of coordinates.
The
y
-axis shows the value of each
\alpha_{i}
while the
x
-axis shows the value of the regularization parameter
\lambda
.
Orange curves correspond to signal nodes (
i\in I^{\star}
), while blue curves correspond to non-signal nodes (
i\notin I^{\star}
).
The dashed red line has a slope of
-1
and the dotted red line has a slope of
-\frac{1}{2}
.

Appendix F
Group Lasso Support Recovery

With the solution path results established in Section
E
, we now turn to establishing conditions under which the group Lasso correctly recovers the support of the underlying signal.
Throughout this section, we retain the notation introduced earlier in Section
E
and,
without loss of generality, assume the true support is given by
{I_{\star}}=[m]
.
We consider the case of applying the group Lasso estimator to a single observed matrix

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)},

(206)

where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
is the output from Step
4
in Algorithm
1
.
We will write
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}=(y_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
, where
y_{ij}
is the
(i,j)
-th entry of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
, to keep the notation simple and consistent with Section
E
.
As before, we assume without loss of generality that the selected index set is
\mathcal{U}=[n]
as discussed at the beginning of Section
4
for a discussion on this.

Our goal is to prove the existence of a regularization parameter
\lambda\geq 0
such that the corresponding group Lasso solution
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\lambda)
satisfies the following:

•

Exact support recovery.

\alpha_{i}>0
for all
i\in[m]

•

KKT equalities for active groups.
For all
i\in[m]
,

\sum_{k\in[m],k\neq i}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}y_{ik}^{2}+\left(\frac{\lambda}{\alpha_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}\left(y_{ii}^{2}+\sum_{k\in[m]^{c}}y_{ik}^{2}\right)=\lambda^{2}.

(207)

•

KKT strict inequalities for inactive groups.

For all
\ell\in[m]^{c}
,

\sum_{k\in[m]}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\alpha_{k}+\lambda}\right)^{2}y_{\ell k}^{2}+\sum_{k\in[m]^{c}}y_{\ell k}^{2}<\lambda^{2}.

(208)

Establishing these conditions will allow us to guarantee that the support recovered by the group Lasso estimator exactly matches the true support of the underlying signal.

We start with a useful upper bound on
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)
.
By Lemma
25
, the group norm satisfies the derivative bound

\frac{d\alpha_{i}}{d\lambda}\geq-1,

For
i\in[n]
, let
\bar{\lambda}_{i}
denote the largest value of
\lambda
such that the
i
-th group is active, i.e.,

\alpha_{i}(\lambda)>0\;\text{ for all }\lambda<\bar{\lambda}_{i},\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha_{i}(\bar{\lambda}_{i})=0,\;\text{ for all }\lambda\geq\bar{\lambda}_{i}.

Then, for any
0\leq\lambda<\bar{\lambda}_{i}
, we have

\alpha_{i}(\lambda)=-\int_{\lambda}^{\bar{\lambda}_{i}}\frac{d\alpha_{i}(u)}{d\lambda}du\leq\bar{\lambda}_{i}-\lambda.

(209)

To establish the existence of
(\alpha_{i})_{i\in[n]}
satisfying the KKT conditions for support recovery, we analyze a simplified setting.
Instead of directly solving the group Lasso problem on the full matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
as given in Equation (
206
), we consider an auxiliary problem with group Lasso applied to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, where

\widetilde{Y}_{{I_{\star}},ij}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{Y}_{ij},&\text{ if }i\in{I_{\star}},j\in[n]\text{ or }i\in[n],j\in{I_{\star}},\\
0,&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

The following lemma provides a sufficient condition under which the group Lasso solution applied to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}
selects all and only selects the first
m
groups.
The proof is provided in Section
F.2.1
.

Lemma 28
.

Suppose that
n>3m
and
\kappa^{2}\sqrt{\tilde{\mu}r}=O(n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n)
and assume without loss of generality that
{I_{\star}}=[m]
.
If

\min_{i\in[m]}\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}B_{ik}^{\star 2}+\frac{1}{6}n\sigma^{2}\geq\frac{n^{1/4}+1}{n^{1/4}-1}\max_{\ell\in[m]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{\ell k}^{\star 2},

(210)

then for sufficiently large
n
,
there exists
\lambda>0
such that the group Lasso applied to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}
selects exactly the first
m
groups with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.
That is, there exists a solution
(\alpha_{1}(\lambda),\alpha_{2}(\lambda),\ldots,\alpha_{n}(\lambda))
such that satisfies the KKT equalities in Equation (
207
) for all
i\in[m]
and the KKT inequalities in Equation (
208
) for all
\ell\in[m]^{c}
when applied to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}
.

Let
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}
denote the largest regularization parameter for which all of the first
m
groups are selected by the group Lasso applied to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}
.
In other words,
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}
is the largest value of
\lambda
such that equation (
198
) holds and
[m]\subseteq E(\lambda)
when applied to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}
.
More generally, for each
k\in[m]
, we define
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(k)}
as the largest value of
\lambda
such that at least
k
groups from the true support
[m]
are selected, and the newly selected group is
\pi(k)
.
Here, the index function
\pi:[m]\to[m]
records the order in which the true support groups enter the active set along the solution path:

\pi(k)
identifies the group that is selected when
\lambda
decreases to
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(k)}
.
To better understand this entry sequence, we next establish that the largest and smallest regularization levels at which groups are selected,
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(1)}
and
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}
, are close to one another.

Lemma 29
.

Suppose
\kappa^{2}\sqrt{\tilde{\mu}r}=o(n^{1/4}\log^{-1/4}n)
and
m=\Omega(\log n)
.
Further, assume that

\min_{i\in[m]}\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|^{2}_{2}\asymp\max_{i\in[m]}\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|^{2}_{2}=\Theta(\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}).

(211)

Then with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, we have

\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(1)}}\right)^{2}\geq 1-C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}.

The proof is provided in Section
F.2.2
.

F.1
Proof of Theorem
12

By Lemma
28
, we have constructed a candidate solution
(\alpha_{1}(\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}),\alpha_{2}(\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}),\dots,\alpha_{m}(\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}))
that satisfies the KKT equalities in Equation (
207
) for the group Lasso applied to the restricted matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}
, we now seek to verify that this solution also satisfies the KKT inequality conditions in Equation (
208
) when extended to the full matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
.
Our approach mirrors the primal-dual witness (PDW) framework, a widely used technique for establishing exact support recovery in structured sparsity problems.
For example, see Chapter 7 of
Wainwright, 2019
for details of the PDW framework in the context of sparse linear regression.
In short, we show that if the KKT inequalities are satisfied, then the group Lasso solution on
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
correctly recovers the support
{I_{\star}}
.
We formalize this result in Theorem
12
, whose proof follows below.

Proof of Theorem
12
.

Without loss of generality, assume that the true support is
{I_{\star}}=[m]
.
Under the conditions of Theorem
12
, one can verify that the sufficient condition in Equation (
210
) holds.
Let
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}
denote the largest regularization parameter such that the group Lasso applied to the restricted matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}
selects all
m
groups as defined above.
By Lemma
28
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, the solution at
\lambda=\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}
selects only the groups in
[m]
when applied to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}
.
Define the event

\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\left\{\text{Group Lasso with }\lambda=\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}\text{ selects exactly the first }m\text{ groups}\right\}.

Since
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}
is the largest value of
\lambda
for which all
m
groups are selected, the KKT condition in Equation (
207
) implies that for all
i\in[m]
,

\sum_{k\in[m]}\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}{\alpha_{k}(\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)})+\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}\right)^{2}y_{ik}^{2}+\sum_{k\in[m]^{c}}y_{ik}^{2}\geq\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}^{2}.

In particular, by definition of
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}
, equality holds for
i=\pi(m)\in[m]
.
Thus, to ensure exact support recovery for
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
, it suffices to show that for all
\ell\in[m]^{c}
and
i\in[m]
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[m]^{c}}(y_{ik}^{2}-y_{\ell k}^{2})

\displaystyle>\sum_{k\in[m]}\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}{\alpha_{k}(\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)})+\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}\right)^{2}\left(y^{2}_{\ell k}-y^{2}_{ik}\right),

(212)

so that the KKT conditions in Equations (
207
) and (
208
) still hold for the group Lasso applied to the full matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
.

By Equation (
209
), we know for all
k\in[m]
,

\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}{\alpha_{k}(\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)})+\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}\right)^{2}\geq\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(1)}}\right)^{2}.

Lemma
29
ensures that with high probability,

\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(1)}}\right)^{2}\geq 1-Cn^{-1/2}\log^{1/2}n.

Define the event

\mathcal{E}_{2}:=\left\{\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(1)}}\right)^{2}\geq 1-Cn^{-1/2}\log^{1/2}n\right\}.

Fix
a=Cn^{-1/2}\log^{1/2}n
and define
a_{k}\in[1-2a,1]
for
k\in[m]
.
To upper bound the right-hand side of Equation (
212
), we consider the term

\displaystyle\sup_{a_{k}\in[1-2a,1],k\in[m]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}(y^{2}_{\ell k}-y^{2}_{ik})

(213)

and upper bound it by decomposition into contributions from noise, signal-noise interaction, and signal as

\displaystyle\sup_{a_{k}\in[1-2a,1],k\in[m]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}(\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{\ell k}\right)^{2}-\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{ik}\right)^{2}),

(214)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sup_{a_{k}\in[1-2a,1],k\in[m]}2\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\left[(B^{\star}_{\ell k}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{\ell k})\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{\ell k}-(B^{\star}_{ik}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{ik})\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{ik}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sup_{a_{k}\in[1-2a,1],k\in[m]}\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}a_{k}\left[(B^{\star}_{\ell k}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{\ell k})^{2}-(B^{\star}_{ik}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{ik})^{2}\right]=:\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}.

By Lemma
30
, each of these three terms can be controlled with high probability.
Specifically, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, for all
i\in[m]
and
\ell\in[m]^{c}
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}=\sup_{a_{k}\in[1-2a,1],k\in[m]}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\left(\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{\ell k}\right)^{2}-\left(\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{ik}\right)^{2}\right)

(215)

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma^{2}mn^{-1/2}\log^{1/2}n+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{m\log n}\leq C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{m\log n}.

and

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}:=\sup_{a_{k}\in[1-2a,1],k\in[m]}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\left[(B^{\star}_{\ell k}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{\ell k})\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{\ell k}-(B^{\star}_{ik}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{ik})\widetilde{W}^{(1)}_{ik}\right]

(216)

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\sqrt{\log n}\left[\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{\ell k}\right)^{1/2}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{ik}\right)^{1/2}+\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\|_{2,\infty}\right].

For
\gamma_{3}
, expanding the squares yields that

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}

\displaystyle a_{k}\left[\left(B^{\star}_{\ell k}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{\ell k}\right)^{2}-\left(B^{\star}_{ik}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{ik}\right)^{2}\right]

(217)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{\ell k}^{\star 2}-(1-2a)\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{ik}^{\star 2}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\widetilde{\Delta}_{\ell k}^{2}+2\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\|_{2,\infty}\left[\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{\ell k}\right)^{1/2}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{ik}\right)^{1/2}\right].

Applying Theorem
2
to Equation (
217
) and combining the upper bounds in Equations (
215
) (
216
) and (
217
) with Equation (
214
), we conclude that the total expression in Equation (
213
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sup_{a_{k}\in[1-2a,1],k\in[m]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}(y^{2}_{\ell k}-y^{2}_{ik})\leq

(218)

\displaystyle C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left[\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{\ell k}\right)^{1/2}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{ik}\right)^{1/2}\right]+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(B_{\ell k}^{\star 2}-(1-2a)B_{ik}^{\star 2}\right)+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.

Meanwhile, for the left-hand side of Equation (
212
), Lemma
31
ensures that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, it holds for all
\ell\in[m]^{c}
and all
i\in[m]
that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[m]^{c}}(y_{ik}^{2}-y_{\ell k}^{2})\geq\sum_{k\in[m]^{c}}B_{ik}^{\star 2}-C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left(\sum_{k\in[m]^{c}}B_{ik}^{\star 2}\right)^{1/2}-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}.

(219)

Define the event

\mathcal{E}_{3}:=\left\{\forall i\in[m],\forall\ell\in[m]^{c}:\sum_{k\in[m]^{c}}(y_{ik}^{2}-y_{\ell k}^{2})\geq\sup_{a_{k}\in[1-2a,1],k\in[m]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\left(y_{\ell k}^{2}-y_{ik}^{2}\right)\right\}.

When there exists a sufficiently large
C
such that

\displaystyle\min_{i\in[m]}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}B_{ik}^{\star 2}-C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}B_{ik}^{\star 2}\right)^{1/2}\right)

(220)

\displaystyle\geq\max_{\ell\in[m]^{c}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{\ell k}^{\star 2}+C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{\ell k}\right)^{1/2}\right)+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n},

we have that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{3})\geq 1-O(n^{-6})

by Equations (
218
) and (
219
).
Under the assumptions of Theorem
12
in Equation (
40
), one can verify that condition (
220
) is satisfied for large enough
C
.
Therefore, combining with events
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we conclude that

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{3}^{c}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{3}^{c})\leq O(n^{-6}),

which establishes that with high probability, the group Lasso estimator recovers the correct support, completing the proof.
∎

F.2
Technical Lemmas for Group Lasso Support Recovery

Recall the following definition of the sub-exponential norm (see Definition 2.8.4 in
Vershynin, 2018
).

Definition 3

(Sub-exponential Orlicz norm)
.

For a real-valued random variable
X
, the Orlicz
\psi_{1}
norm is defined as

\|X\|_{\psi_{1}}:=\inf\left\{t>0:\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{|X|}{t}\right)\right]\leq 2\right\}

This norm quantifies the tail decay of
X
.
A finite
\psi_{1}
-norm implies that
X
is sub-exponential, i.e., it satisfies the tail bound:

\mathbb{P}\left[|X|>t\right]\leq 2\exp\left\{-c\frac{t}{\|X\|_{\psi_{1}}}\right\}\quad\text{ for all }t>0

where
c>0
is an absolute constant.
We will use this definition in the following lemma, which provides a bound on the sum of independent sub-exponential random variables.

Lemma 30
.

Let
\left\{X_{k}\right\}_{k=1}^{m}
be independent mean-zero sub-exponential random variables,
and suppose
a_{k}\in[1-2a,1]
for some
a\in(0,1/2)
and all
k\in[m]
.
Assume
\|X_{k}\|_{\psi_{1}}=C_{k}
for
k\in[m]
.
Then for some constant
c>0
we have

\sup_{a_{l}\in[1-2a,1],l\in[m]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}X_{k}\leq ca\left(\sqrt{\log n}+m^{1/2}\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}C_{k}^{2}\right)^{1/2}+c\sqrt{\log n}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}C_{k}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-7})
.

Proof.

Decompose the sum as

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}X_{k}=\underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{m}(a_{k}-1+a)X_{k}}_{A_{1}}+\underbrace{(1-a)\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{k}}_{A_{2}}.

(221)

To bound
A_{1}
, we note that
a_{k}+a-1\in[-a,a]
.
Hence,

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{a_{k}\in[1-2a,1]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}(a_{k}-1+a)X_{k}\leq a\sum_{k=1}^{m}|X_{k}|=\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\{\pm 1\}^{m}}a\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{k}u_{k}.

Applying a union bound over the
2^{m}
sign vectors and using Bernstein’s inequality for sub-exponential variables
(
Vershynin, 2018
, see Theorem 2.9.1 in)
), we have that for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[A_{1}>at\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m}\exp\left(-c\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{m}C_{k}^{2}},\frac{t}{\max_{k\in[m]}C_{k}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-c\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{m}C_{k}^{2}},\frac{t}{\max_{k\in[m]}C_{k}}\right\}+m\right).

Setting
t=7c^{-1}\log^{1/2}n\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{m}C_{k}^{2}}+c^{-1}\sqrt{m\sum_{k=1}^{m}C_{k}^{2}}
yields that

A_{1}\leq ac^{-1}(7\sqrt{\log n}+c^{-1}m^{1/2})\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}C_{k}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

(222)

with probability
1-O(n^{-7})
.

The term
A_{2}
can be bounded via applying Bernstein’s inequality to
\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{k}
, and it follows that

A_{2}\leq 7c^{-1}\sqrt{\log n}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}C_{k}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

(223)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-7})
.

Applying Equations (
222
) and (
223
) to Equation (
221
) completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 31
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
12
holds.
Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
be the matrix defined in Equation (
206
) and for each
i\in[n]
, let
\boldsymbol{y}_{i}
be the
i
-th row of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
.
Then, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, for all
i\in[n]
and
\ell\in[m]^{c}
, it holds that
With probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, for some sufficiently large constant
C>0
, it holds for all suitably large
n
that for all
i\in[n]
,

\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}+n\sigma^{2}-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}-C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2},

(224)

and

\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}+n\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}+C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}.

(225)

Further, if
m=\Omega(\log n)
, it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
that for all
i\in[n]
,

\sum_{j=1}^{m}y_{ij}^{2}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{ij}+m\sigma^{2}-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}-C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{ij}\right)^{1/2},

(226)

and

\sum_{j=1}^{m}y_{ij}^{2}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{ij}+m\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}+C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}B^{\star 2}_{ij}\right)^{1/2}.

(227)

Proof.

We only present the proof for Equation (
224
), as the other bouds follow by similar arguments.
Straightforward calculation yields that for
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}-2\left|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{i,\cdot}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right|-2\left|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}^{(1)\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{i,\cdot}\right|-2\left|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}^{(1)\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right|

(228)

\displaystyle\geq\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}-2\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}-2\left|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}^{(1)\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{i,\cdot}\right|-2\left|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}^{(1)\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right|,

where the inequality follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
Conditioned on
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
, we apply Gaussian tail bounds to control
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}^{(1)\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{i,\cdot}
, from which

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}^{(1)\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{i,\cdot}\right|\geq t\right]\leq 2\exp\left\{-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}^{2}}\right\}.

Taking
t=C\sigma\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\|_{2,\infty}\sqrt{\log n}
for
C
sufficiently large, it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-7})
that

\left|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}^{(1)\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{i,\cdot}\right|\leq C\sigma\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\|_{2,\infty}\sqrt{\log n}.

(229)

Similarly, also with probability at least
1-O(n^{-7})
,

\left|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}^{(1)\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right|\leq C\sigma\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log n}.

(230)

Applying Equations (
229
) and (
230
) to Equation (
228
) along with Lemma
37
to control the squared
\ell_{2}
norm of rows of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)}
,
it follows that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}+n\sigma^{2}-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}-C\sigma\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\|_{2,\infty}\sqrt{\log n}-(C\sigma\sqrt{\log n}+\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\|_{2,\infty})\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}.

(231)

Applying a union bound over
i\in[n]
yields that Equation (
231
) holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
for all
i\in[n]
, conditioned on
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
.
Conditioned on the event
\{\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|_{2,\infty}\leq C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\}
, we have that

\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}+n\sigma^{2}-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}-C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}

from Equation (
231
).
By Theorem
2
and the assumption that
\kappa^{2}\sqrt{\tilde{\mu}r}=o(n^{1/4}\log^{-1/4}n)
, we have that

\{\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|_{2,\infty}\leq C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\}
holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
for some
C
sufficiently large.
We then conclude that for all
i\in[n]
, Equation (
224
)
holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.
∎

F.2.1
Proof of Lemma
28

Proof.

Write
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}=(y_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}
, where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
is given in Equation (
206
) and consider the condition

\min_{i\in[m]}\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}y_{ik}^{2}>\max_{\ell\in[m]^{c}}\sum_{k=1}^{m}y_{\ell k}^{2}.

(232)

If this condition holds, then we can choose
\tilde{\lambda}
such that

\tilde{\lambda}^{2}\in\left(\max_{\ell\in[m]^{c}}\sum_{k=1}^{m}y_{\ell k}^{2},\min_{i\in[m]}\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}y_{ik}^{2}\right).

With such a choice, the KKT conditions in Equation (
208
) imply

\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\tilde{\lambda}}{\alpha_{k}+\tilde{\lambda}}\right)^{2}y_{ik}^{2}<\tilde{\lambda}^{2},\quad\text{ if }i\in[m]^{c}

and

\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\tilde{\lambda}}{\alpha_{k}+\tilde{\lambda}}\right)^{2}y_{ik}^{2}+\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}y_{ik}^{2}>\tilde{\lambda}^{2},\quad\text{ if }i\in[m].

Hence, with regularization parameter
\lambda=\tilde{\lambda}
, group Lasso will recover exactly the first
m
groups provided that Equation (
232
) holds.

To ensure that Equation (
232
) holds with high probability, we apply the bounds from Lemma
31
.
Specifically, using Equation (
224
) and (
227
), the left hand side of Equation (
232
) is lower bounded with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
by

\displaystyle\min_{i\in[m]}\left(\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}B_{ik}^{\star 2}-C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left(\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}B_{ik}^{\star 2}\right)^{1/2}\right)+(n-m)\sigma^{2}-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}

\displaystyle\geq(1-n^{-1/4})\min_{i\in[m]}\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}B_{ik}^{\star 2}+(n-m)\sigma^{2}-C\sigma^{2}n^{3/4}\log^{1/2}n,

where the inequality holds from the fact that
2ab<n^{-1/4}a^{2}+n^{1/4}b^{2}
.

Using Equations (
225
) and (
226
), the right hand side of Equation (
232
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\max_{\ell\in[m]}

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{\ell k}^{\star 2}+C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{\ell k}^{\star 2}\right)^{1/2}\right)+m\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}

\displaystyle\leq(1+n^{-1/4})\max_{\ell\in[m]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{\ell k}^{\star 2}+m\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}n^{3/4}\log^{1/2}n

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, where the last inequality follows similar from the fact that
2ab<n^{-1/4}a^{2}+n^{1/4}b^{2}
.

Therefore, under condition (
210
) and for sufficiently large
n
, Equation (
232
) holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, completing the proof.
∎

F.2.2
Proof of Lemma
29

Proof.

Under the given assumptions, the assumptions in Lemma
28
hold.
Thus, with high probability, applying group Lasso to
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{{I_{\star}}}
with
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}
only selects the first
m
groups.
By the KKT equality condition in Equation (
207
), this implies that for
\pi(m)\in[m]
,

\sum_{k\in[m]}\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}{\alpha_{k}(\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)})+\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}}\right)^{2}y_{\pi(m)k}^{2}+\sum_{k\in[m]^{c}}y_{\pi(m)k}^{2}=\bar{\lambda}_{\pi(m)}^{2}.

In other words,
\pi(m)
corresponds to the last group in
[m]
to be selected as
\lambda
decreases.
Without loss of generality, we relabel so that
\pi(m)=m
and
\pi(1)=1
.

We now use the upper bound on
\alpha_{k}(\lambda)
from Equation (
209
) and note that since
\bar{\lambda}_{k}
is the largest regularization parameter at which the
k
-th group is selected, we have
\bar{\lambda}_{k}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{1}
for all
k\in[m]
.
Plugging this into the KKT inequality condition (
208
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{m}}{\bar{\lambda}_{k}}\right)^{2}y_{mk}^{2}+\sum_{k=m}^{n}y_{mk}^{2}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{m}^{2}

Using the bound
\bar{\lambda}_{k}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{1}
, this implies

\sum_{k=m}^{n}y_{mk}^{2}\leq\left(1-\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\frac{y_{mk}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{2}}\right)\bar{\lambda}_{m}^{2},

which futther implies that

\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{m}}{\bar{\lambda}_{1}}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{\sum_{k=m}^{n}y_{mk}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}^{2}_{1}-\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}y_{kj}^{2}}=\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{m}}{\bar{\lambda}_{1}}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{\sum_{k=m}^{n}y_{kj}^{2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}y_{1k}^{2}-\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}y_{mk}^{2}}

(233)

where in the last equality, we use the assumption that the first selected group is
\pi(1)=1
so that
\bar{\lambda}_{1}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}y_{1k}^{2}
.
To further bound this ratio from below, we invoke Equations (
224
), (
225
), and (
226
) from Lemma
31
to ensure that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{k=m}^{n}y_{kj}^{2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}y_{1k}^{2}-\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}y_{mk}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(n-m)\sigma^{2}-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}+\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{m,\cdot}\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}B_{mk}^{\star 2}-\delta_{m}}{(n-m)\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}+\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{1,\cdot}\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}B_{mk}^{\star 2}+\delta_{1}}

(234)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}}{(n-m)\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}}

where we define the deviation terms

\delta_{1}=C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left(\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{1,\cdot}\|_{2}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}B_{mk}^{\star 2}\right)^{1/2}\right)\asymp C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}

and

\delta_{m}=C\sigma n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n\left(\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{m,\cdot}\|_{2}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}B_{mk}^{\star 2}\right)^{1/2}\right)\asymp C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}.

The rates of
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{m}
follow from Equation (
211
)
Substituting the lower bound in Equation (
234
) into the right hand side of Equation (
233
) yields

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{m}}{\bar{\lambda}_{1}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}}{(n-m)\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}}.

Applying the fact that
m=o(n)
and
\sigma^{2}>0
is fixed,

\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{m}}{\bar{\lambda}_{1}}\right)^{2}\geq 1-C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}},

completing the proof.
∎

F.3
Proof of Theorem
13

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{m}
denote the hard-thresholding operator that selects the
m
rows (i.e., groups) of a symmetric matrix with the largest
\ell_{2}
norms.
Formally, for any matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\operatorname{Sym}(n)
, let
S\subseteq[n]
be the index set of the
m
largest values of
\|\boldsymbol{A}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}
for
i\in[n]
.
Then,

[\mathcal{H}_{m}(\boldsymbol{A})]_{ij}:=\begin{cases}A_{ij},&\text{ if }i\in S\text{ or }j\in S\\
0,&\text{ otherwise }.\end{cases}

(235)

The hard-thresholding estimator arises from applying the hard-thresholding operator, defined in Equation (
235
), to the entries of the matrix

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(1)},

where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
is the output from Step
4
in Algorithm
1
.
As before, we assume without loss of generality that the selected index set is
\mathcal{U}=[n]
and
{I_{\star}}=[m]
.
Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}=(y_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
.

The hard-thresholding estimator selects the
m
rows of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
with the largest row-wise
\ell_{2}
norms.
Therefore, to show it recovers the node support
{I_{\star}}
, it suffices to show that with high probability,

\min_{i\in[m]}\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{ij}^{2}>\max_{\ell\in[m]^{c}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{\ell j}^{2}.

By Equation (
224
) in Lemma
31
, we have that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{ij}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}+n\sigma^{2}-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}-C\sigma\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}\cdot n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n,

holds for all
i\in[m]
.
Similarly, by Equation (
225
) in Lemma
31
, it also holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{\ell j}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}+n\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}+C\sigma\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\|_{2}\cdot n^{1/4}\log^{1/4}n,

for all
\ell\in[m]^{c}
.
Under the assumptions of Theorem
12
,
particularly the signal strength and separation conditions, we have

\min_{i\in[m]}\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\max_{\ell\in[m]^{c}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\star}_{\ell,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n},

for some sufficiently large constant
C>0
.
It then follows that

\min_{i\in[m]}\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{ij}^{2}>\max_{\ell\in[m]^{c}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{\ell j}^{2},

(236)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
.
Equation (
236
) implies that the top-
m
hard-thresholding rule will select the first
m
indices with high probability, and thus, correctly recovers the true support
{I_{\star}}
.
∎

F.4
Proof of Theorem
14

Proof.

Suppose that without loss of generality,
{I_{\star}}=[m]
.
Since both group Lasso and hard thresholding are selection-based procedures, it suffices to show that with high probability, the
(m+1)
-th group, a non-signal group, has the largest row norm, and thus gets selected before any group in
{I_{\star}}
.
We first bound the squared row norms of the signal and non-signal groups using Lemma
31
.
Applying Equation (
224
) shows that for the
(m+1)
th row,

\|\boldsymbol{y}_{m+1}\|_{2}^{2}\geq m\beta_{1}^{2}+n\sigma^{2}-C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}-C\beta_{1}\sigma\sqrt{m\log n},

with high probability.
Applying Equation (
225
), for any
\ell\in[m+1]^{c}
(i.e., a non-signal row beyond
m+1
):

\max_{\ell\in[m+1]^{c}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}\leq m\beta_{2}^{2}+n\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}+C\beta_{2}\sigma\sqrt{m\log n},

and for any
i\in[m]
(true signal row):

\max_{i\in[m]}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n\beta_{2}^{2}+n\sigma^{2}+C\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n}+C\beta_{2}\sigma\sqrt{n\log n}+\beta_{1}^{2}+C\sigma\beta_{1}\sqrt{\log n}

both with high probability.

Substituting
\beta_{2}=C\sigma n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n
,
the signal contribution to
\boldsymbol{y}_{i}
is
O(n^{1/2}\log^{1/2}n)
,
while the contribution from
\beta_{1}
in
\boldsymbol{y}_{m+1}
is
m\beta_{1}^{2}
.
If
\beta_{1}\geq C\sigma(n\log n)^{1/4}/\sqrt{m}
for large enough
C
, we have

m\beta_{1}^{2}\gg\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log n},

and thus
\|\boldsymbol{y}_{m+1}\|_{2}^{2}>\|\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
and
\|\boldsymbol{y}_{m+1}\|_{2}^{2}>\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}
for all
i\in[m]
and
\ell\in[m+1]^{c}
with high probability.
Therefore, both group Lasso and hard thresholding will incorrectly prioritize the
(m+1)
-th group, leading to failure in exact support recovery.
∎

Appendix G
Proofs of Main Results on Recovering the Common Structure

In this section, we prove the upper bounds on eigenspace and entrywise estimation of the low rank component
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
obtained in Section
5
for Theorems
15
,
16
,
18
,
19
and Corollaries
3
and
4
.

G.1
Proof of Theorem
15

Proof.

The result largely follows from adapting results in
Cheng et al., 2021
and using new concentration results introduced in
Yan and Levin, 2025
.
We will establish the three main results of Theorem
15
, namely
\ell_{2}
-perturbation, perturbation of linear forms and
\ell_{\infty}
-perturbation, in sequence.
We will only provide some key steps in the proof, and refer the reader to
Cheng et al., 2021
for more details.
As a reminder, we remind the reader that
\boldsymbol{H}
is an asymetric noise matrix given in Equation (
45
),
\lambda_{l}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{l}
are the
l
-th eigenvalue and eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{M}
, respectively, as given in Equation (
48
).

\ell_{2}
-perturbation.

By Equations (83) and (84) in
Cheng et al., 2021
, we have

\sqrt{1-\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right|^{2}}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{l}-\sum_{k=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{8\kappa}{3}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{H}\|}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

(237)

The orthonormality of
\{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{i}\}_{i\in[r]}
implies that

\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}=\left(1-\lambda^{\star}_{k}/\lambda_{l}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{l}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{i}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{i}\right).

Taking
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}
in Theorem
23
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
, we have for all
k\neq l
,

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|1-\lambda^{\star}_{k}/\lambda_{l}\right|^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa^{2}r\log^{4}n}{n}}\max\left\{\frac{\bar{\mu}^{1/2}L\log^{3}n}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}\|_{\infty},\left(\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n\log^{3}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\right)^{k_{0}}\right\}

(238)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|1-\lambda^{\star}_{k}/\lambda_{l}\right|^{-1}\frac{\sigma\sqrt{\kappa^{2}r\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{\kappa^{4}r\log^{7}n}}{\delta^{\star}_{l}},

which yields the bound in
56
.
Putting the above in Equations (
237
) and (
238
) together (which are improved results of Equation (84) and (89) in
Cheng et al., 2021
), we obtain the bound in Equation (
55
) (see details in Equation (90) in
Cheng et al., 2021
).
The bound in Equation (
57
) then follows from the identity
\left|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right|=1-\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\pm\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right\}
.

Perturbation of linear forms.

By the triangle inequality,

\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{l}-\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{l}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right)\right|\leq\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{l}-\sum_{k=1}^{r}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}\right)\right|+\sum_{k\neq l,k=1}^{r}\left|\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}\right)\right|.

(239)

Applying Theorem
23
to the first term in the above display, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
, we have for all
k\neq l
,

\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{l}-\sum_{k=1}^{r}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda_{k}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\star}\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{\kappa^{2}r\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

(240)

By Equation (
56
),

\left|\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{k}}\right|\lesssim\frac{\sigma}{\left|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}\right|}\sqrt{\kappa^{4}r\log^{7}n},

(241)

and it follows that the second term in Equation (
239
) is bounded as

\sum_{k\neq l,k=1}^{r}\left|\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda_{k}^{\star}}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k})\right|\lesssim\sigma r\sqrt{\kappa^{4}r\log^{7}n}\left\{\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}}{\left|\lambda_{l}^{\star}-\lambda_{k}^{\star}\right|}\right\}

Combining the bound in the above display and Equation (
240
) with Equation (
239
), it follows that

\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{l}-\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{l}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right)\right|\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\kappa^{2}r\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}+\kappa r\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}\right|}{\left|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}\right|}\right\}.

(242)

Since
