\min\left|1\pm\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{l}}\right|\lesssim\frac{\kappa r^{2}\sigma\sqrt{\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\kappa^{4}r^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta_{l}^{\star}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}n\log n}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\right)^{2}},

we have

\displaystyle\min\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\pm\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{l}-\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda_{l}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right)\right|+\min\left|1\pm\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda_{l}^{\star}}\right|\cdot\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\kappa^{2}r\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\kappa r\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\star}\right|}{\left|\lambda_{l}^{\star}-\lambda_{k}^{\star}\right|}\right\}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}\kappa^{4}r^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta_{l}^{\star}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}n\log n}{\left(\lambda_{\min}^{\star}\right)^{2}}\right)\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right|

which completes the proof of Equation (
58
).

\ell_{\infty}
-perturbation.

Setting
\boldsymbol{a}
to the standard bases yields the bound in (
59
), which completes the proof.
∎

G.2
Proof of Theorem
16

Proof.

We remind the reader that
\boldsymbol{w}_{l}
is the
l
-th left eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{M}
given in Equation (
48
), and they satisfy the same results as stated in Theorem
15
since one can repeat the proof on
\boldsymbol{M}^{\top}
to obtain the same bounds.
By Equation (101) in
Cheng et al., 2021
(and also viewing from Equation (
54
)), the square of
\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}
defined in Equation (
52
) is given by

\left|\frac{\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\cdot\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{l}}+\gamma_{1}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\cdot\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{l}}+\gamma_{2}\right)}{\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{l}}\cdot\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{l}}+\gamma_{3}}\right|,

(243)

where

\gamma_{1}:=\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{l}-\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{l}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right),\quad\gamma_{2}:=\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{w}_{l}-\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{l}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right),\quad\gamma_{3}:=\mathcal{U}_{1}+\mathcal{U}_{2}

(244)

and the quantities
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{k}}\cdot\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{k}}~\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{r}\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{r}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}:s_{1}+s_{2}\geq 1}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{k_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k_{2}}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{k_{2}}}\cdot\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{H}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k_{2}}}{\lambda_{l}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}}.

We will bound
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{3}
separately, and then combine the results to obtain the desired bound in Equation (
60
).

For
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
, it follows from Equation (
242
) that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
,

\max\left\{\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\left|\gamma_{2}\right|\right\}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\kappa^{2}r\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\kappa r\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}\right|}{\left|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}\right|}\right\}.

(245)

To bound
\gamma_{3}
, we bound
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
separately.
For
|\mathcal{U}_{1}|
, it follows from Equation (
241
) that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
,

|\mathcal{U}_{1}|\lesssim r\left(\frac{\sigma}{\delta^{\star}_{l}}\sqrt{\kappa^{4}r\log^{7}n}\right)^{2}.

(246)

For
|\mathcal{U}_{2}|
, using Theorem
24
and the naive bound that for all
k_{1},k_{2},l\in[r]
the inner products
|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}|
and
|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}|
are upper bounded by
1
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
,
we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{U}_{2}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{r}\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{r}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}:s_{1}+s_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{\left|\lambda^{\star}_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{k_{1}}\cdot\lambda^{\star}_{l}/\lambda^{\star}_{k_{2}}\right|}\left|\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{H}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k_{2}}}{\left(\lambda_{l}^{\star}/2\right)^{s_{1}+s_{2}}}\right|.

Recalling the definition of
\kappa=\lambda^{\star}_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{U}_{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa^{2}r^{2}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}:s_{1}+s_{2}\geq 1}\left(\frac{2}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}\right)^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\left|\boldsymbol{u}_{k_{1}}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}r^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}:s_{1}+s_{2}=s}\left(\frac{2}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}\right)^{s}\left|\boldsymbol{u}_{k_{1}}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}_{k_{2}}^{\star}\right|.

Applying the naïve bound
s\leq 2^{s}
,

\left|\mathcal{U}_{2}\right|\leq\kappa^{2}r^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{4}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}\right)^{s}\left|\boldsymbol{u}_{k_{1}}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}_{k_{2}}^{\star}\right|.

Applying Lemma
32
,

\left|\mathcal{U}_{2}\right|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa^{2}r^{2}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

Applying this and Equation (
246
) to bound

\left|\gamma_{3}\right|\leq\left|\mathcal{U}_{1}\right|+\left|\mathcal{U}_{2}\right|,

we obtain that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
,

\left|\gamma_{3}\right|\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}\kappa^{4}r^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma\kappa^{2}r^{2}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

(247)

Before applying the bounds on
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{3}
to Equation (
243
), we simplify the proof by following the same strategy as in
Cheng et al., 2021
to analyze two cases separately, based on the magnitude of
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}
.

(a)

When
\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right|\leq\max\{|\gamma_{1}|,|\gamma_{2}|\}
, it follows from Equation (108) in
Cheng et al., 2021
that

\left(\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}\right)^{2}\lesssim\left(\max\left\{\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\left|\gamma_{2}\right|\right\}\right)^{2}

and we have

\min\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}\pm\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right|\leq\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right|\lesssim\max\left\{\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\left|\gamma_{2}\right|\right\}

(248)

which can then be combined with Equation (
245
).

(b)

Otherwise, repeating Equation (111) in
Cheng et al., 2021
and replacing their upper bounds on
|\gamma_{1}|,|\gamma_{2}|
and
|\gamma_{3}|
with the refined bounds given in Equation (
245
) and Equation (
247
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l})(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{w}_{l})}{\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}_{l}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}}-\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right|\max\left\{\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\left|\gamma_{2}\right|\right\}+\left(\max\left\{\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\left|\gamma_{2}\right|\right\}\right)^{2}+\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right)^{2}\left|\gamma_{3}\right|}{\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{8}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right|\sigma\sqrt{\kappa^{2}r\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\kappa r\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\star}\right|}{\left|\lambda_{l}^{\star}-\lambda_{k}^{\star}\right|}\right\}

\displaystyle+\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}\kappa^{4}r^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma\kappa^{2}r^{2}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\right).

Taking the above display into Equation (113) in
Cheng et al., 2021
yields that

\displaystyle\min\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}\pm\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left|\frac{\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}\right)}{\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\top}\boldsymbol{w}_{l}}-\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right)^{2}\right|}{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right|}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\kappa^{2}r\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\kappa r\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\star}\right|}{\left|\lambda_{l}^{\star}-\lambda_{k}^{\star}\right|}\right\}

\displaystyle+\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right)\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}\kappa^{4}r^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma\kappa^{2}r^{2}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\right).

For ease of presentation,
Under the assumption
\kappa r=O(1)
, it follows that

\min\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a},l}\pm\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right|\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{\star}}+\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\star}\right|}{\left|\lambda_{l}^{\star}-\lambda_{k}^{\star}\right|}\right\}+\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}^{\star}\right|\frac{\sigma^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}}.

(249)

Taking the maximum over Equation (
249
) and Equation (
248
) to cover the two cases, we obtain the bound in Equation (
60
).
∎

Lemma 32
.

For any unit vector
\boldsymbol{a}
, under the same assumptions as Theorem
16
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
, for the asymmetric noise matrix
\boldsymbol{H}
given in Equation (
45
), we have

\max_{j\in[r]}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}\right|}{|\lambda_{l}|^{s}}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

(250)

Proof.

We break the sum on the left hand side of Equation (
250
) into two parts.
For
s\geq 20\log n
, we have

\sum_{s\geq 20\log n}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}\right|}{|\lambda_{l}|^{s}}\leq\sum_{s\geq 20\log n}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{H}\|^{s}}{|\lambda_{l}|^{s}}.

By Theorem
24
, we have

\|\boldsymbol{H}\|\leq\frac{|\lambda_{l}|}{3}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{2}\leq\left|\frac{\lambda_{l}}{\lambda_{l}^{\star}}\right|\leq 2,

and it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{s\geq 20\log n}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}\right|}{|\lambda_{l}|^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s\geq 20\log n}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{H}\|^{s}}{|\lambda_{l}|^{s}}

(251)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|\boldsymbol{H}\|}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\sum_{s\geq 20\log n-1}\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^{s}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\max\left\{L\log n,\sigma\sqrt{n\log n}\right\}\cdot n^{-10}.

On the other hand, for
s\leq 20\log n
, Theorem
22
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{20\log n}\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{j}\right|}{|\lambda_{l}|^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max\left\{\frac{L\log^{5}n}{|\lambda_{l}|}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}}\|\boldsymbol{a}\|_{\infty},\frac{\sigma\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{|\lambda_{l}|}\right\}

(252)

\displaystyle\lesssim\max\left\{\frac{L\log^{5}n}{|\lambda^{\star}_{\min}|}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}}\|\boldsymbol{a}\|_{\infty},\frac{\sigma\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{|\lambda^{\star}_{\min}|}\right\},

Adding the two parts in equations (
251
) and (
252
) together and using the assumption on the growth rate of
L
and
\sigma
in Equation (
4
) completes the proof.
∎

G.3
Proof of Corollary
3

Proof.

The bound in Equation (
64
) follows directly from Equation (
60
).
To estabish Equation (
61
), suppose that Equations (
58
) and (
60
) hold for all
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2},\cdots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n}
, which is the case with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
by Theorems
15
and
16
.
Then, for each
l\in[r]
, we take
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
in Equation (
58
), there is a binary sign
s\in\{\pm 1\}
such that

\displaystyle\left|u_{l,i}-su^{\star}_{l,i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\sqrt{\log^{7}n}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}+\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|u^{\star}_{k,i}\right|}{|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}|}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left|u^{\star}_{l,i}\right|.

Define the index set

\mathcal{A}=\left\{i\in[n]:|u^{\star}_{l,i}|\leq C\sigma\sqrt{\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}+\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|u^{\star}_{k,i}\right|}{|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}|}\right\}\right\}

Recalling the definition of
\widehat{u}_{l,i}
given in Equation (
62
) and applying the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\left|\widehat{u}_{l,i}-su^{\star}_{l,i}\right|

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\left|\operatorname{sgn}(u_{l,i})\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{i},l}\right|-su^{\star}_{l,i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\left|\operatorname{sgn}(u_{l,i})\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{i},l}\right|-\operatorname{sgn}(u_{l,i})\left|u^{\star}_{l,i}\right|\right|+2\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|u^{\star}_{i,l}|.

Applying the definition of
\mathcal{A}
and Equation (
60
), it follows that

\displaystyle\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\left|\widehat{u}_{l,i}-su^{\star}_{l,i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\sqrt{\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}+\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|u^{\star}_{k,i}\right|}{|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}|}\right\}.

(253)

For
i\in\mathcal{A}^{c}
, we have

\left|u_{l,i}-su^{\star}_{l,i}\right|\leq\frac{3}{4}|u^{\star}_{l,i}|,

which implies that

\operatorname{sgn}(u_{l,i})=s\operatorname{sgn}(u^{\star}_{l,i}),\quad\text{for all }i\in\mathcal{A}^{c}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}^{c}}\left|\widehat{u}_{l,i}-su^{\star}_{l,i}\right|

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}^{c}}\left|s\operatorname{sgn}(u^{\star}_{l,i})\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{i},l}\right|-su^{\star}_{l,i}\right|

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}^{c}}\left|\left|\widehat{u}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{i},l}\right|-\left|u^{\star}_{l,i}\right|\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\sqrt{\log^{7}n}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}+\max_{k:k\neq l}\frac{\left|u^{\star}_{k,i}\right|}{|\lambda^{\star}_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{k}|}\right\}+\frac{C|u^{\star}_{l,i}|\sigma^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{l}\right)^{2}},

where the inequality follows from taking
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
for
i\in\mathcal{A}^{c}
in Equation (
60
).
Combining Equation (
253
) with the above display yields the desired bound in Equation (
61
).
∎

G.4
Proof of Theorem
18

Proof.

By the first order approximation in Equation (
54
), we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}+\boldsymbol{E}={\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{E},

where
\boldsymbol{E}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
is given by

E_{i\ell}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{\star}}{\lambda_{\ell}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}\right)\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{\star}}{\lambda_{\ell}^{s}}

(254)

and
\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}
is the singular value decomposition of

\boldsymbol{Q}:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}.

(255)

Our proof proceeds in four steps.
In Step 1, we show that
\boldsymbol{G}^{-1}
is close to
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}
under the operator norm.
Denote the singular value of
\boldsymbol{G}^{-1}
as
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-2}
.
To show that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}
is close to
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
under the operator norm,
we provide an lower bound on
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
in Step 2.
In Step 3, we then symmetrize
\boldsymbol{G}
to get
\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}
and show that
\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}
can produce a bias-corrected matrix
\boldsymbol{\Psi}
in Step 3.
In Step 4, we obtain the bound in Equation (
69
).
Some technical details are left to the end of the proof, after these four steps.

Step 1: Bounding
\|\boldsymbol{G}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\|
.

By the definition of
\boldsymbol{G}
in Equation (
65
), we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{G}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{M}^{(2)\top}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\left[{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{2}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}+\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\right]\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{Q}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Q}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\right)\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1},

where
\boldsymbol{Q}
is defined in Equation (
255
).
Defining

\boldsymbol{E}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}+\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\right)\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}~\text{ and }~\boldsymbol{E}_{2}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1},

and rearranging, we have

\boldsymbol{G}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{E}_{1}+\boldsymbol{E}_{2},

where
\boldsymbol{Q}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Q}=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}
follows from the SVD of
\boldsymbol{Q}
given by
\boldsymbol{Q}=\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}
.

Thus, to bound
\|\boldsymbol{G}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\|
, it will suffice to control the operator norms of
\boldsymbol{E}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{E}_{2}
.
By the triangle inequality,

\|\boldsymbol{E}_{1}\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\right\|+\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\right\|.

It suffices to control the first term in the above display, as we can handle the other term in exactly the same manner.
Taking the spectral decomposition of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\right\|

(256)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\right\|^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right\|\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right\|.

To bound
\|\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\|
, we note that by submultiplicativity of the norm,

\|\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\right\|^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\boldsymbol{U}\right\|.

(257)

We show below that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)\top}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}

(258)

and

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right\|\lesssim\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}.

(259)

Therefore, applying Equation (
258
) to Equation (
256
) yields

\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)\top}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}

and applying Equation (
259
) in Equation (
257
) yields

\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)\top}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n}}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\right)^{2}}.

It follows from the above two displays that

\left\|\boldsymbol{G}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|\leq\|\boldsymbol{E}_{1}\|+\|\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

(260)

Additionally, denote the singular values of
\boldsymbol{G}^{-1}
as
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}
, we have by Weyl’s inequality,

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

(261)

Step 2: Upper and lower bounds on singular values of
\boldsymbol{Q}
.

To obtain an upper bound on
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|
, we need to provide a lower bound on the singular values of
\boldsymbol{Q}
.
Recall that by Theorem
15
,

|Q_{kk}|=\left|\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\right|\geq 1-O\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}\kappa^{4}r^{2}\log^{7}n}{\left(\delta^{\star}_{k}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}n\log n}{\left(\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\right)^{2}}\right)

and for any
k\neq\ell
, we have

\sum_{\ell\neq k}\left|Q_{k\ell}\right|=\sum_{\ell\neq k}\left|\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{k}}{\lambda_{\ell}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}\right|\lesssim\sum_{\ell\neq k}\frac{\sigma\kappa^{3}\sqrt{r\log^{7}n}}{\delta^{\star}_{\ell}}.

Thus, for
\delta^{\star}_{\ell}\gg\sigma\kappa^{3}r\sqrt{r\log^{7}n}
and
n
sufficiently large, we have

\min_{k\in[r]}~\min\left\{\left|Q_{kk}\right|-\sum_{\ell\neq k}\left|Q_{\ell k}\right|,~\left|Q_{kk}\right|-\sum_{\ell\neq k}\left|Q_{\ell k}\right|\right\}\geq\frac{1}{2},

and thus by Theorem
27
, the smallest singular value of
\boldsymbol{Q}
is lower-bounded as

\sigma_{r}(\boldsymbol{Q})\geq\frac{1}{2}

(262)

when
n
is sufficiently large.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right\|^{-1}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\right\|

(263)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right\|^{-1}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}},

where the first inequality follows from the fact that both
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
are nonnegative diagonal matrices and the last inequality combines Equations (
261
) and (
262
).
Additionally, we can also obtain an upper bound on
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|
.
By Lemma
42
, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{Q}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{k\in[r]}\{|Q_{kk}|\}=2\max_{k\in[r]}\left\{\left|\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\right|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{k\in[r]}\left\{\left|\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{k}\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\|_{2}\right|\right\}=2\max_{k\in[r]}\left\{\left|\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}\right|\right\}.

By Theorem
24
, when
n
is sufficiently large, we have

\frac{1}{2}\leq\max_{k\in[r]}\left\{\left|\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}\right|\right\}\leq 2.

Thus, it follows that for
n
sufficiently large,

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|=\|\boldsymbol{Q}\|\leq 4.

(264)

Combining the fact that
\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}-{\boldsymbol{B}}^{-1}\|\leq\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\|\|\boldsymbol{A}-{\boldsymbol{B}}\|\|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{-1}\|
with Equations (
262
), (
260
) and (
261
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{G}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-2}\right\|\left\|\boldsymbol{G}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-2}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

(265)

Step 3: Bounds on
\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
.

Recall the definition of
\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}
in Equation (
66
).
By the triangle inequality, it follows from Equation (
265
) that

\left\|\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|=\left\|\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{G}+\boldsymbol{G}^{\top})-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

(266)

Combining the above display with Weyl’s inequality, we have

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-2}_{\text{symm}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-2}\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

It is straightforward to see that
\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}
is a positive semidefinite matrix as its eigenvalues are nonnegative combining the above display with the upper bound in Equation (
264
).
Taking the matrix square root of
\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}
given by

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\text{symm}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}_{\text{symm}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\text{symm}}^{\top},

it follows from Lemma
43
and Equation (
266
) that

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|\lesssim\left\|\boldsymbol{G}_{\text{symm}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

(267)

Step 4: Bounding the
\ell_{2,\infty}
norm.

Now consider the bias-corrected estimator
\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\|\boldsymbol{E}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\|_{2,\infty}

(268)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right)\right\|_{2,\infty}+\|\boldsymbol{E}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\|_{2,\infty}

For the first right-hand term, it follows from Equation (
267
) that

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right)\right\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}\left\|\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|

(269)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}r}{n}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right\|\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa r\sqrt{\log n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}},

where the last inequality follows from Equation (
264
).

For the second right-hand term in Equation (
268
), recall the definition of
\boldsymbol{E}
in Equation (
254
), we have for all
i\in[n]
and
l\in[r]
,

\displaystyle|E_{il}|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\left|\lambda_{j}^{\star}\right|}{\left|\lambda_{l}\right|}\left|\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right|\right)\left\{\max_{j\in[r]}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\lambda_{l}\right|^{s}}\left|\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{\star}\right|\right\}

(270)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\lesssim}}\kappa\sqrt{r}\left\{\max_{j\in[r]}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\lambda_{l}\right|^{s}}\left|\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{\star}\right|\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\lesssim}}\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}},

where inequality
(i)
follows from Equation (50) in
Chen et al., 2021a
and inequality
(ii)
follows from Lemma
32
.
We obtain that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{E}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2,\infty}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa r\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}},

where the second inequality follows from Equation (
270
).
Noting that
\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\in\mathbb{O}_{r}
, we conclude that

\min_{\boldsymbol{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{r}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{O}\right\|_{2,\infty}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa r\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}},

obtaining the desired bound.

Supporting technical bounds.

It remains for us to return the bounds in Equations (
258
) and (
259
).

To bound the term
\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)\top}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|
, we instead consider bounding
\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|
for any
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}
that satisfies the same assumptions as
\boldsymbol{H}
.
The reason is that for any symmetric random matrix
\boldsymbol{W}
, we can decompose
\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{H}_{1}+\boldsymbol{H}_{2}
, where
\boldsymbol{H}_{1}
contains the upper-triangular entries of
\boldsymbol{W}
, and
\boldsymbol{H}_{2}
contains the strict lower-triangular entries of
\boldsymbol{W}
. Both
\boldsymbol{H}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{H}_{2}
satisfy the same set of assumptions as
\boldsymbol{H}
and one can use triangle inequality to control bounds involving
\boldsymbol{W}
.
Thus, it suffices to bound
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}
.

To bound
\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\|
, the idea here is to express
\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}
as a sum of independent matrices and apply the matrix Bernstein inequality stated in Theorem
26
.
Noting that

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{H}_{ij}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top},

we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{H}_{ij}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}_{i,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{j,\cdot}=:\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}.

Since

\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}_{ij}\right]=\sigma^{2}_{ij}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}_{i,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}_{i,\cdot}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{j,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2},\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}_{ij}\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\right]=\sigma^{2}_{ij}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}_{j,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{j,\cdot}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2},

we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}_{ij}\right]

\displaystyle\preceq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}_{i,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}_{i,\cdot}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{j,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(271)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{U}_{i,\cdot}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}_{i,\cdot}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{j,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}=r\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\right]

\displaystyle\preceq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}_{j,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}_{j,\cdot}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(272)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}=r\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I},

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\boldsymbol{U}
has unit norm columns as
\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{2},\cdots,\boldsymbol{u}_{r}
are unit eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{M}
as given in Equation (
48
).
Thus, combining Equations (
271
) and (
272
),

v:=\max\left\{\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}_{ij}\right]\right\|,\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\right]\right\|\right\}\lesssim\sigma^{2}r.

Additionally, we have

\max_{i,j}\left\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\right\|\lesssim\frac{L\bar{\mu}r}{n}=:L_{0}.

By matrix Bernstein inequality given in Theorem
26
, it follows that

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}+L_{0}\log n\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{r\log n},

holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
when choosing sufficiently large constants in the bound, completing the proof of Equation (
258
).

To control the other term
\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\boldsymbol{U}\|
in Equation (
259
), let us first fix

\boldsymbol{A}:=\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\boldsymbol{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}.

(273)

Similar to the previous case, we decompose
\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}
into two copies with independent entries. Without loss of generality, we denote one copy to be
\boldsymbol{H}^{(1)}
and only bound
\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{A}\|
.
Set

\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}=H^{(1)}_{ij}\boldsymbol{U}_{i,\cdot}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}_{j,\cdot},\text{ for all }i,j\in[n].

It follows that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}_{ij}\right]\preceq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}_{i,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}_{i,\cdot}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}_{j,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sigma^{2}\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}

and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\right]\preceq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}_{j,\cdot}\boldsymbol{A}_{j,\cdot}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{i,\cdot}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sigma^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}.

Therefore, we have

v:=\max\left\{\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}_{ij}\right]\right\|,\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\right]\right\|\right\}\leq\sigma^{2}\max\left\{\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{U}\|^{2},\|\boldsymbol{A}\|^{2}\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\right\}

and

\max_{i,j}\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\|\leq L\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}r}{n}}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\right\|_{2,\infty}

Thus, conditioned on
\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}
, it follows from the matrix Bernstein inequality given in Theorem
26
that

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right\|\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\log n}\max\left\{\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\|\boldsymbol{U}\|,\|\boldsymbol{A}\|\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\right\}+L\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}r}{n}}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\right\|_{2,\infty}\log n.

holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
.
Now turning our attention to controlling
\boldsymbol{A}
as given in Equation (
273
), we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}\right\|\|\boldsymbol{U}\|\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{n},\quad\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{r}\|\boldsymbol{A}\|\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{rn}.

For
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2,\infty}
,

\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2,\infty}=\max_{\ell\in[n]}\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{r}\max_{\ell\in[n]}\max_{k\in[r]}\left|\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\right|.

Since

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left(W_{\ell i}U_{ik}\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i\ell}^{2}\sigma^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}\|^{2}\sigma^{2}=\sigma^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\left|W_{\ell i}U_{ik}\right|\leq|U_{ik}|L\lesssim L\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}r}{n}},

applying Bernstein’s inequality as in Lemma
38
yields for all
\ell\in[n]

\left\|\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)}_{\ell,\cdot}\boldsymbol{U}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{r\log n}+Lr\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}}\lesssim\sigma r\sqrt{r\log n},

holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
and it follows that

\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\sigma r\sqrt{r\log n}.

Hence, we obtain

\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{W}^{(2)\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right\|\lesssim\sigma^{2}\sqrt{rn\log n},

holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
,
which establishes Equation (
259
) and completes the proof.
∎

G.5
Proof of Corollary
4

Proof.

Recall that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}
is defined in Equation (
63
) with its entries defined in Equation (
62
).
Let
\boldsymbol{S}=\operatorname{diag}\left(s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{r}\right)
be a diagonal matrix, where for all
l\in[r]
,

\|s_{l}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\|_{\infty}=\min\left\{\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l}\pm\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}_{l}\right\|_{\infty}\right\},\;s_{l}\in\{1,-1\}.

By the triangle inequality and basic properties of the norm,

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{S}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)\boldsymbol{S}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{S}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{S}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{S}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)\boldsymbol{S}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{S}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}})\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{S}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{S}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right)^{\top}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right\|+2\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{S}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right\|\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}.

Applying Corollary
3
and Theorem
24
to the above diplay, it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
that

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}\log^{7}n}{n}}+\frac{\sigma\lambda^{\star}_{\max}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\delta^{\star}_{\min}}\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n},

as desired.
∎

G.6
Proof of Theorem
19

Proof.

We remind the reader that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
is defined in Equation (
68
),
\boldsymbol{Q}
is defined in Equation (
255
), and
\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}
is the singular value decomposition of
\boldsymbol{Q}
.
By the triangle inequality and basic properties of the norm,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\!\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\!-\!\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\!\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\!-\!\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)\!\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\!\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\!-\!{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\right\|_{\infty}

(274)

\displaystyle+\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left(\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right\|+\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left(\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|_{2,\infty}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right\|\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|_{2,\infty}\right).

Combining results in Theorems
18
and
24
, it is straightforward to show that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right\|

\displaystyle+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}-{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|_{2,\infty}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right\|\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\right\|_{2,\infty}\right)

(275)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\bar{\mu}r}{n}\frac{\sigma r^{2}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}+\sigma\kappa^{2}r\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}\log^{7}n}{n}}\lesssim\sigma\kappa^{2}r^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mu}\log^{7}n}{n}}

where the second inequality holds since
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
satisfies Assumption
8
.

By basic properties of the norm,

\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left(\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right\|.

(276)

For ease of notation,
Denote
\boldsymbol{O}:=\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}

\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}=\boldsymbol{O}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{O}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}.

(277)

Define
\boldsymbol{Q}:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}
and observe that by construction,
\boldsymbol{O}
is the unitary polar factor of
\boldsymbol{Q}
.

Plugging in the definition of
\boldsymbol{Q}
and applying the triangle inequality,

\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}-\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}-\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}\right)\right\|+\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}-\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\right\|,

(278)

where for a matrix
A
, we use
\operatorname{diag}A
to denote the matrix obtained by setting all elements of
A
to zero aside from the diagonal.
Using Theorem
24
with Equation (
46
), it is straightforward to show that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{-1}-\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}\right)\right\|\lesssim\frac{\kappa\sigma r^{2}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}.

By Equation (
56
) in Theorem
15
, since we assume the same assumptions as Theorem
15
,
it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-17})
that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)^{-1}-\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}-\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa^{3}r\sqrt{r\log^{7}n}}{\delta_{\min}^{\star}}.

Applying the above two displays to Equation (
278
) and using the fact that
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\geq\delta^{\star}_{\min}
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}-\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa^{3}r\sqrt{r\log^{7}n}}{\delta^{\star}_{\min}}.

(279)

Let
\boldsymbol{S}
be a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are given by the signs of
\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)
.
Recall that for any real matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
, its unitary polar factor is given by

\boldsymbol{A}\left(\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\right)^{-1/2}.

Thus, the polar factor of
\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)
is given by

\boldsymbol{S}\left|\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\right|\left(\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\right)^{-1/2}=\boldsymbol{S}.

As observed above,
\boldsymbol{O}
is the unitary polar factor of
\boldsymbol{Q}
.
Thus, by perturbation bounds of unitary polar factors
(
Bhatia, 2013
, see Theorem VII.5.1 in)
,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{O}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}^{-1}\right\|^{-1}+\left\|\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)^{-1}\right\|^{-1}}\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}-\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\right\|

(280)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}-\operatorname{diag}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\right\|\lesssim\frac{\sigma\kappa^{3}r\sqrt{r\log^{7}n}}{\delta_{\min}^{\star}},

where the second inequality holds since
\|\boldsymbol{Q}^{-1}\|^{-1}
is lower bounded by
1/2
as shown in Equation (
262
) and
\|\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{U})^{-1}\|^{-1}
is lower bounded by some constant by Equation (
57
) in Theorem
15
under the growth rate assumption on
\delta^{\star}_{l}
and
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
, the last inequality follows from Equation (
279
).

Noting that for all
i,j\in[r]
,

\left(\boldsymbol{O}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{O}\right)_{ij}=O_{ij}\left(\lambda^{\star}_{i}-\lambda^{\star}_{j}\right)

and thus by basic properties of the norm,

\left\|\boldsymbol{O}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{O}\right\|\leq\delta^{\star}_{\max}\left(\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq r}O_{ij}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{r}\delta^{\star}_{\max}\left\|\boldsymbol{O}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\|

(281)

where the first inequality holds since
\left(\boldsymbol{O}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{O}\right)_{ii}=0
for all
i\in[r]
.
Applying Equation (
280
),

\left\|\boldsymbol{O}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{O}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\delta^{\star}_{\max}}{\delta^{\star}_{\min}}\sigma\kappa^{3}r^{2}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}.

Since the operator norm is unitarily invariant, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{O}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{O}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right\|=\left\|\boldsymbol{O}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{O}\right\|\lesssim\frac{\delta^{\star}_{\max}}{\delta^{\star}_{\min}}\sigma\kappa^{3}r^{2}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}.

Recalling the equality in Equation (
277
), we further bound Equation (
276
) as

\left\|{\boldsymbol{U}^{\star}}\left(\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\star}\right)\boldsymbol{U}^{\star\top}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\frac{\delta^{\star}_{\max}}{\delta^{\star}_{\min}}\frac{\bar{\mu}}{n}\sigma\kappa^{3}r^{2}\sqrt{\log^{7}n}.

Applying this and Equation (
275
) to Equation (
274
) completes the proof.
∎

Appendix H
Proofs of Minimax Results on Recovering the Common Structure

In this section, we prove the minimax lower bounds related to the low rank component
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
, establishing Theorems
3
,
20
and Corollary
5
.

H.1
Proof of Theorem
3

Before stating the proof of Theorem
3
, we establish a few technical lemmas.

Lemma 33

(Generalized Fano method)
.

Let
N\geq 1
be an integer and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\dots,\theta_{N}\subset\Theta
index a collection of probability measures
\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{i}}
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
.
Let
D
be a pseudometric on
\Theta
and suppose that for
\alpha_{N},\beta_{N}>0
all
i\neq j
,

D(\theta_{i},\theta_{j})\geq\alpha_{N}

and

\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{i}}\left\|\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{j}}\right.\right)\leq\beta_{N}.

Then every
\mathcal{F}
-measurable estimator
\widehat{\theta}
satisfies

\max_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{i}}d(\widehat{\theta},\theta_{i})\geq\frac{\alpha_{N}}{2}\left(1-\frac{\beta_{N}+\log 2}{\log N}\right)

See Lemma 3 in
Yu, 1997
for a proof of the above lemma.

Lemma 34

(Varshamov-Gilbert Bound)
.

Let
d
be an integer satisfying
1\leq d\leq p/4
.
There exists a set
\Omega^{(p)}_{d}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{p}
that satisfies the following properties:

1.

\|\boldsymbol{\omega}\|_{0}=d
for all
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega^{(p)}_{d}
;

2.

\|\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\|_{0}\geq d/2
for all distinct pairs
\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{(p)}_{d}
;

3.

\log\left|\Omega^{(p)}_{d}\right|\geq cd\log(p/d)
, where
c\geq 0.233
.

See Lemma 4.10 in
Massart, 2007
.

With the above lemmas, we are equipped to prove the minimax lower bound in Theorem
3
.

Proof.

We will make use of the generalized Fano method stated in Lemma
33
.
Let
p=\lceil n/2\rceil-1
,
d=\lfloor p/4\rfloor
and
\Omega^{(p)}_{d}
be the set defined in Lemma
34
.
For a pair of given
\lambda^{\star}
and
N
, and for each
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega^{(p)}_{d}
, we associate them with the vectors

\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)}:=\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{N}\lambda^{\star}}\left(2\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{1}_{p}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{p},

(282)

and

\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega)}:=\left(u_{0},\;\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)\top},\;\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}^{\top}\right)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(283)

where
u_{0}=\sqrt{\mu/n}
,
c_{1},c_{2}>0
are normalization factors to be specified later so that
\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega)}\right\|_{2}=1
.
For notational simplicity, we let
q:=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
.
For each
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega_{d}^{(p)}
, we set

\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}:=\lambda^{\star}\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega)\top}=\lambda^{\star}\begin{pmatrix}u_{0}^{2}&u_{0}\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)\top}&\frac{c_{2}u_{0}}{\sqrt{q}}\boldsymbol{1}_{q}^{\top}\\
u_{0}\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)}&\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)}\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)\top}&\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{q}}\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)}\boldsymbol{1}_{q}^{\top}\\
\frac{c_{2}u_{0}}{\sqrt{q}}\boldsymbol{1}_{q}&\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{q}}\boldsymbol{1}_{q}\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)\top}&\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{q}\boldsymbol{1}_{q}\boldsymbol{1}_{q}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}.

(284)

and for distinct pairs of
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega^{\prime})}\right\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}_{1,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega^{\prime})}_{1,\cdot}\right\|_{2}=\lambda^{\star}u_{0}\left\|\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)}-\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega^{\prime})}\right\|_{2}

(285)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\lambda^{\star}\frac{2u_{0}c_{1}}{\sqrt{N}\lambda^{\star}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\geq}}c_{1}u_{0}\sqrt{\frac{2d}{N}}.

where equality
(i)
follows from the definition of
\boldsymbol{v}^{(\omega)}
in Equation (
282
), and inequality
(ii)
follows from the fact that
\|\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}\|_{0}\geq\frac{d}{2}
by Lemma
34
.
Let
\mathbb{P}^{(\omega)}_{N}
be the probability measure over
\operatorname{Sym}(n)
associated with

\boldsymbol{M}_{l}=\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}+\boldsymbol{W}_{l},\quad\text{for }l\in[N],

where
\{\boldsymbol{W}_{l}\}_{l\in[N]}
are symmetric noise matrices with independent entries
W_{l,ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{l,ij})
on and above diagonal.
It follows that

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}^{(\omega)}_{N}\left\|\mathbb{P}^{(\omega^{\prime})}_{N}\right.\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{N}\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}^{(\omega)}_{N,ij}\left\|\mathbb{P}^{(\omega^{\prime})}_{N,ij}\right.\right)\leq\frac{N\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega^{\prime})}\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}}{\sigma^{2}_{\min}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\frac{N(\lambda^{\star})^{2}}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega)}-\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega^{\prime})}\right\|_{2}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}\frac{N(\lambda^{\star})^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\min}}\cdot\frac{4c^{2}_{1}}{N\left(\lambda^{\star}\right)^{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4c_{1}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\min}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\right\|_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{8c_{1}^{2}d}{\sigma^{2}_{\min}}

where inequality
(i)
follows from Equation (
284
) using the definition of
\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega^{\prime})}
, equality
(ii)
follows from the definition of
\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega)}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega^{\prime})}
in Equation (
283
), equality
(iii)
follows from Lemma
34
.
Thus, combining the above display with Equation (
285
), by Lemma
33
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega_{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}\right\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}u_{0}\sqrt{\frac{2d}{N}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\log\left|\Omega^{(p)}_{d}\right|}\left(\frac{8c_{1}^{2}d}{\sigma^{2}_{\min}}+\log 2\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}u_{0}\sqrt{\frac{2d}{N}}\left(1-\frac{1}{c\log\left(\frac{p}{d}\right)}\left(\frac{8c_{1}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\min}}+\frac{\log 2}{d}\right)\right)

Recalling that
d=\lfloor p/4\rfloor
and taking
c_{1}=\min\{\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2\log 4}}\sigma_{\min},c_{1}^{\prime}\}
where
c_{1}^{\prime}>0
is a sufficiently small constant that allows us to normalize
\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega)}
to have unit norm when
\sigma_{\min}
is large, it holds for sufficiently large
n
that

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega_{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(\omega)}\right\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sigma_{\min}u_{0}}{8\sqrt{\log 4}}\sqrt{\frac{d}{N}}\geq\frac{\sigma_{\min}u_{0}}{27\sqrt{\log 4}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{N}}.

Recalling that
u_{0}=\sqrt{\mu/n}
and picking a suitable
c_{2}
to make
\boldsymbol{u}^{(\omega)}
to have unit norm finishes the proof.
∎

H.2
Proof of Theorem
20

Proof.

Consider testing the following two hypotheses

\mathcal{H}_{0}:\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}+\boldsymbol{W}\quad\text{ versus }\quad\mathcal{H}_{1}:\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{W},

where
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}=\lambda^{\star}\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)\top}
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}=\lambda^{\star}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)\top}
are rank-one matrices and, observed subject to additive noise
\boldsymbol{W}
, where
\boldsymbol{W}
is an independent mean-zero Gaussian matrix with
\mathbb{E}W_{ij}^{2}=\sigma^{2}_{ij}\geq\sigma^{2}_{\min}
.
Let
\mathbb{P}_{k,i,j}
stand for the distribution of
\boldsymbol{M}^{(k)}_{ij}
for
k=0,1
and
\mathbb{P}_{k}
denote the product measure obtained from
\mathbb{P}_{k,i,j}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}_{k}=\prod_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mathbb{P}_{k,i,j}
for
k=0,1
.
We show how to obtain a lower bound for
\|\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{M}}\|_{\infty}
by choosing suitable
\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}
such that the KL divergence
\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}\left\|\mathbb{P}_{0}\right.\right)
is small, while
\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}\|_{\infty}
is large, and then applying standard tools from
Tsybakov, 2008
to get the minimax rate.

Step 1: calculation of KL divergence.

We first calculate the KL divergence of
\mathbb{P}_{0}
from
\mathbb{P}_{1}

as follows

\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}\left\|\mathbb{P}_{0}\right.\right)=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1,i,j}\left\|\mathbb{P}_{0,i,j}\right.\right)=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\frac{\left(M^{(0)}_{ij}-M^{(1)}_{ij}\right)^{2}}{2\sigma_{ij}^{2}}\leq\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}}{2\sigma^{2}_{\min}},

where the first equality holds by the decoupling property of the KL divergence
(
Wainwright, 2019
, see Equation (15.11a) in)
and the second equality fllows from properties of Gaussian KL divergence
(
Wainwright, 2019
, see Example 15.13 in)
.

By definition of
\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}
,

\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}\left\|\mathbb{P}_{0}\right.\right)=\frac{(\lambda^{\star})^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)\top}-\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)\top}\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}}{2\sigma^{2}_{\min}}\leq\frac{(\lambda^{\star})^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\min}},

(286)

where the last inequality holds due to the fact that for any unit vectors
\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{v}

(
Cheng et al., 2021
, here we repeat Equation (179) in)
,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{4}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{4}-2\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top},\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\right\rangle=2-2\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(2-2\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right)\left(1+\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}.

Step 2: bounding minimax probability of error.

Define the minimax probability of errors as

p_{e,1}:=\inf_{\psi}\max\left\{~\mathbb{P}\left(\psi\text{ rejects }\mathcal{H}_{0}\mid\mathcal{H}_{0}\right),\quad\mathbb{P}\left(\psi\text{ rejects }\mathcal{H}_{1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{1}\right)~\right\},

where the infimum is over all tests.
By Theorem 2.2 in
Tsybakov, 2008
, if
\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}\left\|\mathbb{P}_{0}\right.\right)\leq\alpha
, then

p_{e,1}\geq\max\left(\frac{1}{4}\exp(-\alpha),\frac{1-\sqrt{\alpha/2}}{2}\right).

Taking
\alpha=1/16
, it implies that when

\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}\left\|\mathbb{P}_{0}\right.\right)\leq 1/8

(287)

we have
p_{e,1}\geq 3/8
.
Therefore, we will construct
\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}
such that

\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{4\lambda^{\star}}

(288)

so that by Equation (
286
), we have
\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}\left\|\mathbb{P}_{0}\right.\right)\leq 1/8
.
Since

\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}\right\|_{\infty}\geq\max_{i\in[n]}\lambda^{\star}\left|u^{(0)}_{i}-u^{(1)}_{i}\right|\left|u^{(0)}_{i}+u^{(1)}_{i}\right|,

(289)

we consider

u_{1}^{(k)}=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{n}}\cos\theta_{k},\quad u_{2}^{(k)}=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{n}}\sin\theta_{k}

and
u^{(0)}_{i}=u^{(1)}_{i}
for all
2\leq i\leq n
.
It is straightforward to guarantee that the constructed
\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}
satisfies that
\boldsymbol{M}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{M}(\mu,\sigma_{\min})
, where
\mathcal{M}
is defined in Equation (
71
).
By the construction, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\right\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(u_{1}^{(1)}-u_{1}^{(0)}\right)^{2}+\left(u_{2}^{(1)}-u_{2}^{(0)}\right)^{2}=\frac{\mu}{n}\left(\left(\cos\theta_{1}-\cos\theta_{0}\right)^{2}+\left(\sin\theta_{1}-\sin\theta_{0}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\mu}{n}\left(1-\cos(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})\right).

By Equation (
71
), we have

\lambda^{\star}\geq\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{\frac{n}{\mu}}

(290)

so that we can set

\cos(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})=1-\frac{n\sigma^{2}_{\min}}{32\mu\left(\lambda^{\star}\right)^{2}}\geq\frac{31}{32}

and Equation (
288
) holds.
Additionally, we have

\displaystyle\left|u^{(0)}_{1}-u^{(1)}_{1}\right|\left|u^{(0)}_{1}+u^{(1)}_{1}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{n}\left|\cos^{2}\theta_{1}-\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{n}\left|\sin\left(\theta_{1}+\theta_{0}\right)\right|\cdot\sqrt{1-\cos^{2}\left(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0}\right)}.

Taking
\theta_{1}+\theta_{0}=\pi/2
, so that

\theta_{0}=\frac{\pi}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\arccos\left(1-\frac{n\sigma^{2}_{\min}}{32\mu\left(\lambda^{\star}\right)^{2}}\right),\quad\theta_{1}=\frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\arccos\left(1-\frac{n\sigma^{2}_{\min}}{32\mu\left(\lambda^{\star}\right)^{2}}\right).

One can verify that
\cos\theta_{k}\geq 1/2
for
k=0
and
1
under Equation (
290
), implying that

\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}\right\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{n}},\quad\text{for }k=0,1.

It follows that

\left|u^{(0)}_{1}-u^{(1)}_{1}\right|\left|u^{(0)}_{1}+u^{(1)}_{1}\right|\geq\frac{\mu}{n}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n\sigma^{2}_{\min}}{32\mu\left(\lambda^{\star}\right)^{2}}}=\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{8\lambda^{\star}}\sqrt{\frac{3\mu}{n}}

and by Equation (
289
), we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}\right\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{8}\sqrt{\frac{3\mu}{n}}.

Thus, combining the above bound and Equation (
287
) with Theorem 2.2 in
Tsybakov, 2008
completes the proof.
∎

H.3
Proof of Corollary
5

Proof.

Consider the following collection of hypothesis tests:

\mathcal{H}_{0}:\boldsymbol{M}^{(l)}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(0)}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}\quad\text{ versus}\quad\mathcal{H}_{1}:\boldsymbol{M}^{(l)}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)},\quad\text{for }l\in[N].

Let
\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{k,i,j}
stand for the distribution of
M^{(l)}_{ij}
under
\mathcal{H}_{k}
for
k=0,1
,
l\in[N]
and
1\leq i,j\leq n
.
We have

\operatorname{KL}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}^{(l)}\left\|\prod_{l=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{0}\right.\right)\leq\sum_{l=1}^{N}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\operatorname{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1,i,j}\left\|\mathbb{P}_{0,i,j}\right.\right)\leq\frac{N}{2\sigma^{2}_{\min}}\left\|\boldsymbol{M}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{M}^{(1)}\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}.

Following the same proof and construction as Theorem
20
with
\sigma_{\min}
substituted by
\sigma_{\min}/\sqrt{N}
, we have

\operatorname{KL}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}^{(l)}\left\|\prod_{l=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}^{(l)}_{0}\right.\right)\leq\frac{N\left(\lambda^{\star}\right)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}_{\min}}\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{8}

and

\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(0)}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(1)}\right\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{8}\sqrt{\frac{3\mu}{nN}}.

Combining the above two dislay equations and applying Theorem 2.2 in
Tsybakov, 2008
again yields the desired minimax lower bound.
∎

Appendix I
Technical Results in
Yan and Levin, 2025

The following are results related to
Yan and Levin, 2025
, which we reproduce here for ease of reference.

Lemma 35

(Neumann Expansion)
.

Consider
\boldsymbol{M}
given in Equation (
45
). Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{l}
and
\lambda_{l}
be the
l
-th leading right eigenvector and the
l
-th leading eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{M}
, respectively. If
\|\boldsymbol{H}\|<\left|\lambda^{\star}_{l}\right|
, then one has

\boldsymbol{u}_{l}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{\star}}{\lambda_{l}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{\star\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\right)\left\{\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{l}^{s}}\boldsymbol{H}^{s}\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{\star}\right\}.

Proof.

See Lemma 1 in
Cheng et al., 2021
.
∎

Theorem 22

(Theorem 3 in
Yan and Levin, 2025
)
.

Consider a random matrix
\boldsymbol{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with independent entries satisfying Assumption
1
and the bound in Equation (
4
), for any fixed unit vectors
\boldsymbol{x}
and
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it holds with probability at least
1-\tilde{O}(n^{-40})
that for all integers
k
satisfying
2\leq k\leq 20\log n
,

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{k}\boldsymbol{y}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}^{k}\boldsymbol{y}\right|\lesssim c_{2}^{k}(\log n)^{2}\max\Biggl\{\!\sqrt{\frac{(\sigma^{2}n\log^{3}\!n)^{k}}{n}},(L\log^{3}n)^{k}\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\infty}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\infty}\!\Biggr\}.

(291)

Theorem 23

(Theorem 6 in
Yan and Levin, 2025
)
.

Consider a rank-
r
symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with incoherence parameter
\mu
.
Define
\kappa:=\lambda^{\star}_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
, where
\lambda^{\star}_{\max}
and
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
are the largest and smallest eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{M}^{\star}
in magnitude, respectively.
Suppose the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{H}
obeys Assumption
1
, and assume the existence of some sufficiently large constant
C_{2}\geq 0
such that

\lambda^{\star}_{\min}\geq C_{2}\max\left\{\sigma\sqrt{n\log^{3}n},L\log^{3}n\right\}.

(292)

Then for any fixed unit vector
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and for any
l\in[r]
, with probability at least
1-O(rn^{-20})
,

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda^{\star}_{j}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star\top}_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}}{\lambda_{l}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}^{\star}\right|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\kappa^{2}r\log^{4}n}{n}}\max\Bigg\{\frac{\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}L\log^{3}n}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\|\boldsymbol{a}\|_{\infty},\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n\log^{3}n}}{\lambda^{\star}_{\min}}\Bigg\}.

(293)

Theorem 24
.

Suppose that the noise parameters defined in Assumption
1
satisfy

\displaystyle\delta^{\star}_{l}\geq c_{3}r^{2}\!\max\!\left\{\!\frac{\kappa\mu L\!\log^{5}\!n}{n},\kappa\sigma\log^{7/2}\right\}

and
\lambda^{\star}_{\min}
satisfies condition (
292
) for some sufficiently large constants
c_{3}>0
and
C_{2}>0
.
Then given any
l\in[r]
, with probability
1-O(r^{2}n^{-20})
, the eigenvalue
\lambda_{l}
is real-valued, and

\displaystyle|\lambda_{l}-\lambda^{\star}_{l}|\leq c_{3}r^{2}\max\Bigg\{\frac{\kappa\mu L\log^{5}n}{n},\kappa\sigma\log^{7/2}n\Bigg\}.

(294)

Appendix J
Technical Bounds and Auxiliary Lemmas

Here we collect auxiliary technical results related to concentration inequalities and linear algebra, which are used in proving our main results.

J.1
Probability Inequalities

Lemma 36
.

For any symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
\boldsymbol{W}
satisfying Assumption
1
, it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-40})
that

\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\circ\boldsymbol{W}\right\|\lesssim\sigma\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\right\|_{2,\infty}+L\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\right\|_{\infty}\sqrt{\log n}.

Proof.

We use the matrix Bernstein inequality stated in Theorem
25
.
Since

|A_{ij}W_{ij}|\leq\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\infty}L\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{i}\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}(A_{ij}W_{ij})^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2,\infty}^{2},

we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\circ\boldsymbol{W}\right\|\geq 4\sigma\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2,\infty}+t\right\}\lesssim n\exp\left(-\frac{ct^{2}}{L^{2}\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\infty}^{2}}\right).

Taking
t=CL\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2,\infty}\sqrt{\log n}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 37

(Example 2.11 in
Wainwright, 2019
)
.

For
Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots,Z_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and any
t\in(0,1)
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}Z_{j}^{2}-1\right|\geq t\right)\leq 2e^{-kt^{2}/8}.

For
n
and
k_{n}
such that
k_{n}^{-1}\log n=o(1)
, taking
t=7\sqrt{k_{n}/\log n}
yields that for sufficiently large
n
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-6})
,

\left|\sum_{j=1}^{k_{n}}Z_{k_{n}}^{2}-k_{n}\right|\leq 7\sqrt{k_{n}\log n}.

Lemma 38

(Bernstein’s inequality)
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{k}
be independent zero-mean random variables such that
|X_{i}|\leq L
and
\mathbb{E}X_{i}^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}
for all
i\in[k]
.
Then

\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}\right|\geq t\right\}\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}/2}{k\sigma^{2}+Lt/3}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-c\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{k\sigma^{2}},\frac{t}{L}\right\}\right).

In other words, by choosing a suitable and universal constant
C>0
, it holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-40})
, that

\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(X_{i}-\mathbb{E}X_{i}\right)\right|\lesssim\max\left\{\sigma\sqrt{k\log n},L\log n\right\}.

Proof.

See Theorem 2.8.4 in
Vershynin, 2018
.
∎

Theorem 25

(Theorem 3.4 in
Chen et al., 2021b
)
.

Consider a symmetric random matrix
\boldsymbol{X}=\left[X_{i,j}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, whose entries are independently generated and obey

\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i,j}\right]=0,\quad\text{ and }\quad\left|X_{i,j}\right|\leq B,\quad 1\leq i,j\leq n.

Define

\nu:=\max_{i}\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i,j}^{2}\right].

Then there exists some universal constant
c>0
such that for any
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|\geq 4\sqrt{\nu}+t\}\leq n\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{cB^{2}}\right).

Theorem 26

(Corollary 3.3 in
Chen et al., 2021b
)
.

Let
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}
be a set of independent real random matrices of dimension
n_{1}\times n_{2}
. Suppose that

\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right\|\leq L_{0},\quad\text{ for all }i

and set
n:=\max\left\{n_{1},n_{2}\right\}
.
Define the matrix variance statistic
v
as

\displaystyle v:=\max\left\{\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)^{\top}\right]\right\|,\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)\right]\right\|\right\}.

For any
a\geq 2
, with probability exceeding
1-2n^{-a+1}
one has

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right\|\leq\sqrt{2av\log n}+\frac{2a}{3}L_{0}\log n.

Lemma 39
.

For two random variables
A
and
B
, we say that
A
is stochastically dominated by
B
(
A\preceq B
) if for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{P}\{B\geq t\}\geq\mathbb{P}\{A\geq t\}.

If
u:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a function increasing in each variable and
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
are independent sets of random variables with
A_{i}\preceq B_{i}
for each
i
,
then
u\left(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\right)\preceq u\left(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}\right)
and in particular
\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\preceq\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}
.

Proof.

See Theorem 3.3.11 in
Müller and Stoyan, 2002
.
∎

J.2
Linear Algebra Lemmas

Lemma 40
.

If
\boldsymbol{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a row stochastic matrix and
\boldsymbol{S}^{2}>0
holds entrywise, then
\boldsymbol{I}_{n}+\boldsymbol{S}
is invertible.

Proof.

By the Gershgorin circle theorem, all of
\boldsymbol{S}
’s eigenvalues are bounded between
-1
and
1
.
Thus, all eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{S}^{2}
are bounded between
0
and
1
.
Noting that
\boldsymbol{S}\mathbf{1}_{n}=\mathbf{1}_{n}
, we see that
1
is a eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{S}
.
Since
\boldsymbol{S}^{2}>0
,
\boldsymbol{S}
is irreducible and by the Perron–Frobenius theorem, we have that
1
is an algebraically simple eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{S}^{2}
.
Thus, all eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{S}
must be strictly larger than
-1
,
and it follows that
\boldsymbol{I}_{n}+\boldsymbol{S}
is invertible.
∎

Lemma 41
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a row stochastic matrix and
\boldsymbol{I}_{n}+\boldsymbol{S}
is invertible.
Then
\mathbf{1}_{n}
is an eigenvector of
(\boldsymbol{I}_{n}+\boldsymbol{S})^{-1}
with eigenvalue
1/2
.

Proof.

Since
\boldsymbol{S}
is row stochastic, we have

\boldsymbol{S}\mathbf{1}_{n}=\mathbf{1}_{n}\quad\text{ and }\quad(\boldsymbol{I}_{n}+\boldsymbol{S})\mathbf{1}_{n}=2\mathbf{1}_{n}.

Since
\boldsymbol{I}_{n}+\boldsymbol{S}
is invertible, it follows that

(\boldsymbol{I}_{n}+\boldsymbol{S})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{n}.

∎

Theorem 27
.

For a square matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
, set
\alpha=\min_{k\in[r]}\left\{|A_{kk}|-\sum_{j\neq k}|A_{kj}|\right\}
and
\beta=\min_{k\in[r]}\{|A_{kk}|-\sum_{j\neq k}|A_{jk}|\}
.
If
\boldsymbol{A}
is diagonally dominant both by rows and by columns, then

\sigma_{r}(\boldsymbol{A})\geq\sqrt{\alpha\beta},

where
\sigma_{r}(\boldsymbol{A})
is the smallest singular value of
\boldsymbol{A}
.

Proof.

See Theorem 1, Corollary 1 and Corollary 2 in
Varah, 1975
.
∎

Lemma 42
.

For a square matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
, if
\boldsymbol{A}
is diagonally dominant both by rows and by columns,then

\|\boldsymbol{A}\|\leq 2\max_{i\in[r]}\left\{|A_{ii}|\right\}.

Proof.

We have

\|\boldsymbol{A}\|\leq\sqrt{\max_{1\leq j\leq r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}|A_{ij}|}\sqrt{\max_{1\leq i\leq r}\sum_{j=1}^{r}|A_{ij}|}\leq\max_{j\in[r]}\sqrt{2|A_{jj}|}\max_{i\in[r]}\sqrt{2|A_{ii}|}=2\max_{i\in[r]}\left\{|A_{ii}|\right\},

where the first inequality follows from Corollary 2 in
Varah, 1975
and the second inequality follows from the fact that
\boldsymbol{A}
is diagonally dominant both by rows and by columns.
∎

Lemma 43
.

For symmetric positive definite matrices
\boldsymbol{A},{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, suppose that the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{A}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}
are all greater than
\lambda_{\min}>0
, then

\left\|\boldsymbol{A}^{1/2}-{\boldsymbol{B}}^{1/2}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}-{\boldsymbol{B}}\right\|.

Proof.

For any unit eigenvector
\boldsymbol{x}
of
\boldsymbol{A}^{1/2}-{\boldsymbol{B}}^{1/2}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{A}-{\boldsymbol{B}}\right)\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}^{1/2}\left(\boldsymbol{A}^{1/2}-{\boldsymbol{B}}^{1/2}\right)\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}^{1/2}\left(\boldsymbol{A}^{1/2}-{\boldsymbol{B}}^{1/2}\right)\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{A}^{1/2}+{\boldsymbol{B}}^{1/2}\right)\boldsymbol{x}.

Thus, it follows that

\left|\lambda\right|\leq\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{A}-{\boldsymbol{B}}\right\|}{\min_{\boldsymbol{x}:\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{2}=1}\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{x}+\min_{\boldsymbol{y}:\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}=1}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{y}}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}-{\boldsymbol{B}}\right\|.

Taking the maximum on the left hand side over all eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{A}^{1/2}-{\boldsymbol{B}}^{1/2}
yields the desired bound.
∎
